## 1. Explanation of the work carried out and Overview of the progress 

The main objective of SUREWAVE is to develop and test an innovative concept of a Floating Photo-Voltaic (FPV) system consisting of an external floating breakwater structure made of new circular materials acting as protection against severe wave loads on the FPV structure itself, allowing increased operational availability and energy output. Such a concept is designed to operate in all the European sea-basins including open-sea very harsh environments with high wind (speed \>36 m/s), current (\>1.2 m/s), and wave (height \>14 m), thus unlocking potential deployment of offshore FPV at European and worldwide level.

### 1.1 Objectives

The realization of the overall objective has been achieved through the specific objectives described in the Grant Agreement. During the SUREWAVE project period M19-M48, the following actions have been performed to meet the specific objectives as described in the Grant Agreement.

Table 1: Specific objectives of SUREWAVE (Short summary)

| **Objective** | **Proposal Target** | **Actual Achievement** | **Status** |
|----|----|----|----|
| O1 | Novel offshore FPV design | Breakwater, mooring, connections, integrated 7.16 MW concept, basin and marine validation | ✅ Fully achieved |
| O2 | Circular concrete materials | HPC, LWAC and cellular concrete developed, tested and demonstrated | ✅ Fully achieved |
| O3 | Modelling tools, ANN and SHMS | Delivered and validated | ✅ Fully achieved |
| O4 | Advanced testing and validation framework | Laboratory, basin and marine validation completed | ✅ Fully achieved |
| O5 | Societal benefits | SAB, stakeholder engagement and Social-LCA completed | ✅ Fully achieved |
| O6 | Environmental impact minimisation | \>90% GHG reduction demonstrated | ✅ Fully achieved |
| O7 | Economic impact | Mediterranean LCOE 0.062 €/kWh, positive NPV and IRR | ✅ Achieved But calculated LCoE is slightly higher than the DoA LCOE target |
| O8 | High exploitability | KERs identified, exploitation pathways defined | ✅ Fully achieved |

Table 1: Specific objectives of SUREWAVE (Detailed description)

<table style="width:100%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><strong>Objective 1 (O1): NOVEL FLOATING PV DESIGN FOR OFFSHORE EXTREME CONDITIONS:</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p>This will consist of:</p>
<ol type="a">
<li><p>Research and design on novel breakwater concept with a large wave attenuation effect, as an external element for the protection and attenuation of the wind, waves, and currents harmful to the internal FPV elements whilst ensures that overall structure is safely and non-destructively conducting associated forces into the anchorage, not impairing the functionality of the PV-systems.</p></li>
<li><p>Research and optimization of the coupling design between the external breakwater and internal FPV, including innovative mechanical joint designs to ensure a robust and flexible design and optimal structural behaviour.</p></li>
<li><p>Optimizing anchoring and mooring of the full FPV system including it in the whole breakwater design as a critical element affecting its structural behaviour owing to extreme offshore conditions.</p></li>
<li><p>Improved energy connection systems through routing and collection of the DC homeruns and through the physical interface between the floating solar installation and the breakwater.</p></li>
</ol></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Status: Fully achieved at the targeted technology-development level</strong></p>
<p>Objective O1 focused on developing an integrated offshore floating photovoltaic system comprising a protective floating breakwater, reliable coupling between the breakwater and the internal FPV array, optimised anchoring and mooring solutions, and an integrated electrical architecture. The progress and principal achievements are summarised below.</p>
<p><strong>(a) Research and Design of a Novel Floating-Breakwater Concept</strong></p>
<p>An innovative modular floating breakwater was designed and optimised using hydrodynamic calculations, computational fluid dynamics simulations and structural analyses. Its performance was subsequently evaluated through laboratory testing, wave-basin campaigns and marine prototype deployment.</p>
<p>The selected design demonstrated effective attenuation of short-period waves and reduced the motions and connection loads experienced by the protected FPV array. Circular high-performance concrete, lightweight aggregate concrete and cellular-concrete formulations were developed, validated and incorporated into the breakwater prototype.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D3.2, D4.1, D4.2, D4.3, D5.1, D5.3, D6.2 and D6.4.</p>
<p><strong>(b) Coupling Between the External Breakwater and Internal FPV System</strong></p>
<p>Flexible transition elements and mechanical connections were developed to accommodate the different dynamic responses of the comparatively heavy breakwater and the lightweight FPV array.</p>
<p>Laboratory and wave-basin testing identified compression-induced buckling and snap loading in the original FPV hinge. These findings led to the redesign of the connection system, resulting in improved tensile, compressive and fatigue performance. The redesigned hinge was successfully evaluated during the second wave-basin campaign, where no measurable buckling or snap loading was observed under the tested conditions.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D3.1, D3.3, D5.1, D6.1, D6.2 and D6.3.</p>
<p><strong>(c) Optimisation of Anchoring and Mooring Systems</strong></p>
<p>Mooring and anchoring requirements were established for representative deployment conditions in the Baltic Sea, Mediterranean Sea and North Sea. A circular system layout was selected because it provided the most favourable combination of load distribution, anchoring requirements, manufacturability, material use and marine-space utilisation.</p>
<p>Preliminary catenary-chain mooring and anchor designs were developed for the three locations. Subsequent time-domain modelling and optimisation reduced the calculated mooring-system mass by approximately <strong>34–56%</strong> for selected Mediterranean and North Sea configurations while satisfying the applied structural constraints.</p>
<p>The resulting solutions provide a sound basis for further engineering. Final qualification will require coupled dynamic analysis, applicable limit-state checks, fatigue assessment and site-specific geotechnical data.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D2.1, D2.2, D2.3, D3.3, D3.4, D5.1 and D5.7.</p>
<p><strong>(d) Improved Energy-Connection System</strong></p>
<p>An integrated electrical architecture was developed for the offshore FPV installation. The design included:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>collection and routing of DC homeruns through designated corridors;</p></li>
<li><p>combiner boxes for the FPV strings;</p></li>
<li><p>accessible inverter locations on internal breakwater bridges;</p></li>
<li><p>AC cable routing;</p></li>
<li><p>a central transformer interface; and</p></li>
<li><p>provisions for grounding, surge protection, isolation and emergency disconnection.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The electrical architecture was incorporated into the final global system design and considered the effects of seawater exposure, overwash, slamming, ultraviolet radiation, corrosion, biofouling and relative motion between system components.</p>
<p>The proposed reduction in electrical-installation time was not measured through a full-scale comparative installation campaign and therefore remains a projected benefit requiring validation at pilot scale.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D2.2, D2.3 and D3.4.</p>
<p><strong>Overall Achievement of Objective O1</strong></p>
<p>Objective O1 was <strong>fully achieved at the targeted technology-development level</strong>. The project delivered an integrated offshore FPV concept comprising:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>a modular floating breakwater;</p></li>
<li><p>improved FPV hinges and transition connections;</p></li>
<li><p>site-specific anchoring and mooring solutions;</p></li>
<li><p>an integrated electrical architecture; and</p></li>
<li><p>validated modelling and monitoring methodologies.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The concept and its principal components were evaluated through numerical modelling, material and component testing, two wave-basin campaigns and marine prototype deployment. The combined results support classification of the integrated concept at <strong>TRL 5</strong>.</p>
<p><mark>Further progression towards TRL 6 will require a larger open-sea demonstrator, long-term operational monitoring, final component qualification, coupled dynamic and fatigue assessments, site-specific foundation engineering and</mark> <mark>independent certification.</mark></p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D3.4, D5.7, D6.1, D6.2, D6.3, D6.4, D7.2 and D7.6.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>Objective 2 (O2): NEW CIRCULAR CONCRETE MATERIALS DEVELOPMENT:</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><ol type="a">
<li><p>Develop and test innovative circular concrete materials solutions for the external protective breakwater, enabling reduced environmental impacts with lower CO<sub>2</sub> footprint, ensuring structural integrity in severe marine environments (very high wind speed &gt;36 m/s, current &gt;1.2 m/s and waves height &gt;14 m) and improved material durability through corrosion and biofouling resistance.</p></li>
<li><p>Lightweight solution with reduced production &amp; installation costs and easy to cast and transport.</p></li>
</ol></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Status: Fully achieved at material-development and prototype-demonstration level. Cost reductions were not directly quantified at commercial scale.</strong></p>
<p>Objective O2 focused on developing circular concrete solutions for the external floating breakwater that combine reduced environmental impact, structural performance, low density and durability under severe marine conditions. The objective also addressed the manufacturability, handling, transportation and installation of the breakwater.</p>
<p><strong>(a) Development and Testing of Circular Concrete Solutions</strong></p>
<p>Three complementary material families were developed for different functions within the floating-breakwater structure:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Lightweight Aggregate Concrete (LWAC)</strong> for lightweight structural components;</p></li>
<li><p><strong>High-Performance Concrete (HPC)</strong> for highly loaded and exposed parts of the structural shell; and</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Cellular concrete</strong> for the low-density internal core.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>A total of <strong>18 LWAC formulations, 2 HPC formulations and 26 cellular-concrete formulations</strong> were developed and evaluated. The programme covered physical, mechanical and durability properties, including density, compressive strength, bending behaviour, cyclic and impact response, water penetration, chloride migration and shell-core interaction.</p>
<p>The selected LWAC achieved a compressive strength of approximately <strong>68 MPa</strong> and a density of about <strong>1,900 kg/m³</strong>. It incorporated recycled concrete aggregate and recycled-glass lightweight aggregate, reducing the use of virgin raw materials. Also, a low carbon cement CEM III/A was used.</p>
<p>The selected HPC reached approximately <strong>128 MPa compressive strength</strong> and demonstrated superior bending, cyclic, impact and strength-to-weight performance. It was therefore selected for the most highly loaded and exposed parts of the breakwater shell.</p>
<p>The selected cellular concrete achieved a density of approximately <strong>365 kg/m³</strong> and incorporated recycled or secondary materials. Its low density substantially reduced the mass of the breakwater core compared with conventional concrete.</p>
<p>Durability testing confirmed the suitability of the selected structural materials for marine exposure. All selected HPC and LWAC formulations satisfied the applicable water-penetration requirements, while the selected HPC showed no measurable water penetration. Chloride-migration coefficients were approximately <strong>0.4–0.9 × 10⁻¹² m²/s</strong>, indicating extremely high resistance to chloride ingress.</p>
<p>The validated formulations were successfully scaled from laboratory batches to prototype-production volumes and incorporated into the reduced-scale floating-breakwater prototype.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D4.1, D4.2 and D4.3.</p>
<p><strong>(b) Lightweight, Manufacturable and Transportable Breakwater Solution</strong></p>
<p>The combination of HPC, LWAC and cellular concrete enabled a shell-core design in which materials were assigned according to their required function. HPC provided high structural capacity and marine durability, LWAC reduced the mass of the upper structural components, and cellular concrete provided a low-density internal core.</p>
<p>A reduced-scale prototype measuring approximately <strong>3.40 m × 1.70 m × 1.05 m</strong> was successfully manufactured using conventional reinforcement, moulding, casting and curing processes. Approximately <strong>1.5 m³ of HPC, 0.5 m³ of LWAC and 0.7 m³ of cellular concrete</strong> were produced for prototype construction.</p>
<p>The manufacturing campaign demonstrated that the selected circular formulations could be scaled up and cast using practical construction methods. The completed prototype was transported, launched, moored and deployed at the Port of Gijón (Spain), confirming the feasibility of the principal handling and marine-installation operations. These tests provide evidence of TRL 5 verifications.</p>
<p>Following approximately <strong>nine months of marine exposure</strong>, post-deployment inspection found:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>no significant structural deterioration of the concrete;</p></li>
<li><p>no measurable reduction in reinforcement thickness; and</p></li>
<li><p>only localised chloride ingress near openings introduced for monitoring equipment.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>These results supported the durability and constructability of the developed material concept under relevant marine conditions.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D4.2, D4.3 and D6.4.</p>
<p><strong>Circularity and Environmental Performance</strong></p>
<p>The developed formulations reduced reliance on primary materials through the incorporation of recycled concrete aggregate, recycled glass-derived lightweight aggregate, fly ash and ground-granulated blast-furnace slag.</p>
<p>The selected cellular concrete formulation used approximately 25 <strong>kg of cement per m³</strong>, compared with at least <strong>150 kg/m³</strong> for a conventional non-structural concrete reference. This corresponds to an approximate <strong>76% reduction in cement consumption</strong> for this material.</p>
<p>The selected HPC formulation used approximately <strong>380 kg of cement per m³</strong>, compared with the <strong>850 kg/m³</strong> conventional ultra-high-performance concrete reference applied in the project. This corresponds to an approximate <strong>55% reduction in cement consumption</strong> while retaining the required structural and durability performance.</p>
<p>The selected LWAC formulation incorporated low-carbon blast-furnace slag cement (CEM III/A), further <strong>reducing the clinker content and associated carbon emissions by approximately 40-50%</strong> compared with conventional Portland cement (CEM I).</p>
<p>The environmental assessment confirmed the benefits of incorporating recycled, secondary and low carbon materials.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D4.1, D7.3, D7.4 and D7.6.</p>
<p><strong>Deviations and Corrective Actions</strong></p>
<p>The selected cellular-concrete formulation achieved a density of approximately <strong>365 kg/m³</strong>, compared with the original target of about <strong>300 kg/m³</strong>. Although the target was not fully reached, due to material performance and integrity, the material remained substantially lighter than conventional concrete and provided a suitable low-density solution for the breakwater core.</p>
<p>At reduced scale, buoyancy and stability calculations showed that a core made entirely from cellular concrete would require a prototype approaching full-scale dimensions. A low-density expanded-polystyrene component was therefore introduced around the cellular-concrete core to make the reduced-scale prototype feasible. The material was enclosed within the concrete structure, including a protective HPC bottom layer, to prevent direct seawater contact and minimise the risk of material release.</p>
<p>This adaptation was specific to the scale effects of the prototype. Full-scale calculations indicated that a breakwater based on the developed circular concrete solutions could satisfy the required buoyancy and stability conditions without the same scale-dependent constraint.</p>
<p>The original ambition to reduce production and installation costs was supported through lightweight design, modular construction, conventional casting and successful prototype deployment. However, the percentage cost reduction was not directly quantified through parallel manufacture and installation of equivalent conventional and circular full-scale breakwaters.</p>
<p><strong>Overall Achievement of Objective O2</strong></p>
<p>Objective O2 was <strong>fully achieved at the targeted material-development and prototype-demonstration level</strong>. The project:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>developed and evaluated <strong>46 circular concrete formulations</strong>;</p></li>
<li><p>selected structural materials reaching up to <strong>128 MPa compressive strength</strong>;</p></li>
<li><p>developed a cellular-concrete core with a density of approximately <strong>365 kg/m³</strong>;</p></li>
<li><p>demonstrated substantial reductions in cement and virgin-material consumption, thereby promoting circularity and enhancing environmental performance;</p></li>
<li><p>confirmed high resistance to water and chloride penetration;</p></li>
<li><p>successfully manufactured and deployed a floating-breakwater prototype; and</p></li>
<li><p>confirmed satisfactory material and structural condition after approximately nine months of marine exposure.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The lightweight and circular material concept was therefore validated through laboratory characterisation, prototype manufacture and relevant marine exposure. Commercial-scale production, lifecycle-cost reductions and full long-term offshore durability require confirmation through a larger open-sea demonstrator and industrial-scale manufacturing.</p>
<p><strong>Key deliverables:</strong> D4.1, D4.2, D4.3, D6.4, D7.3, D7.4 and D7.6.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><strong>Objective 3 (O3):</strong> <strong>PREDICTIVE COMPUTATIONAL MODELLING &amp; SIMULATION FRAMEWORK DEVELOPMENT COMBINING</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><ol type="a">
<li><p>Improved motion and loading prediction of full FPV system through a high fidelity <strong>coupled aerodynamic and hydrodynamic</strong> modelling tools.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Structural integrity modelling</strong> tool to assess the structural integrity of the most critical parts of the FPV solution by means of advanced finite element techniques.</p></li>
</ol>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Artificial Neural Network (ANN)</strong> based <strong>material properties modelling tool</strong> for optimization of the circular concrete material composition.</p></li>
</ul>
<ol start="3" type="a">
<li><p><strong>Structural Health Management System (SHMS)</strong> to ensure the safe, <strong>reliable and durable operation</strong> of the FPV solution <strong>in service</strong> and to <strong>e</strong>stablish <strong>inspection, maintenance and replacement periods</strong> by combining structural health monitoring, corrosion management and fatigue assessments and thereby reducing CAPEX and OPEX.</p></li>
</ol></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Status: Fully achieved at the targeted modelling, validation and demonstrator level</strong></p>
<p>Objective O3 aimed to develop an integrated computational and monitoring framework for predicting the performance of the offshore FPV system, assessing the structural integrity of critical components, supporting optimisation of circular concrete materials, and enabling condition-based inspection and maintenance.</p>
<p><strong>(a) Coupled Aerodynamic and Hydrodynamic Modelling</strong></p>
<p>Coupled aerodynamic and hydrodynamic modelling tools were developed for representative conditions in the Baltic Sea, Mediterranean Sea and North Sea. The framework combined potential-flow methods, time-domain analysis and high-fidelity CFD simulations to assess:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>wave attenuation by the floating breakwater;</p></li>
<li><p>motions of the breakwater and FPV modules;</p></li>
<li><p>relative motions and loads between adjacent modules;</p></li>
<li><p>aerodynamic lift and drag;</p></li>
<li><p>mooring and anchoring loads;</p></li>
<li><p>standing-wave effects inside the protected area; and</p></li>
<li><p>wave-drift forces acting on the integrated system.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The hydrodynamic models were validated against measurements from the WP6 wave-basin campaigns. The validated framework was subsequently applied to a representative <strong>2 MWp offshore FPV farm comprising 3,660 FPV modules and 32 interconnected floating breakwaters</strong>.</p>
<p>The analyses demonstrated that the breakwater reduced the short-period wave energy reaching the FPV array. Under selected steep irregular-wave conditions, vertical connector loads and overturning moments were reduced by up to approximately <strong>50%</strong>. The simulations also identified standing-wave amplification and increased wave-drift loading, allowing these effects to be considered in the mooring and system design.</p>
<p>Aerodynamic CFD simulations confirmed that the floating breakwater also acts as a wind barrier and can substantially reduce lift and drag forces on the protected FPV array. The effectiveness of this shielding depends on the breakwater height, wind direction, panel inclination and separation distance between the breakwater and FPV modules.</p>
<p>The validated models were integrated into an optimisation workflow using time-domain simulations, surrogate models and genetic algorithms. In selected Mediterranean and North Sea cases, the optimisation reduced calculated mooring-system mass by approximately <strong>34–56%</strong> while satisfying the applied structural constraints.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D5.1, D5.7 and D6.2.</p>
<p><strong>(b) Structural-Integrity and Material-Property Modelling</strong></p>
<p>A detailed finite-element framework was developed in ABAQUS to assess the structural integrity of the floating concrete breakwater and identify critical locations for detailed assessment and monitoring.</p>
<p>The concrete constitutive model was calibrated using experimental results from WP4. The model incorporated more than <strong>2,300 reinforcement bars</strong>, and <strong>33 extreme-load simulations</strong> were completed for representative Baltic Sea, Mediterranean Sea and North Sea conditions.</p>
<p>The analyses identified the mooring-load application regions, connections between breakwater units and nearby geometric discontinuities as the most highly loaded areas. In the assessed cases, the maximum calculated reinforcement stress remained at approximately <strong>7% of the 500 MPa yield strength</strong>, while fatigue-safety factors remained substantially above unity under the modelled non-corroded conditions. These results confirmed considerable structural margins while also providing the basis for defining monitoring locations and fatigue-assessment priorities.</p>
<p>In parallel, the <strong>Surewave-ANN</strong> platform was developed to predict concrete compressive strength from mix-composition parameters. The underlying database contained <strong>1,030 concrete mixtures</strong>, combining experimental results generated in WP4 with published concrete data.</p>
<p>Several machine-learning approaches were evaluated, and a weighted-ensemble model provided the strongest overall predictive performance, with a test root-mean-square error of approximately <strong>3.99 MPa</strong>. A web-based interface was developed to allow users to enter mix-design parameters, predict compressive strength, compare predictions with experimental results and add new mixtures to the database.</p>
<p>The platform supports early screening and optimisation of circular concrete formulations. However, laboratory testing remains necessary for final material qualification, particularly for formulations containing constituents or aggregate characteristics that are insufficiently represented in the training database.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D4.1, D4.2, D5.2, D5.3 and D5.7.</p>
<p><strong>(c) Structural Health Management System</strong></p>
<p>An integrated Structural Health Management System was developed by combining structural analysis, fatigue-crack propagation, corrosion monitoring and remaining-life assessment.</p>
<p>New stress-intensity-factor solutions were generated for narrow elliptical cracks in circular reinforcement bars. The work included <strong>598 finite-element simulations</strong>, covering different crack shapes and relative crack depths. Crack-front development was evaluated at <strong>153 points</strong>, enabling non-uniform crack propagation under complex stress distributions to be represented.</p>
<p>The resulting fatigue algorithms were incorporated into a MATLAB-based SHMS demonstrator with three principal functions:</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>propagation assessment for a defined crack and orientation;</p></li>
<li><p>identification of the most critical crack orientation; and</p></li>
<li><p>determination of the critical initial crack size.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>The SHMS supports remaining-life estimation and risk-based inspection planning. A conservative routine inspection interval of <strong>four years</strong> was implemented, supplemented by exceptional inspections triggered when calculated stress-intensity factors reach predefined proportions of the critical value.</p>
<p>An eddy-current corrosion-monitoring system was also developed to assess reinforcement condition through approximately <strong>5–6 cm of concrete cover</strong>. The sensor technology was initially validated under laboratory conditions and subsequently operated on the breakwater prototype during the WP6 marine campaign.</p>
<p>Environmental compensation reduced the practical variation in the corrosion-sensor response from approximately <strong>±448 Ω to ±135 Ω</strong>, representing an improvement of about <strong>70%</strong>. Wireless accelerometers also operated during the marine-exposure campaign and identified dominant structural-response frequencies in the range of approximately <strong>0.35–0.45 Hz</strong>.</p>
<p>These results demonstrated the feasibility of integrating numerical structural assessment with corrosion and vibration-monitoring data. Longer-term open-sea measurements are still required to validate quantified lifetime extension, maintenance savings and remaining-life predictions.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D5.4, D5.5, D5.6, D5.7 and D6.4.</p>
<p><strong>Validation and Application of the Framework</strong></p>
<p>The modelling and monitoring tools were validated using complementary evidence from WP4 and WP6, including:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>experimental concrete properties;</p></li>
<li><p>ultimate-strength and fatigue testing of critical connections;</p></li>
<li><p>measurements from two wave-basin campaigns;</p></li>
<li><p>marine corrosion and vibration data;</p></li>
<li><p>published analytical and numerical benchmarks; and</p></li>
<li><p>relevant ABS and DNV guidance.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>This validation improved confidence in the predictive framework and supported its application to system optimisation, critical-component identification and inspection planning.</p>
<p>Some limitations remain, including simplified treatment of nonlinear waves, incomplete representation of localised pitting corrosion, absence of full probabilistic uncertainty analysis and the limited duration of marine-monitoring data. These limitations were documented and provide clear priorities for further development.</p>
<p><strong>Overall Achievement of Objective O3</strong></p>
<p>Objective O3 was <strong>fully achieved at the targeted modelling, validation and demonstrator level</strong>. The project delivered:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>validated aerodynamic and hydrodynamic modelling tools;</p></li>
<li><p>an advanced structural-integrity finite-element framework;</p></li>
<li><p>the Surewave-ANN material-property prediction platform;</p></li>
<li><p>fatigue-crack-propagation and remaining-life algorithms;</p></li>
<li><p>corrosion and vibration-monitoring technologies; and</p></li>
<li><p>an operational SHMS demonstrator for condition assessment and inspection planning.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The original ambition of a single AI-supported platform evolved into a connected framework combining physics-based modelling, machine learning, numerical optimisation and sensor-informed structural-health assessment. This adaptation improved technical transparency and allowed each tool to be validated using the most appropriate experimental evidence.</p>
<p>Together, the tools provide a comprehensive digital framework for site-specific design, structural assessment, material optimisation and condition-based maintenance of offshore FPV systems. Further development should focus on probabilistic uncertainty, nonlinear coupled loading, localised corrosion, live integration of sensor data and validation through a larger open-sea demonstrator.</p>
<p><strong>Key deliverables:</strong> D5.1–D5.7 and D6.1–D6.4.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><strong>Objective 4 (O4): ADVANCED TESTING AND VALIDATION FRAMEWORK FOR OFFSHORE FPV</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><ol type="a">
<li><p>To ensure <strong>high level of techno-economic feasibility</strong> of the solution <strong>reducing degradation and failure</strong> rates while ensuring high availability of system operation offshore in harsh conditions (<strong>maximizing energy output</strong>).</p></li>
<li><p>To achieve <strong>high level of fidelity of the simulation framework</strong> developed and validated by advanced lab testing allowing a safe reduction in the over-engineering of devices’ specifications.</p></li>
</ol></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Status: Fully achieved at the targeted laboratory, wave-basin and marine-demonstration level</strong></p>
<p>Objective O4 aimed to validate the technical feasibility, structural reliability and durability of the integrated offshore FPV concept, while generating experimental data for improving and validating the WP5 simulation framework.</p>
<p><strong>(a) System Reliability and Techno-Economic Feasibility</strong></p>
<p>Two <strong>1:10-scale wave-basin campaigns</strong> assessed the integrated floating breakwater, FPV array, mooring system and mechanical connections under representative operational and survival conditions. The breakwater reduced short-period wave energy and FPV motions by factors of approximately <strong>2–3</strong> in the relevant frequency range.</p>
<p>The first campaign identified buckling and snap loading in the original FPV hinge. Following redesign, the second campaign showed no measurable buckling or snap loading under the tested conditions.</p>
<p>Full-scale laboratory testing was also completed for the critical connection systems. The redesigned FPV hinge increased average tensile capacity from approximately <strong>3.69 kN to 8.36 kN</strong>, an improvement of about <strong>127%</strong>, and achieved an average compression capacity of approximately <strong>20.23 kN</strong>. Fatigue assessment indicated compatibility with the intended <strong>25-year service life</strong> under the evaluated loading assumptions.</p>
<p>The concrete breakwater prototype was additionally deployed at the Port of Gijón for approximately <strong>nine months</strong>. Post-exposure inspections identified no significant structural deterioration or measurable reinforcement loss. Corrosion and vibration-monitoring technologies also operated successfully under real marine conditions.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D4.3 and D6.1–D6.4.</p>
<p><strong>(b) Validation of the Simulation Framework</strong></p>
<p>Experimental data from material testing, connection testing, wave-basin campaigns and marine exposure were used to validate the hydrodynamic, structural-integrity, fatigue and Structural Health Management System tools developed in WP5.</p>
<p>The validation improved confidence in predictions of system motions, connector loads, structural stresses, fatigue behaviour and remaining life. It also supported design optimisation, including calculated reductions of approximately <strong>34–56% in mooring-system mass</strong> for selected configurations while maintaining the applied structural constraints.</p>
<p>A single project-wide reduction in over-engineering was not calculated. Further validation is required for nonlinear combined loading, long-term degradation, probabilistic uncertainty and full-scale open-sea operation.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D5.1, D5.3 and D5.5–D5.7.</p>
<p><strong>Overall Achievement of Objective O4</strong></p>
<p>Objective O4 was <strong>fully achieved at the targeted validation level</strong>. The project established an advanced framework combining:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>material and full-scale component testing;</p></li>
<li><p>fatigue assessment of critical connections;</p></li>
<li><p>two integrated wave-basin campaigns;</p></li>
<li><p>marine deployment and monitoring of the breakwater prototype; and</p></li>
<li><p>validation of the WP5 modelling and SHMS tools.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The activities identified and corrected a critical hinge failure mechanism, confirmed the protective effect of the floating breakwater, demonstrated satisfactory prototype durability and generated high-quality datasets for model validation and design optimisation.</p>
<p>The results support classification of the integrated concept at <strong>TRL 5</strong>. Progression towards TRL 6 will require a larger open-sea demonstrator, extended operational monitoring, final component qualification and independent certification.</p>
<p><strong>Key deliverables:</strong> D4.3, D5.1, D5.3, D5.5–D5.7 and D6.1–D6.4.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>Objective 5 (O5): SOCIETAL BENEFITS MAXIMIZATION</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><ol type="a">
<li><p>Social sustainability assessment including social life cycle assessment (sLCA) to identify critical risks early on.</p></li>
<li><p>Societal engagement: Identify stakeholders (e.g. in fishing, navigation, tourism) and take their needs, e.g. on spatial planning into account in designing a compatible solution that receives public acceptance.</p></li>
<li><p>Creating new businesses and job opportunities for on-site shore assembly, transport, installation, O&amp;M.</p></li>
</ol></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Status: Fully achieved at the social-assessment and stakeholder-engagement level. Future employment and business impacts remain prospective.</strong></p>
<p>Objective O5 aimed to assess social sustainability across the offshore FPV value chain, incorporate stakeholder needs into the system’s development, and identify opportunities for new businesses, services and employment associated with future deployment.</p>
<p><strong>(a) Social Sustainability Assessment</strong></p>
<p>A Social Life Cycle Assessment was completed to identify potential social risks and benefits across the offshore FPV value chain. The assessment considered the production of PV modules, aluminium, steel, copper, concrete and other system components, together with installation, operation, maintenance and end-of-life activities.</p>
<p>The principal social-risk hotspots were associated with:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>solar-cell and PV-module manufacturing;</p></li>
<li><p>extraction and processing of aluminium, steel and copper;</p></li>
<li><p>occupational health and safety in manufacturing and offshore operations;</p></li>
<li><p>supply-chain traceability; and</p></li>
<li><p>sourcing from regions with elevated labour and governance risks.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Recommendations were developed for responsible procurement, supplier qualification, improved traceability, supply-chain diversification and increased use of European manufacturing and recycled materials. The assessment also showed that favourable solar resources, shorter electrical connections and reduced material demand can improve the combined social, environmental and economic performance of offshore FPV.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D7.1, D7.5 and D7.6.</p>
<p><strong>(b) Societal Engagement and Stakeholder Involvement</strong></p>
<p>A Stakeholder Advisory Board was established to provide external perspectives on technical development, environmental impacts, investment, regulation and public acceptance. The engagement activities included surveys, interviews, workshops, expert reviews and SWOT analysis.</p>
<p>Stakeholders represented relevant areas such as:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>offshore project development and FPV technology;</p></li>
<li><p>utilities, finance and insurance;</p></li>
<li><p>marine regulation and spatial planning;</p></li>
<li><p>environmental organisations;</p></li>
<li><p>installation and O&amp;M services; and</p></li>
<li><p>research and public policy.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The engagement identified important deployment considerations related to fisheries, navigation, tourism, coastal communities, grid connection, marine ecosystems, offshore-worker safety and competing uses of marine space. The findings confirmed that early engagement with port authorities, fisheries organisations, regulators, environmental bodies and local communities is essential for site selection, permitting and public acceptance.</p>
<p>Stakeholder input was incorporated into the technological, environmental, economic and integrated sustainability assessments and helped refine the recommended market-entry pathway.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D2.4, D8.1, D8.2 and D8.5.</p>
<p><strong>(c) Business and Employment Opportunities</strong></p>
<p>The project identified potential business and employment opportunities throughout the offshore FPV value chain, including:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>production of FPV modules and mechanical connections;</p></li>
<li><p>manufacturing and port-side casting of concrete breakwaters;</p></li>
<li><p>harbour assembly and integration;</p></li>
<li><p>towing, installation and marine operations;</p></li>
<li><p>mooring and anchoring services;</p></li>
<li><p>electrical integration and grid connection;</p></li>
<li><p>inspection, monitoring and predictive maintenance;</p></li>
<li><p>environmental monitoring and biofouling management; and</p></li>
<li><p>engineering, modelling, certification and consultancy services.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Several Key Exploitable Results were defined, including the integrated offshore FPV concept, floating-breakwater and mooring technologies, circular concrete materials, redesigned connection systems, hydrodynamic-modelling tools, corrosion sensors and the Structural Health Management System.</p>
<p>The economic and sustainability assessments identified the Mediterranean as the most promising initial deployment region. The representative 10 MW Mediterranean scenario achieved an estimated LCOE of approximately <strong>€62/MWh</strong>, supporting a credible pathway towards pilot-scale demonstration and subsequent commercial application.</p>
<p>The project did not directly quantify the number of jobs or businesses created because commercial deployment had not begun by project completion. Employment and revenue effects should therefore be treated as prospective impacts dependent on further open-sea demonstration, certification, investment and market uptake.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D1.4, D7.4, D7.6, D8.3, D8.5 and the partner exploitation plans.</p>
<p><strong>Overall Achievement of Objective O5</strong></p>
<p>Objective O5 was <strong>fully achieved at the targeted assessment and engagement level</strong>. The project:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>completed a comprehensive Social Life Cycle Assessment;</p></li>
<li><p>identified supply-chain and occupational social-risk hotspots;</p></li>
<li><p>established and engaged a multidisciplinary Stakeholder Advisory Board;</p></li>
<li><p>incorporated stakeholder needs into the sustainability and deployment assessments;</p></li>
<li><p>identified barriers related to permitting, spatial planning, environmental impacts and public acceptance; and</p></li>
<li><p>defined exploitable technologies and potential business and employment opportunities across the offshore FPV value chain.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Actual job creation, utility uptake and commercial revenues remain post-project impacts. Their realisation will depend on successful open-sea demonstration, qualification and certification, access to investment, responsible supply-chain development and the establishment of commercial offshore FPV projects.</p>
<p><strong>Key deliverables:</strong> D2.4, D7.1, D7.4–D7.6, D8.1–D8.3 and D8.5.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>Objective 6 (O6): ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT MINIMIZATION</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>SUREWAVE will massively extend the area suitable for large-scale PV installation supporting the EU in powering a decarbonized economy in time for the 1.5°C goal.</p>
<ul>
<li><p>A specific <strong>Life Cycle Assessment</strong> (LCA) and elements of <strong>Environmental Impact Assessment</strong> (EIA) will ensure that targeted GHG savings are achieved throughout the supply chain by circularity and that no significant harm is done to the marine ecosystem or any other planetary boundary in addition to the climate.</p></li>
<li><p>A maximized <strong>contribution to the objectives of the Mission Healthy Oceans</strong> by-design, aiming to integrate with the marine economy promoting circular, low-carbon, multi-purpose use of marine space.</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Status: Fully achieved at the environmental-assessment and prototype-monitoring level</strong></p>
<p>Objective O6 aimed to demonstrate that offshore FPV can expand the area available for renewable-energy generation while minimising lifecycle emissions, resource consumption and impacts on marine ecosystems. The work combined Life Cycle Assessment, local environmental-impact assessment, circular-material development and monitoring of the breakwater prototype under real marine conditions.</p>
<p><strong>(a) Life Cycle Assessment and Environmental-Impact Assessment</strong></p>
<p>A cradle-to-grave Life Cycle Assessment was completed for representative deployment scenarios in the Baltic Sea, Mediterranean Sea and North Sea. The assessment covered:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>raw-material extraction and component manufacturing;</p></li>
<li><p>transport and installation;</p></li>
<li><p>operation and maintenance;</p></li>
<li><p>replacement of components;</p></li>
<li><p>decommissioning; and</p></li>
<li><p>end-of-life treatment.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The results showed that electricity generated by the offshore FPV concept could deliver <strong>more than 90% lower greenhouse-gas emissions than coal-based electricity generation</strong>, depending on the deployment scenario and assumptions applied.</p>
<p>The Mediterranean scenario showed the strongest overall environmental performance, primarily because of its higher solar yield and more favourable installation and operating conditions. The principal environmental hotspots were identified as:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>PV-module production;</p></li>
<li><p>vessel operations during installation and maintenance;</p></li>
<li><p>anchoring and mooring components;</p></li>
<li><p>breakwater construction; and</p></li>
<li><p>metals such as aluminium, steel and copper.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>These findings provided clear optimisation priorities, including reducing vessel use, increasing recycled-content materials, extending component lifetime, sharing offshore infrastructure and improving end-of-life recovery.</p>
<p>The local environmental assessment also considered potential effects on marine habitats, flora and fauna, water quality, seabed disturbance, fisheries, navigation, landscape and competing uses of marine space. The assessment concluded that these impacts require site-specific evaluation and can be reduced through appropriate site selection, marine spatial planning, construction scheduling, monitoring and stakeholder engagement.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D7.1, D7.3 and D7.6.</p>
<p><strong>(b) Circularity and Resource Efficiency</strong></p>
<p>The project developed HPC, LWAC and cellular-concrete mixtures containing recycled concrete aggregate, recycled glass, fly ash and ground-granulated blast-furnace slag.</p>
<p>The selected cellular concrete used approximately <strong>36 kg of cement per m³</strong>, compared with at least 150 kg/m³ for the conventional non-structural concrete reference adopted in the project. This corresponds to an approximate <strong>76% reduction in cement consumption</strong>.</p>
<p>The selected structural HPC used approximately <strong>380 kg of cement per m³</strong>, representing about <strong>55% less cement</strong> than the 850 kg/m³ conventional ultra-high-performance concrete reference. Marine-grade aluminium was selected for the FPV floaters because of its corrosion resistance and established recycling pathways.</p>
<p>The project also considered reuse of the long-life breakwater, mooring and anchoring infrastructure for more than one generation of PV modules. However, complete closed-loop recycling of the integrated system was not demonstrated. Further work is required for PV laminates, polyurethane hinges, mixed-material connections and commercial-scale decommissioning.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D3.1, D4.1–D4.3, D7.3 and D7.6.</p>
<p><strong>(c) Protection of the Marine Environment</strong></p>
<p>The breakwater prototype was exposed at the Port of Gijón for approximately <strong>nine months</strong> to assess structural condition, corrosion, chloride ingress, dynamic response and biological colonisation.</p>
<p>Post-exposure inspections found:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>no significant deterioration of the concrete structure;</p></li>
<li><p>no measurable reduction in reinforcement thickness; and</p></li>
<li><p>localised chloride ingress only near openings introduced for monitoring equipment.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Biofouling coverage exceeded <strong>85%</strong> on the assessed surfaces and reached <strong>100%</strong> on one lateral surface. Wet biomass of approximately <strong>6.3–9.7 kg/m²</strong> was measured on the evaluated lateral surfaces. No measurable structural deterioration attributable to biofouling was observed during the monitoring period.</p>
<p>The campaign generated valuable data for future assessment of added mass, hydrodynamic drag, mooring demand, ecological colonisation and cleaning requirements. It did not, however, constitute a long-term ecosystem assessment or demonstrate the absence of environmental effects at commercial scale.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D6.4 and D7.3.</p>
<p><strong>(d) Contribution to the Mission “Healthy Oceans, Seas, Coastal and Inland Waters”</strong></p>
<p>The project contributed to the Mission objective of making the blue economy more carbon-neutral and circular by developing an offshore renewable-energy concept that:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>generates electricity without additional land occupation;</p></li>
<li><p>incorporates recycled and secondary materials;</p></li>
<li><p>supports reuse of long-life marine infrastructure;</p></li>
<li><p>enables multi-purpose use of offshore space; and</p></li>
<li><p>can potentially share electrical and operational infrastructure with offshore wind and other marine-energy activities.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The environmental, social and stakeholder assessments highlighted the need for early engagement with fisheries, navigation interests, port authorities, environmental organisations, regulators and coastal communities. Co-location and shared infrastructure can improve marine-space efficiency, but must be supported by site-specific environmental assessment and marine spatial planning.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D2.4, D7.3, D7.5, D7.6 and D8.2.</p>
<p><strong>Overall Achievement of Objective O6</strong></p>
<p>Objective O6 was <strong>fully achieved at the targeted environmental-assessment and prototype-monitoring level</strong>. The project:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>completed cradle-to-grave environmental assessments for three offshore scenarios;</p></li>
<li><p>demonstrated the potential for <strong>more than 90% lower greenhouse-gas emissions than coal-based electricity</strong>;</p></li>
<li><p>developed and demonstrated circular concrete solutions;</p></li>
<li><p>identified lifecycle environmental hotspots and improvement measures;</p></li>
<li><p>assessed potential local effects on marine ecosystems and competing sea uses; and</p></li>
<li><p>generated real marine data on corrosion, chloride ingress, durability and biofouling.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The results confirm the strong potential of offshore FPV to contribute to European decarbonisation and sustainable use of marine space. Nevertheless, the conclusion that no significant harm will occur at commercial scale requires confirmation through site-specific permitting studies, longer open-sea monitoring and a larger demonstrator. Downstream applications such as renewable hydrogen or synthetic fuels for maritime transport were identified as future opportunities but were not demonstrated within the project.</p>
<p><strong>Key deliverables:</strong> D4.1–D4.3, D6.4, D7.1, D7.3, D7.5, D7.6 and D8.2.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>Objective 7 (O7): HIGH ECONOMIC IMPACT</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>to ensure a feasible and scalable uptake of the solution by the market thanks to:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Reducing Levelized Cost of Energy (LCoE)</strong> to around <strong>54 €/MWh</strong>, making <strong>offshore FPV a competitive renewable energy</strong> (in line with SET Plan targets for offshore wind1F1F1F<a href="#fn1" class="footnote-ref" id="fnref1" role="doc-noteref"><sup>1</sup></a>, e.g. 35-45 €/MWh for fixed, 62-106 €/MWh for floating).</p></li>
<li><p>Ensuring <strong>high replicability</strong> of the solution, also in less demanding conditions (e.g. Mediterranean Sea).</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Reducing investment risk</strong> thanks to smart structural health monitoring and predictive computational tools for ensuring structural integrity to reduce failure and to increase installability and maintainability.</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Status: Largely achieved</strong></p>
<p>Objective O7 aimed to establish a credible pathway for market uptake by reducing LCOE, demonstrating replicability across different offshore environments and reducing investment risk.</p>
<p><strong>Reduction of LCOE</strong></p>
<p>A techno-economic assessment covering <strong>27 cases</strong> was completed for the Mediterranean Sea, Baltic Sea and North Sea. The representative <strong>10 MW Mediterranean case</strong> achieved:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>LCOE:</strong> approximately <strong>€62/MWh</strong></p></li>
<li><p><strong>NPV:</strong> approximately <strong>€8.9 million</strong></p></li>
<li><p><strong>IRR:</strong> approximately <strong>10.6%</strong></p></li>
<li><p><strong>Payback period:</strong> approximately <strong>8 years</strong></p></li>
</ul>
<p>The corresponding LCOE values were approximately <strong>€96/MWh for the Baltic Sea</strong> and <strong>€139/MWh for the North Sea</strong>. The Mediterranean result was approximately <strong>14.8% above</strong> the original target of €54/MWh, but remained at the lower boundary of the SET Plan range cited in the Description of Action for floating offshore wind.</p>
<p>The difference from the original target resulted mainly from more detailed and conservative final assumptions, including updated CAPEX, location-specific O&amp;M costs and a higher discount rate.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D7.1, D7.4 and D7.6.</p>
<p><strong>Replicability and Scalability</strong></p>
<p>The concept was assessed for mild, moderate and harsh conditions in the Baltic, Mediterranean and North Seas. A detailed <strong>7.16 MWp commercial configuration</strong> and a <strong>420 kWp pilot configuration</strong> were developed, together with modular breakwaters, FPV matrices, mooring systems and electrical infrastructure.</p>
<p>The results identified the Mediterranean as the preferred initial market. Baltic deployment remains possible under favourable financing and grid conditions, while North Sea deployment requires further optimisation, shared infrastructure or hybridisation with other offshore energy systems.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D2.1–D2.3, D3.3, D3.4, D5.1, D7.2, D7.4 and D7.6.</p>
<p><strong>Reduction of Investment Risk</strong></p>
<p>Validated modelling, laboratory testing, wave-basin campaigns and marine demonstration reduced uncertainty in structural performance and maintenance requirements. Key results included:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>redesign and validation of the critical FPV hinge;</p></li>
<li><p>approximately <strong>127% improvement in average hinge tensile capacity</strong>;</p></li>
<li><p>calculated mooring-mass reductions of approximately <strong>34–56%</strong> in selected cases;</p></li>
<li><p>validation of the breakwater materials and connections; and</p></li>
<li><p>development of corrosion sensors and a Structural Health Management System for remaining-life and inspection planning.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>These results support future certification, investor due diligence and condition-based maintenance. However, reductions in insurance, financing and O&amp;M costs were not directly measured and require operational evidence from a larger offshore demonstrator.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D5.1, D5.3–D5.7, D6.1–D6.4 and D7.2.</p>
<p><strong>Overall Achievement of Objective O7</strong></p>
<p>Objective O7 was <strong>largely achieved</strong>. The project demonstrated a credible business case for Mediterranean deployment and delivered modular designs, validated digital tools and exploitable technologies that support replication and reduce technical uncertainty.</p>
<p>Although the <strong>€54/MWh</strong> target was not fully reached, the final <strong>€62/MWh</strong> estimate confirmed a competitive pathway for offshore FPV in favourable locations. Further cost reductions will depend on industrial-scale manufacturing, lower financing risk, shared grid infrastructure, certification and learning from a larger open-sea demonstrator.</p>
<p><strong>Key deliverables:</strong> D1.4, D2.1–D2.3, D3.3, D3.4, D5.1–D5.7, D6.1–D6.4, D7.1, D7.2, D7.4, D7.6, D8.3 and D8.5.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>Objective 8 (O8): HIGH EXPLOITABILITY</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><ul>
<li><p>To achieve the best pathways for the commercialization of the global solution a definitive business model and definitive business plan for the exploitation of the solution will be defined. The plan will include specific Offshore Renewable Energy market complexity, IPR, regulatory framework, market up-take barriers, and fostering the large-scale replication into the offshore energy sector.</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Objective O8: High Exploitability</strong></p>
<p>The following activities were completed to achieve Objective O8:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Development of exploitation pathways and business planning:</strong> The project identified and assessed the exploitation potential of the key technologies developed within SUREWAVE, including the floating breakwater concept, circular concrete materials, offshore FPV connection systems, numerical modelling tools, ANN-based material optimisation platform, corrosion-monitoring sensors and the Structural Health Management System (SHMS). Exploitation strategies and ownership arrangements were established through the IPR management framework and continuously updated throughout the project.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Business and market assessment:</strong> Market opportunities, deployment barriers and commercialisation pathways were evaluated as part of the economic, sustainability and exploitation activities. The assessments considered regulatory frameworks, offshore renewable-energy market conditions, technical risks, environmental constraints and stakeholder requirements. The Mediterranean Sea was identified as the most promising initial market, supported by a positive business case with an estimated LCOE of <strong>€62/MWh</strong>, <strong>NPV of €8.9 million</strong> and <strong>IRR of 10.6%</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Commercialisation readiness:</strong> A portfolio of Key Exploitable Results (KERs) was established, covering the integrated SUREWAVE offshore FPV solution as well as individual technologies developed by consortium partners. Commercialisation pathways were defined ranging from further demonstration activities and certification to future pilot deployments and large-scale market uptake. Particular emphasis was placed on integration with offshore renewable-energy infrastructure and future hybrid offshore energy systems.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Replication potential:</strong> The project demonstrated the applicability of the SUREWAVE concept across representative Baltic Sea, Mediterranean and North Sea deployment scenarios, supporting future replication and adaptation to different offshore environments and market conditions.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Achievement of Objective O8:</strong> Objective O8 was <strong>fully achieved</strong>. The project established a clear exploitation framework, identified and protected key exploitable results, evaluated market opportunities and barriers, and developed credible pathways towards commercial deployment. The results provide a strong foundation for future demonstration projects, certification activities and market uptake of offshore floating photovoltaic technologies.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

\#@WRK-PLA-WP@#

### 1.2 Explanation of the work carried out per WP

The SUREWAVE project is dedicated to advancing a sustainable and resilient FPV system that integrates wave breakers for enhanced stability and energy generation. This innovative approach aims to harness solar power while reducing the impact of ocean waves on FPV infrastructure, making it viable for offshore environments where waves can be a significant challenge.

The project structure is organized into several work packages (WPs), each focusing on distinct elements necessary for successful system development. Each work package (WP) covers specific activities, from design and engineering to environmental impact assessments and cost analyses. Below is an overview of the main tasks completed within each WP during the period from M19 to M48:

#### 1.2.1 Work Package 1

Explain the work carried out in WP1 during the reporting period giving details of the work carried out by each beneficiary/affiliated entity involved.

<table style="width:100%;">

<tbody>
<tr>
<td>This WP is devoted to the daily coordination and management of the project, including IPR, financial, legal management, and communication with the EC. The activities involve regular updates of the project status to the EC and implementing effective decision-making procedures and project administration. Additionally, the project encompasses data management, adept risk management, IPR management, and collaborative activities with specific BRIDGE and other EU projects and initiatives to foster synergy</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>During the period from M19 to M48, project management and coordination activities were successfully completed, ensuring the timely execution of technical, administrative and financial aspects of the project. The coordinator maintained regular communication with the PO, proactively reporting deliverable delays, technical deviations and mitigation measures whenever required. Throughout the project, all significant issues were addressed in accordance with the Grant Agreement requirements, ensuring transparency and effective project governance.</p>
<p>The consortium continued to hold regular coordination meetings, including monthly online meetings, dedicated technical workshops and six-monthly face-to-face consortium meetings hosted by project partners. These meetings supported technical integration across work packages, monitoring of risks and milestones, and coordination of validation, demonstration and reporting activities. In addition, targeted technical meetings were organised whenever needed to address specific design, testing and validation challenges.</p>
<p>A major focus during the final reporting period was the successful completion of all remaining project activities, including wave-basin testing, numerical validation, marine demonstration, sustainability assessments and exploitation planning. Continuous monitoring of milestones, deliverables and resource utilisation enabled the consortium to implement corrective actions where necessary and maintain alignment with the project objectives.</p>
<p>The coordinator also managed the internal quality assurance process for project outputs. All project deliverables underwent internal review before submission, involving contributions from work package leaders, consortium experts and designated reviewers. This process ensured the technical quality, consistency and completeness of the submitted results. By the end of the project, all planned deliverables and milestones had been completed, providing a comprehensive set of validated technical, scientific and sustainability outcomes that support the future development and commercialisation of the SUREWAVE concept.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

<td><p><strong><u>Task 1.1: Administrative, legal and financial coordination (SINTEF)</u></strong></p>
<p><strong>A Quality Management Plan</strong> will be defined including project procedures (information, reporting, dissemination, etc.). Simple output indicators will be developed for each WP to monitor the fulfilment.</p>
<p><strong>Administrative assistance</strong> to project partners: SINTEF will act as Project Secretary, assuming: Financial control, review and submission of progress, and continuous reports to the EC.</p>
<p><strong>Project reporting</strong>: SINTEF will assume the liaison between project partners and the EC. The consortium will prepare 2 intermediate Progress Reports and 2 official complete Periodic Reports (M1 to M18 and M19 to M36).</p>
<p><strong><u>Task 1.2: Technical Coordination (SINTEF)</u></strong></p>
<p><strong>Monitoring meetings</strong>: At M1, there will be a Kick-off meeting to review the project schedule, deliverables and performance. The Technical Committee (TC) will meet at least twice a year, with WPs monthly meetings.</p>
<p><strong>Risk Management</strong>: overall risk management: i) Identification of new risks arising; ii) Evaluation of the causes and effects of the identified risks, iii) Definition of alternative tasks/pathways and iv) continuous risks follow-up.</p>
<p><strong><u>Task 1.3: Data Management (L: SINTEF; P: ALL; Duration: M19- M36)</u></strong>: Data Management Plan (DMP), including an analysis of the main elements of the data management policy (IEC 62.531): [1] Data set reference and name; [2] Data set description; [3] Standards and metadata; [4] Data sharing; [5] Archiving and long-term preservation.</p>
<p><strong><u>Task 1.4: IPR Management (L: SINTEF; P: ALL; Duration: M19- M36)</u></strong>: The partners will specify, and agree on, clear IPR management rules in a CA to be signed by all. Unclear IP situations will be identified in the early stages before they create an obstacle for the project. Management of IPR is a key activity since the proposal preparation.</p>
<p><strong><u>Task 1.5: Synergy-building and clustering activities (L: SINTEF; P: ALL; Duration: M19- M36)</u></strong>:</p>
<p>to build fruitful synergies with: 1) previous and ongoing H2020 results (PV systems, new circular materials, floating offshore energy systems-structures, structural health modelling and predictive tools), 2) contributing within the activities of BRIDGE, related to the smart grid integration of the FPV solution as well as related to the business models and citizens engagement barriers; 3) EU platforms (Solar Power Europe, Ocean Energy Europe), (ETIP Ocean)</p></td>
