Progress

<table style="width:100%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p><em>Described the work carried out during the reporting period towards the achievement of each listed objective. Provide clear and measurable details</em>.</p>
<p><strong>O1: Coordinate and organize the project at the strategic level, ensuring project follow-up, project progress control and planning</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status: Completed</strong></p>
<p><strong>This objective is covered by Task 1.1 and partially by Task 1.2.</strong></p>
<p><strong>Task 1.1: Administrative, legal and financial coordination (L: SINTEF; P: ALL; D: M1-M48)</strong></p>
<p>During the M19-M48 reporting period, the coordinator ensured the successful completion of all remaining project activities, deliverables and milestones. The Quality Management Plan (QMP) continued to be applied as the reference framework for project governance, reporting, financial monitoring, quality assurance and risk management.</p>
<p>A total of <strong>11 project deliverables</strong> were prepared, internally reviewed and submitted during the reporting period, covering validation activities, sustainability assessments, dissemination and exploitation planning. Continuous monitoring of deliverables, milestones, person-month consumption and budget utilisation enabled timely identification and mitigation of potential risks. Particular attention was given to coordinating final technical activities, including wave-basin validation, prototype deployment and monitoring, sustainability assessments and preparation of the final project reporting package.</p>
<p>The consortium also completed the implementation of the approved Grant Agreement amendment following the withdrawal of RES Energy Global Services. The redistribution of tasks, deliverables, person-months and budget was successfully managed without affecting the overall project objectives, scope or planned outcomes.</p>
<p><strong>Task 1.2: Technical Coordination (L: SINTEF; P: ALL)</strong></p>
<p>Technical coordination focused on ensuring effective integration of activities across all work packages and the successful completion of validation and demonstration activities. Monthly consortium meetings continued throughout the reporting period, supplemented by dedicated technical meetings addressing numerical validation, wave-basin testing, prototype monitoring, sustainability assessment and exploitation activities.</p>
<p>During the reporting period, the consortium organised:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>The <strong>M48 project meeting hosted by SINTEF</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Multiple dedicated technical workshops supporting validation and demonstration activities.</p></li>
<li><p>Regular monthly consortium meetings for progress monitoring, risk assessment and coordination of deliverables and milestones.</p></li>
<li><p>Final technical coordination meetings supporting preparation of the final reporting package.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The coordinator maintained regular communication with the Project Officer regarding project progress, deliverable submissions, the project extension and final reporting activities. By project completion, <strong>all planned work packages had been completed, all project objectives had been achieved, and the project had progressed from TRL 2-3 to TRL 5 through numerical validation, laboratory testing, wave-basin testing and marine demonstration activities.</strong></p>
<p><strong>Achievement of Objective O1:</strong> Objective O1 was <strong>fully achieved</strong>. The project was successfully coordinated throughout its 48-month duration, resulting in the completion of all planned technical activities, delivery of all project results and effective management of administrative, legal, financial and technical aspects without changes to the overall project scope.</p>
<p><strong>O2: Continuous update of the project status to the EC</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status: Completed</strong></p>
<p><strong>This objective is covered by Task 1.1 and Task 1.2.</strong></p>
<p>During the M19-M48 reporting period, the coordinator maintained continuous communication with the European Commission and the Project Officer to ensure transparent reporting of project progress, achievements, deliverables, milestones, risks and deviations. Regular updates were provided on the status of ongoing technical activities, including wave-basin validation, structural testing, prototype deployment and monitoring, sustainability assessments, exploitation activities and final reporting preparations.</p>
<p>Particular attention was given to communicating the progress of the project extension period, the completion of remaining validation activities, and the implementation of mitigation measures associated with identified technical challenges, such as the redesign and validation of the FPV hinge system. All significant deviations, delays and corrective actions were reported in a timely manner and discussed with the Project Officer to ensure continued compliance with the Grant Agreement.</p>
<p>During the reporting period, the consortium completed and submitted the remaining project deliverables and maintained regular monitoring of milestones, resource utilisation and technical progress. The coordinator also led the preparation of the final technical and financial reporting activities, ensuring that project achievements, impacts and results were properly documented and communicated.</p>
<p><strong>Achievement of Objective O2:</strong> Objective O2 was <strong>fully achieved</strong>. Continuous communication with the European Commission was maintained throughout the reporting period, ensuring timely reporting of project progress, successful management of deviations and risks, and effective completion of all remaining technical, administrative and reporting activities.</p>
<p><strong>O3: Decision-making procedures and suitable project administration</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status: Completed</strong></p>
<p><strong>This objective is covered by Task 1.1 and Task 1.2.</strong></p>
<p>During the M191-M48 reporting period, the established governance structure continued to support efficient management of technical, administrative and financial activities. Regular monthly consortium meetings and dedicated technical workshops were held to monitor progress, review deliverables and milestones, and address risks and deviations.</p>
<p>The consortium successfully managed key project activities, including the completion of validation campaigns, breakwater prototype monitoring, sustainability assessments, exploitation planning and final reporting. Decision-making procedures were also used to address technical challenges, most notably the redesign and validation of the FPV hinge system following testing results.</p>
<p>Project administration activities included monitoring deliverables, milestones, budget utilisation and person-month consumption, as well as implementing the approved Grant Agreement amendment. Internal quality-review procedures were applied to all deliverables before submission.</p>
<p><strong>Achievement of Objective O3:</strong> Objective O3 was <strong>fully achieved</strong>. The project administration and decision-making framework enabled effective coordination of all activities and contributed to the successful completion of all planned deliverables, milestones and project objectives.</p>
<p><strong>O4: Data management, appropriate Risks management, IPR management</strong></p>
<p><strong>Objective O4: Data Management, Risk Management and IPR Management</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status: Completed</strong></p>
<p>The objective related to data management, risk management and IPR management is covered by <strong>Task 1.3</strong> and <strong>Task 1.4</strong>.</p>
<p><strong>Task 1.3: Data Management (L: SINTEF; P: ALL; D: M1-M48)</strong></p>
<p>The Data Management Plan (DMP) continued to guide all data-related activities during the M19-M48 reporting period. The consortium collected, documented, shared and maintained datasets generated through numerical modelling, laboratory testing, wave-basin campaigns, marine demonstration activities, sustainability assessments and techno-economic analyses.</p>
<p>During this reporting period, particular emphasis was placed on consolidating project datasets and ensuring long-term accessibility and reuse of project results. Project-generated data from WP4, WP5, WP6 and WP7 were organised and documented according to the FAIR (Findable, Accessible, Interoperable and Reusable) principles. The project SharePoint environment continued to serve as the main collaborative platform for data storage and exchange, while public datasets and deliverables were prepared for dissemination through appropriate repositories.</p>
<p>The reporting period resulted in the generation and consolidation of several important datasets, including:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Wave-basin test datasets and validation results.</p></li>
<li><p>Structural monitoring and marine demonstration datasets.</p></li>
<li><p>Circular concrete material characterisation datasets.</p></li>
<li><p>Aero-hydrodynamic modelling datasets.</p></li>
<li><p>Mooring and anchoring simulation data.</p></li>
<li><p>Environmental, economic and social assessment datasets.</p></li>
<li><p>Structural integrity and fatigue assessment datasets.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The collected data supported validation activities, sustainability assessments, numerical simulations and preparation of the final project results. Open science requirements continued to be monitored, and data suitable for public release were managed in accordance with the Grant Agreement and DMP.</p>
<p><strong>Task 1.4: IPR and Risk Management (L: SINTEF; P: ALL; D: M1-M48)</strong></p>
<p>Risk management activities continued throughout the reporting period through regular project meetings and technical reviews. The consortium monitored technical, managerial and exploitation-related risks and implemented mitigation measures where necessary. A key example was the identification of limitations in the original FPV hinge design during testing activities, which led to the development and validation of an improved hinge solution without affecting the overall project scope or objectives.</p>
<p>IPR management activities focused on identifying, documenting and protecting project results with exploitation potential. During the reporting period, Key Exploitable Results (KERs) were reviewed and consolidated, including the floating breakwater technology, circular concrete solutions, Surewave-ANN platform, mooring optimisation methodology, corrosion-monitoring sensors, structural integrity assessment tools, fatigue crack propagation algorithms and the Structural Health Management System (SHMS).</p>
<p>Ownership, access rights and exploitation pathways were continuously reviewed to support future commercialisation and uptake of project results. No major IPR disputes or ownership conflicts were identified during the project, and dissemination activities were carried out in accordance with the established review and approval procedures.</p>
<p>The risk management and IPR frameworks contributed significantly to the successful completion of the project and the preparation of future exploitation activities.</p>
<p><strong>Achievement of Objective O4:</strong> Objective O4 was fully achieved. Effective data management ensured the availability, quality and long-term usability of project data, while systematic risk and IPR management supported successful project execution and the protection and future exploitation of project results.</p>
<p><strong>O5: Synergy-building and clustering activities with specific BRIDGE and other EU projects and initiatives.</strong></p>
<p>The activities associated with this objective was started in M13. We have been trying to create collaborations and partnerships with various stakeholders involved in these projects. In the last six months we had a meeting with HEU funded project NatureSea-PV and we agreed to organize a joint workshop.</p>
<p><strong>O5: Synergy-building and clustering activities with specific BRIDGE and other EU projects and initiatives</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status: Completed</strong></p>
<p><strong>This objective is covered by Task 1.5.</strong></p>
<p><strong>Task 1.5: Synergy-building and clustering activities (L: SINTEF; P: ALL; D: M1-M48)</strong></p>
<p>During the M191-M48 reporting period, the consortium continued its synergy-building and clustering activities with European projects, industrial stakeholders and sectoral platforms to maximize the impact and exploitation potential of the SUREWAVE results.</p>
<p>A close collaboration was established with the <strong>NaturSea-PV</strong> project, funded under the same Horizon Europe call. The collaboration included regular exchanges of technical knowledge, dissemination activities and joint discussions on offshore floating photovoltaic technologies. The two projects jointly organized webinars and workshops to share experiences and promote collaboration within the emerging offshore FPV sector.</p>
<p>SUREWAVE actively participated in the <strong>BRIDGE</strong> initiative, contributing to discussions related to renewable energy integration, digitalisation and innovative energy systems. Participation in BRIDGE activities enabled knowledge exchange with other Horizon 2020 and Horizon Europe projects and increased the visibility of SUREWAVE results within the European energy innovation community.</p>
<p>The consortium also engaged with relevant European networks and stakeholder groups, including organisations active in offshore renewable energy, solar energy and marine technologies. These interactions provided valuable feedback on technological developments, market barriers, regulatory frameworks and future deployment opportunities for offshore floating photovoltaics.</p>
<p>Through these activities, SUREWAVE strengthened its links with the wider European offshore renewable energy ecosystem, supported dissemination and exploitation of project results, and identified opportunities for future collaborations and follow-up projects.</p>
<p><strong>Achievement of Objective O5:</strong> Objective O5 was <strong>fully achieved</strong>. The project successfully established collaborations with sister projects, contributed to BRIDGE activities, engaged with relevant European stakeholder networks, and created a strong foundation for future cooperation, dissemination and exploitation of offshore floating photovoltaic technologies.</p></td>
</tr>

**Publishable summary**

<table style="width:100%;">

<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p>Work Package 2 (WP2), of the SUREWAVE project, titled 'Global Framework Definition &amp; General Specifications of the Floating PV Solution,' is tasked with determining the comprehensive and detailed requirements for feasible floating photovoltaic (FPV) installations, explicitly focusing on highly challenging conditions including high wind (speeds above 36 m/s), strong current (over 1.2 m/s), large waves (heights above 14 m), and issues related to corrosion and biofouling. The package also considers installation, operation and maintenance requirements, and decommissioning procedures. The main objectives of WP2 are:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Establish a technical Stakeholders-Advisory Board (SAB)</p></li>
<li><p>Research, analysis, and preliminary requirements of critical components for SUREWAVE FPV concepts.</p></li>
<li><p>Integration requirements with breakwater and other critical components</p></li>
<li><p>Use-case scenario definition</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;">The WP2 has completed most of the tasks except continue engagement with the advisory board.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

<tr>
<td><p><strong>Objective 1: Establish a technical Stakeholders-Advisory Board (SAB)</strong></p>
<p>This objective is linked to Task 2.4. During the first reporting period, a Technical Stakeholders-Advisory Board (SAB) was established to provide valuable insights and guidance for the offshore Floating Photovoltaic (FPV) industry. The SAB consists of seven experts representing key sectors, including project development, technology, regulation, finance, insurance, end-users (energy utilities), installers, and policymaking. In the subsequent period (M19–M36), discussions were held with SAB members regarding the organization of a workshop aimed at disseminating project results. A follow-up meeting took place in M30, and preparations for a joint workshop are ongoing.</p>
<p><strong>Objective 2: Research, analysis, and preliminary requirements of critical components</strong></p>
<p>This objective was associated with Task 2.2 which is completed</p>
<p>Status: Completed:</p>
<p><strong>Objective 3: Integration requirements with breakwater and other critical components</strong></p>
<p>This objective was associated with Task 2.3 which is completed</p>
<p>Status: Completed:</p>
<p><strong>Objective 4: Use-case scenario definition</strong></p>
<p>This objective was associated with Task 2.1 which is completed</p>
<p>Status: Completed:</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

**Significant Results**

<table style="width:100%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><ol type="1">
<li><p>HSE Considerations for O&amp;M: Focus on HSE requirements enhances safety protocols and enables remote monitoring and predictive maintenance, improving operational efficiency.</p></li>
<li><p>Decommissioning Requirements: Early definition of decommissioning requirements ensures environmentally responsible practices and enhances sustainability.</p></li>
<li><p>Improved Cost Projections: Including decommissioning costs in LCoE calculations leads to more accurate project assessments and better financial planning.</p></li>
<li><p>Enhanced Industry Relevance: Stakeholder engagement ensures project outcomes align with industry needs and regulations, promoting widespread adoption of floating PV solutions and advancing the offshore FPV industry.</p></li>
</ol></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

**Exploitation of results**

<table style="width:100%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><p>The main exploitable outcome of WP2 is the <strong>common design basis for offshore FPV</strong>, covering representative deployment scenarios, environmental loads, system requirements, installation constraints and lifecycle assumptions. These results provide reusable inputs for future engineering studies, site screening, system dimensioning and early-stage cost assessment.</p>
<p>A particularly relevant exploitation pathway is the improvement of lifecycle-cost projections. The WP2 design basis enables technical risks, environmental severity, design life, installation requirements and decommissioning activities to be incorporated more consistently into LCOE calculations. Including decommissioning, component replacement and risk-related cost assumptions provides investors, financial institutions, insurers and project developers with a more realistic view of long-term project performance.</p>
<p>The results can therefore be exploited to:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>support preliminary feasibility and bankability assessments for offshore FPV projects;</p></li>
<li><p>compare deployment opportunities in mild, moderate and harsh offshore environments;</p></li>
<li><p>improve CAPEX, OPEX and decommissioning-cost estimates;</p></li>
<li><p>reduce uncertainty in LCOE and investment-risk calculations;</p></li>
<li><p>provide design inputs for breakwaters, FPV modules, mooring, anchoring and electrical systems; and</p></li>
<li><p>support discussions with investors, insurers, certification bodies and permitting authorities.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The WP2 results were subsequently used throughout the project in the system design, modelling, testing and sustainability assessments. They also provide a transferable methodology for future offshore FPV projects. However, the resulting cost projections remain dependent on site-specific metocean conditions, water depth, seabed characteristics, grid-connection distance, installation strategy and financing assumptions.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

#### 1.2.3 Work Package 3 – Development of the floating PV solution based on the breakwater concept

**Publishable summary**

<table style="width:100%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p>This Work Package (WP3) focuses on the development of floating photovoltaic (PV) systems, which incorporate wave attenuation through a modular floating breakwater. In the context of Work Package 2 (WP2) environmental conditions, researchers investigate various sizes and shapes of modular breakwater pontoons to achieve optimal wave attenuation effects. The layout arrangement of breakwater elements is carefully examined, considering interactions with necessary anchoring and mooring systems. Additionally, the work package addresses connections between individual breakwater components and their integration with the PV system. The floating PV modules are specifically adapted and optimized for offshore conditions, including those behind the breakwater. These are objectives:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Investigate different sizes and shapes of modular breakwater pontoons to achieve optimal wave attenuation effects.</p></li>
<li><p>Examine the arrangement of breakwater elements in the layout, considering interactions with necessary anchoring and mooring systems.</p></li>
<li><p>Address connections between individual breakwater components and their integration with the photovoltaic (PV) system.</p></li>
<li><p>Adapt and optimize floating PV modules for offshore conditions, including those behind the breakwater.</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>As part of WP3, the following activities were completed during M19–M48. Although the main WP3 design activities were technically completed during the first reporting period, the breakwater design was subsequently updated to accommodate the final material solutions developed in WP4. These included high-performance concrete, lightweight aggregate concrete and cellular concrete for the structural shell, upper slab and inner core.</p>
<p>The target density of the cellular-concrete core was initially <strong>300 kg/m³</strong>, while the selected formulation achieved approximately <strong>365 kg/m³</strong>. The breakwater geometry and material distribution were therefore revised to maintain the required structural performance, buoyancy and stability.</p>
<p>The original system concept was based on a single <strong>10 MW installation</strong>. Further design development showed that such a large continuous system would present significant challenges for access, inspection, maintenance and component replacement. The commercial architecture was therefore revised to a modular arrangement comprising <strong>five 2 MW units</strong>. This maintained the overall 10 MW capacity ambition while improving modularity, maintainability, installation planning and the possibility of isolating individual units during</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

**Objectives**

|  |
|----|
| Building on the research findings and specifications from WP2, specifically tasks 2.3 and 2.4, this WP aims to develop an innovative floating PV system concept with integrated wave protection. The design is guided by a close feedback loop with WP4 and WP5, which focuses on circular material solutions and predictive tools for material properties. This collaboration enables the creation of a PV floating system with an external wave protection structure (breakwater or damper) constructed from sustainable, circular materials. |

**Work planned for this reporting period (M19-M48)**

<table style="width:100%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Task 3.1. Overall design of the internal individual floating PV components (Leader: Sunlit Sea)</strong></p>
<p><em><mark>Status: Completed</mark></em></p>
<p><strong>Task 3.2: Overall design of the external breakwater (L: CLEMENT; P: SIS, MARIN, ACC; Duration: M5-M10):</strong></p>
<p><em><mark>Status: Completed</mark></em> for the baseline materials, with updates reflecting the new material properties for the inner and outer cores.</p>
<p><strong>Task 3.3: Improved mooring line and anchoring system (Leader: CLEMENT; Participants: SIS, ACC; Duration: M9-M12):</strong></p>
<p><em><mark>Status: Completed</mark></em>. Initially, the project aimed for a 10 MW design, but this size proved to complicate for operation and maintenance. Therefore, the decision was made to design a system of five 2 MW units instead. Therefore, both the mooring line and the anchoring system were designed for the 2 MW system as well.</p>
<p><strong>Task 3.4. Development of connection system between breakwater and solar modules (Leader: CLEMENT)</strong></p>
<p><em><mark>Status: Completed</mark></em></p>
<p><strong>Task 3.5. Design of coupled breakwater &amp; floating PV and global system design (Leader: Sunlit Sea)</strong></p>
<p><em><mark>Status: Completed</mark></em></p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

**Progress**

<table style="width:100%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Objective:</strong> To develop the novel concept of PV floating system with external wave protection “breakwater/damper” made of circular materials.</p>
<p>The objective was successfully achieved during the first reporting period. Targeted design updates were subsequently completed during M19–M48 to incorporate the final material properties and improve the system’s modularity and maintainability.</p>
<p><strong>Task 3.1. Overall design of the internal individual floating PV components (Leader: Sunlit Sea)</strong></p>
<p><em><mark>Duration: M5-M9</mark></em></p>
<p><em><mark>Status: Completed</mark></em></p>
<p><strong>Task 3.2. Overall design of the external breakwater (Leader: CLEMENT)</strong></p>
<p><em>Duration: M5-M11</em></p>
<p><em>Status: Completed, but based on results of subsequent tasks, improvements have been implemented</em></p>
<p><em>Although this task was completed, further improvements of the floating breakwater (FB) were made to account for density. The target density for the inner core was set at 300 kg/m³, but the achieved density reached 360 kg/m³. A comparison is provided in the following text.</em></p>
<p></p>
<p>Figure 1 - Rendering of the chosen floating breakwater (FB) pontoon.</p>
<p><em><strong>CLEMENT</strong> led the overall design of the external floating breakwater, including its geometry, structural arrangement, buoyancy, stability and modular configuration. <strong>ACC</strong> developed the HPC, LWAC and cellular-concrete solutions and provided the final material properties required for the design updates. <strong>SIS</strong> contributed the FPV-system geometry and interface requirements, while <strong>MARIN</strong> supported the hydrodynamic assessment of the integrated breakwater and FPV system.</em></p>
<p><em>Density of concrete for FB elements</em></p>
<p>Based on the results of Deliverable D4.1 on the concrete mix designs for the outer shell and the core, the effect on floatability and floating stability was investigated, because the density differed from initial assumptions. Two approaches were considered:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>The external curvature has been kept constant. The higher densities result in a reduced freeboard and a different metacentric height.</p></li>
</ul>
<ul>
<li><p>Freeboard and draught remain constant. This has been achieved by reducing the height of the keels and increasing the height of the buoyancy compartment.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The following cases are determined:</p>

<p><em>Connections between FB elements</em></p>
<p>The connections between FB elements are designed as hinges with load and moment transfer, except rotation around the y-axis. The principle is shown in the following <a href="#_Ref179527049">Figure 2</a>:</p>
<p><span id="_Ref179527049" class="anchor"></span>Figure 2 - Principle of FB connections</p>
<p>In relation to Deliverable D5.1, the dynamic behaviour of the breakwaters at the selected sites was investigated. As a result, the forces and moments to be transmitted by the pontoon couplings between the individual breakwater elements were determined.</p>
<p></p>
<p>Figure 3 - Mean 3-hour connector splitting loads (summarised from deliverable D5.1)</p>
<p></p>
<p>Figure 4 - Mean 3-hour connector horizontal moments (summarsed from deliverable D5.1)</p>
<p>The steel ropes for these connections need to be of a higher strength than ropes used for other applications such as marinas or smaller breakwater pontoons.</p>
<p></p>
<p>Figure 5 - Minimum breaking load of steel ropes</p>

<p><strong>Task 3.3. Improved mooring line and anchoring system (Leader: CLEMENT)</strong></p>
<p><em><mark>Duration: M9-M15</mark></em></p>
<p><em><mark>Status: Completed. The task</mark></em> <em><mark>was</mark></em> <em><mark>further modified to account for the 2 MW size.</mark></em></p>
<p><em><strong>CLEMENT</strong> led the development of the system layout and the preliminary design of the mooring lines, anchoring system and breakwater-to-FPV transition elements. <strong>SIS</strong> provided the FPV-array configuration and the functional requirements for coupling the internal FPV system to the external breakwater. <strong>ACC</strong> supplied the breakwater mass, buoyancy and material data required for the mooring and anchoring calculations.</em></p>
<p>For comparison, a layout size and associated circle diameter was also selected that contained only one-fifth of the FPV panels (2 MWp) compared to the layout with 10 MWp. This layout only has 32 FB pontoons and an outer diameter of 173 m. The purpose of this comparison was to see how the size of the plant affects the anchoring design.</p>

<p>Figure 7 - Layout details</p>
<p>The following assumptions were made for the design of the anchorage:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>The layout of the system was circular with a total diameter of 346 meters for 10 MWp and 173 meters for 2 MWp respectively.</p></li>
<li><p>Anchor points outside the layout were selected in the required number according to structural requirements. A uniform spacing was maintained.</p></li>
<li><p>Anchor chains were also arranged on the opposite side (inwards) to balance the tension of the chains from the outside.</p></li>
</ul>
<p></p>
<p>Figure 8 - Principle of section of FB with anchor chains</p>
<ul>
<li><p>The load capacity of the chains was always determined according to the maximum forces from deliverable D3.2. The forces were applied in the pulling direction of the chains. To be on the safe side, the effective width of the circular layout for an anchorage point was assumed to be straight and the above forces were scaled up accordingly.</p></li>
<li><p>The water depths from deliverable D2.1 were selected for the respective location. Because of variations in water depths at these sites, it demonstrates that the design approach can be extended or adapted to different locations as well.</p></li>
<li><p>The displacements from deliverable D3.2 were used to determine the required chain length. It was assumed that the chains must be able to follow these displacements. As these displacements have been determined on the unanchored system, it is expected that they will be lower when an anchorage is considered. The approach is thus on the safe side.</p></li>
<li><p>The chain length was also selected in such a way that even with the greatest displacements at the anchor point, the catenary line only introduces horizontal forces.</p></li>
<li><p>The soil conditions for the driven piles were unknown and were therefore assumed to be normal medium-dense sand with the following soil parameters:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Density of soil <span class="math inline">$\gamma_{k} = 18.0\frac{kN}{m³}$</span></p></li>
<li><p>Density of soil under buoyancy <span class="math inline">${\gamma'}_{k} = 10.5\frac{kN}{m³}$</span></p></li>
<li><p>Angle of friction <span class="math inline"><em>φ</em><sub><em>k</em></sub> = 32.5<sup>∘</sup></span></p></li>
</ul></li>
<li><p>Corrosion was to be considered at the pile to ensure durability. Bureau Veritas [5] recommends for steel structures under the mudline a minimum corrosion margin of 0.1 mm/year per metal face exposed to the soil. A reduction of wall thicknesses was expected with 8 mm in total.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Anchorage for 2 MWp plant size:</strong></p>
<p>The design of the anchor chain and anchor pile follows the principles set out in D3.3. Furthermore, the anchor chains have already been optimised using the results from D5.1, whilst the pile dimensions initially serve only to confirm technical feasibility.</p>

**Significant Results**

<table style="width:100%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p>WP3 delivered the following principal results:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>An optimised floating-breakwater cross-section and material composition.</p></li>
<li><p>Quantification of breakwater hydrodynamic behaviour and wave-transmission performance.</p></li>
<li><p>Selection of the preferred breakwater and FPV system layout.</p></li>
<li><p>Preliminary anchoring and mooring designs for 2 MW units under Baltic, Mediterranean and North Sea conditions.</p></li>
<li><p>Functional design of the FPV hinges, breakwater connections and breakwater-to-FPV transition system.</p></li>
<li><p>Completion of the integrated global system design.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The mechanical connections were recognised as critical to system reliability and load transfer. Their material selection and geometry were therefore iteratively optimised and subsequently validated through modelling and testing.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

**Exploitation of results**

<table style="width:100%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Innovative floating-breakwater design:</strong> The project developed an integrated modular floating-breakwater concept to protect offshore FPV installations from wave and wind loading. The design can be further exploited in floating-solar projects and adapted for other marine applications, including aquaculture installations, marinas and floating infrastructure. Its commercial value lies in improving the survivability and deployment range of floating assets in exposed coastal and offshore environments.</p>
<p><strong>Exploitation pathway:</strong> The concept can be exploited through engineering and design services, technology licensing, joint development with offshore-energy developers, and integration into future pilot and commercial projects. Further open-sea demonstration, component qualification and certification will be required before commercial deployment.</p>
<p><strong>Floating breakwater using circular materials:</strong> The breakwater combines a durable concrete shell with a lightweight core incorporating circular concrete materials. The approach reduces reliance on conventional concrete and primary raw materials while maintaining the required structural performance, buoyancy and marine durability. A limited number of EPS was used in the reduced-scale prototype because of scale-dependent buoyancy constraints; this was enclosed within the concrete structure to prevent direct contact with seawater.</p>
<p><strong>Exploitation pathway:</strong> The lightweight shell-core concept can be adapted to concrete pontoons, floating breakwaters, docks and other buoyant marine structures. Potential exploitation routes include licensing the material and structural concept to marine-construction companies, providing specialised concrete formulations, and offering design and engineering services to renewable-energy and infrastructure developers. Industrial-scale optimisation is still needed to demonstrate cost competitiveness and eliminate or minimise persistent polymer content in future full-scale applications.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

**Deviations**

<table style="width:100%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p><em><strong>Density of structural shell</strong></em>: As the results of Task 4.1 were not available when Task when Task 3.2 was completed, the interim values for density of the structural shell was assumed to be 1,900 kg/m³. Although this density was achieved with Sample 17 in Task 4.1, Sample 18 has better strength properties but also a higher density of 2,000 kg/m³. As greater strength is required, particularly in the area of high load applications, it is proposed to use the values from Sample 18. But the higher density has an impact on the total weight of the breakwater and therefore on the buoyancy and stability properties.</p>
<p><em><strong>Density of inner light weight core:</strong></em> As the results of Task 4.2 were not available when Task 3.2 was completed, the target density of the material for the inner core of the breakwater was assumed to be 300 kg/m³. However, this value was exceeded by 65 kg/m³. This higher density has an impact on the total weight of the breakwater and therefore on the buoyancy and stability properties.</p>
<p><em><strong>Continuation of the WP</strong></em>: The duration of WP3 was originally planned between months 5 and 16. Therefore, the WP should have been finished in the first reporting period. However, based on the results of subsequent tasks, there were some issues/improvements that needed to be addressed. The following subjects were affected:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>As part of the investigations for D5.1, the dynamic behaviour of the entire breakwater ring, including anchoring, was investigated and related <em><strong>mooring line loads</strong></em> were determined.</p>
<p>For the selected locations in the Mediterranean and the North Sea, the previously determined mooring line loads showed potential for optimisation, so that a revision of the sizing of anchor chains seemed reasonable.</p>
<p>For the selected location in the Baltic Sea, the permissible chain loads were exceeded due to the shallow water depth and the associated limited range of motion. Therefore, both the chain size and the chain lengths were adjusted to meet the structural requirements..</p></li>
<li><p>The investigation of the dynamic behaviour of the entire breakwater ring also included the determination of forces and moments in the <em><strong>breakwater connections</strong></em>. The connection elements could be specified and quantified more precisely on the basis of these results. At the same time, the strength properties of the concrete for the outer shell from D4.1 could also be included in this design.</p></li>
<li><p>For comparison, a layout size and associated circle diameter was also assessed that contained only on-fifth of the FPV panels compared to the reference layout of 10 MW. This should demonstrate the impact that the size of the overall plant has in terms of loads, material quantities, and qualities.</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

**Corrective actions**

<table>
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 100%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p><em><strong>Density of structural shell and inner light weight core:</strong></em> The impact of the increased density was investigated and summarized in an appendix to D3.2. To compensate for the additional weight, the core of the breakwater pontoons was made partly of polystyrene. This has the added benefit of making complex core shapes in polystyrene easier and more economical to produce. The dynamic behaviour and wave breaking effect is unaffected.</p>
<p><em><strong>Continuation of the WP</strong></em>: Improvements/modifications needed to be addressed to implement the results of subsequent tasks, which made the overall system more robust. The results related to these investigations were compiled on appendices to D3.2 and D3.3.</p>
<p>There was no influence on other tasks or the overall project schedule.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="3" style="text-align: left;">WP4 NEW CIRCULAR MATERIAL SOLUTIONS</td>
<td style="text-align: left;"><strong>Aurora María Casado Barrasa</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="3" style="text-align: left;">REPORTING PERIOD</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">REPORTING RESPONSIBILITY</td>

<td style="text-align: left;">From:<br />
01.04.2024</td>

30.09.2026</td>

Publishable summary

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 98%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p>WP4 will develop novel circular material solutions for the floating breakwater with high structural integrity, mechanical, physical and durability properties.</p>
<p>The specific objectives are:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Design of a set of new circular concrete solutions for the structural shell.</p></li>
<li><p>Design of a set of new circular concrete solutions for the inorganic inner core.</p></li>
<li><p>Analysis and selection of the optimal concrete solutions for the breakwater attending to the different combinative shell-inner core alternative options.</p></li>
<li><p>Desing and prototyping of the floating breakwater.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p>During the first reporting period, several concrete mixes for each type of concrete were developed changing compositions, water-binder ratios and, in some cases, curing conditions to meet the technical requirements. In a second step, recycled and other secondary materials were incorporated in the concrete mixes to make them circular.</p>
<p>In particular, eighteen LWAC and two HPC formulations were casted for the structural shell. Twenty-six different cellular concrete formulations were developed for the non-structural inner core.</p>
<p>The next step after selecting the optimal concrete mixes was to carry out a series of mechanical tests. These included three-point bending tests (both static and cyclic), pullout tests, and impact tests, aimed at characterizing the behavior of the interface between the shell and inner core.</p>
<p>During the period from M19 to M36, the final stage involved integrating the shell and inner core, which led to the successful development of a prototype for the floating breakwater. This breakwater prototype was subsequently tested as a part of WP6 in the open sea environment. Before creating the breakwater prototype, it was essential to conduct water and chloride penetration tests. These durability tests ensure that the prototype can withstand the corrosive and harsh environment when exposed to seawater. These tests were carried out on the optimal circular concrete solutions.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

Objectives

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 98%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p>WP4 specific objectives are:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Design of a set of new circular concrete solutions for the structural shell.</p></li>
<li><p>Design of a set of new circular concrete solutions for the inorganic inner core.</p></li>
<li><p>Analysis and selection of the optimal concrete solutions for the breakwater attending to the different combinative shell-inner core alternative options.</p></li>
<li><p>Desing and prototyping of the floating breakwater.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>

Work planned for this reporting period (M19-M48)

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 98%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>Task 4.1. Research and design of new circular material solutions for the structural shell of the breakwater</strong></p>
<p><strong>(Leader: ACC; Participants: SINTEF, CLEMENT; Duration: M5-M15)</strong></p>
<p><strong>Task 4.2. Research and design of new circular material solutions for the inner core of the breakwater</strong></p>
<p><strong>(Leader: ACC; Participants: SINTEF, CLEMENT; Duration: M5-M15)</strong></p>
<p><strong>Task 4.3. Durability assessment of the interface of shell &amp; inner core</strong></p>
<p><strong>(Leader: SINTEF; Participants: CEIT, ACC, CLEMENT; Duration: M12-M18)</strong></p>
<p><mark>Task 4.1 to 4.3 were completed in the first reporting period</mark></p>
<p><strong>Task 4.4. Floating concrete breakwater solution and prototyping (Leader: ACC; Participants: SINTEF, CLEMENT; Duration: M18-M20)</strong></p>
<p>This task intends to integrate the shell and the inner core to develop a prototype of the breakwater. The first step will be to analyse and select the optimal solution of circular concrete breakwater attending to the different combinative alternative options of structural shell solutions with the inner core solutions. Then, a small prototype of the breakwater will be cast. Once this prototype has been cast, it will be tested in harsh environmental conditions in T6.5.</p></th>

Progress

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 98%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p>O1: Design of a set of new circular concrete solutions for the structural shell.</p>
<p>O2: Design of a set of new circular concrete solutions for the inorganic inner core.</p>
<p>O3: Analysis and selection of the optimal concrete solutions for the breakwater attending to the different combinative shell-inner core alternative options.</p>
<p><mark>Objectives O1, O2, and O3 are associated with Tasks 4.1, 4.2, and 4.3. These objectives were achieved in the first reporting period.</mark></p>
<p><strong>O4: Design and prototyping of the floating breakwater</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status: Completed</strong></p>
<p><strong>Objective O4 was fully achieved.</strong> The project successfully designed, manufactured and validated a reduced-scale floating breakwater prototype (<strong>3.4 m × 1.7 m × 1.05 m</strong>) using the circular concrete solutions developed within WP4. This represents one of the major technical outcomes of the SUREWAVE project and provided the basis for the subsequent marine demonstration and monitoring activities carried out in WP6. This objective is covered by Task 4.4</p>
<p><strong>Task 4.4. Floating concrete breakwater solution and prototyping (Leader: ACC; Participants: SINTEF, CLEMENT; Duration: M18-M20)</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status:</strong> Completed</p>
<p><strong>ACC</strong> led the detailed design, manufacture and assembly of the reduced-scale floating concrete breakwater prototype. This included adapting the shell-core configuration to the final HPC, LWAC and cellular-concrete properties developed in WP4, scaling up the selected formulations, casting the structural components and integrating the lifting, mooring and monitoring interfaces.</p>
<p><strong>CLEMENT</strong> provided the breakwater geometry, structural requirements, buoyancy and stability inputs needed to translate the full-scale concept into the reduced-scale prototype. <strong>SINTEF</strong> contributed marine-deployment requirements, environmental-load considerations and technical support for the prototype’s instrumentation, monitoring and subsequent performance assessment.</p>
<p>During the reporting period, the final circular concrete solutions were validated, selected and incorporated into the breakwater prototype. Durability testing, including water penetration and chloride penetration assessments, was completed on the selected LWAC and HPC formulations to verify their suitability for long-term marine exposure. All selected formulations demonstrated excellent durability performance, meeting the requirements for reinforced concrete structures operating in aggressive marine environments. The selected HPC solution exhibited no measurable water penetration, while all tested solutions showed very high resistance to chloride ingress.</p>
<p>Following material validation, the breakwater prototype was manufactured using a combination of:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>High-Performance Concrete (HPC) for the structural shell,</p></li>
<li><p>Lightweight Aggregate Concrete (LWAC) components,</p></li>
<li><p>Cellular lightweight concrete core material,</p></li>
<li><p>Recycled and secondary raw materials, including recycled construction and demolition aggregate, recycled glass-derived lightweight aggregate and industrial by-products.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>A dedicated manufacturing process was developed and successfully implemented, demonstrating the practical feasibility and scalability of the circular breakwater concept. The prototype was designed to withstand offshore environmental loads while maintaining buoyancy, durability and structural integrity.</p>
<p>The completed prototype was subsequently equipped, prepared and deployed for long-term exposure and monitoring in the Port of Gijón, providing a real-scale validation platform for durability, structural performance, monitoring technologies and environmental exposure assessment activities conducted in WP6.</p>
<p><strong>Main achievements:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Completion of durability validation for the selected LWAC and HPC concrete solutions.</p></li>
<li><p>Demonstration of excellent water- and chloride-penetration resistance for all selected circular concrete formulations.</p></li>
<li><p>Selection of the final circular material solutions for breakwater manufacturing.</p></li>
<li><p>Integration of HPC, LWAC and cellular concrete into the final breakwater design.</p></li>
<li><p>Successful manufacture of a reduced-scale floating breakwater prototype (3.4 m × 1.7 m × 1.05 m).</p></li>
<li><p>Incorporation of recycled and secondary materials supporting the project's circularity objectives.</p></li>
<li><p>Demonstration of manufacturability and constructability of the circular breakwater concept.</p></li>
<li><p>Development and implementation of the prototype mooring and deployment solution.</p></li>
<li><p>Preparation and deployment of the prototype for long-term marine validation.</p></li>
<li><p>Delivery of <strong>D4.3 – Breakwater Prototype</strong>, representing one of the key technical outcomes of the project and enabling the subsequent marine demonstration and monitoring activities performed in WP6.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Achievement of Objective O4:</strong> The objective was fully achieved through the successful design, manufacture and deployment of a floating breakwater prototype based on innovative circular concrete materials. The prototype demonstrated the technical feasibility of the SUREWAVE breakwater concept and provided a crucial validation platform linking material development (WP4) with marine demonstration and monitoring activities (WP6).</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

Exploitation of results (M19-M48)

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 98%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p>The exploitable results (ERs) associated with the WP4 are:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Cellular lightweight concrete for non-structural solutions</p></li>
<li><p>Lightweight aggregate concrete (LWAC) and high-performance concrete (HPC) for structural solutions.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>These ERs will be exploited through steps.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p><strong>Comprehensive Evaluation</strong>: Conduct a thorough analysis of the two ERs, including competitor analysis, Intellectual Property Rights (IPR) assessment, market demand, and technological feasibility. This step will ensure a clear understanding of the potential and unique advantages of each solution.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Preliminary Business Model Development</strong>: Based on the evaluation, develop a preliminary business model for each ER. This model should outline the value proposition, target markets, revenue streams, cost structure, and potential partnerships.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Integration into ACCIONA Projects</strong>: Utilize the identified ERs in ongoing and future construction projects within ACCIONA. This practical application will provide real-world data on performance, cost-effectiveness, and scalability.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Further Research and Development</strong>: Use the ERs as a foundation for further research initiatives to enhance their properties, expand their applications, and adapt them to different project requirements. Collaborate with academic institutions, research organizations, and industry partners to drive innovation.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Consultancy Services</strong>: Offer consultancy services leveraging the expertise gained from developing and implementing these ERs. This can position ACCIONA as a leader in sustainable and innovative construction solutions, providing added value to clients through advanced material technologies.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>By following this strategy, ACCIONA can effectively exploit the potential of Cellular Lightweight Concrete and LWAC/HPC, driving both technological advancement and business growth.</p></th>
</tr>

Deviations

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 98%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><ul>
<li><p><strong>Delay in Deliverable D4.3 (Breakwater Prototype):</strong> Deliverable D4.3 was submitted approximately three months later than originally planned. The delay was mainly due to the need to complete the mechanical characterisation, durability assessment and validation of the circular concrete solutions before finalising the prototype design and manufacturing. In addition, Task 4.4 required the evaluation and selection of the optimal combination of structural shell and inner-core materials prior to prototype construction. During this process, buoyancy and stability analyses identified significant challenges with the originally proposed circular cellular concrete core. The resulting prototype dimensions would have been prohibitively large, making manufacturing impractical within the project scope. To address this, the inner-core design was modified by incorporating a low-density water-resistant material (expanded polystyrene, EPS), reducing the overall weight while maintaining buoyancy and operational safety. A protective HPC layer was added to prevent direct exposure of the EPS to seawater and to avoid potential environmental impacts. The delay did not affect the overall project objectives and enabled the successful manufacture and deployment of the breakwater prototype.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Resource utilisation deviation:</strong> The person-months reported by ACCIONA in WP4 exceeded the originally allocated effort. This was because the design activities initially planned for subcontracting (budgeted at approximately €30,000) were ultimately carried out internally by ACCIONA personnel. The deviation did not affect the project budget or technical objectives and resulted in increased in-house know-how and direct involvement in prototype development.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Circular HPC formulation:</strong> The project originally targeted a cement content of <strong>350 kg/m³</strong> for the circular HPC solution. The final selected formulation required <strong>380 kg/m³</strong>, corresponding to an increase of approximately <strong>8.6%</strong> compared to the target. Despite this deviation, the material achieved the required structural performance and durability while still significantly reducing cement consumption compared with conventional ultra-high-performance concrete solutions.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Circular cellular concrete density:</strong> The project targeted a density of <strong>300 kg/m³</strong> for the cellular concrete used in the breakwater core. The final optimised formulation achieved a density of <strong>365 kg/m³,</strong> maintaining adequate material performance and integrity. Although slightly higher than originally planned, the solution still provided substantial weight reduction compared with conventional concrete and fulfilled the functional requirements of the breakwater design.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Cellular concrete composition:</strong> The original concept envisaged the use of Ground Granulated Blast Furnace Slag (GGBFS) and fly ash in the cellular concrete formulation. The final optimised solution did not include GGBFS. Nevertheless, significant environmental benefits were achieved through the use of recycled and lightweight aggregates (Minolite) and a very low cement content of approximately <strong>25 kg/m³</strong>, compared with around <strong>150 kg/m³</strong> typically required for conventional non-structural concrete. As a result, the intended reduction in environmental impact was still achieved.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Impact on project scope:</strong> None of the above deviations affected the overall scope or objectives of WP4. The implemented modifications improved the feasibility, manufacturability and offshore applicability of the floating breakwater prototype while maintaining the intended technical and sustainability objectives.</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

Corrective actions

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 98%" />

<td><ul>
<li><p>While the prototype is not fully 100% circular for the small-scale size, it was possible to achieve full circularity in the full-scale version. Our initial calculations indicate that a full-scale breakwater made of circular mixes will be stable and floatable. This study has been included in WP3.</p></li>
</ul></td>

#### 1.2.5 Work Package 5 – PREDICTIVE MODELLING AND SIMULATION TOOLS FOR MATERIAL PROPERTIES AND STRUCTURAL INTEGRITY MANAGEMENT

<table style="width:99%;">
