Publishable summary

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 98%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>WP5 aims to develop a set of complementary computational modelling tools to: (1) predict the material properties of the novel materials developed in WP4; (2) optimize the aero-hydrodynamic design of the floating PV solution developed in WP3; (3) ensure the structural integrity of the PV solution and its components. In addition, a Structural Health Management System (SHMS) will be developed as an easy-to-use APP to ensure and monitor the structural integrity of the PV solution throughout its service life.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><ul>
<li><p>SINTEF has developed an empirical model using Machine Learning Model Order Reduction techniques (artificial neural network) to predict the mechanical properties of lightweight aggregate (LWA) concretes based on mixture composition. The model's predictions were validated against experimental mixing and testing results from Task 4.5. Additionally, SINTEF delivered a graphical user interface (GUI) for the Concrete Database (ConcreteDB) and a web application (Surewave-ann) that integrates the neural network predictive models as an advanced predictive tool. Deliverable D5.1 details these predictive tools and the web application, including a use-case demonstration.</p></li>
<li><p>In the aerodynamic studies of FPV systems conducted by SINTEF, results showed that breakwaters effectively reduced aerodynamic forces on PV modules. This research was published in a journal article: <a href="https://www.mdpi.com/1996-1073/17/19/4873"><u>https://www.mdpi.com/1996-1073/17/19/4873</u></a>.</p></li>
<li><p>Marin simulated the behavior of three connected PV modules using a high-fidelity CFD model, while the 2 MW breakwater ring was analyzed with a low-fidelity model.</p></li>
<li><p>CEIT developed and implemented a MATLAB code to calculate Stress Intensity Factors (SIFs) and fatigue crack growth under simplified loading conditions in rectangular and circular sections.</p></li>
<li><p>CEIT also evaluated the structural health of the breakwater system using inputs from other partners' analyses, providing data for the Structural Health Management System for fatigue crack growth simulations.</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

Objectives

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 98%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p>This WP aims to develop a set of complementary computational modelling tools to:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>O1: Predict the material properties of the novel materials developed in WP4;</p></li>
<li><p>O2: Optimize the aero-hydrodynamic design of the floating PV solution developed in WP3;</p></li>
<li><p>O3: Ensure the structural integrity of the PV solution and its components.</p></li>
<li><p>O4: A Structural Health Management System (SHMS) will be developed in the form of easy-to-use APP to ensure and monitor the structural integrity of the PV solution through its life in service.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
</tbody>
</table>

Work planned for this reporting period (M19-M48)

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 98%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Task 5.1. Concrete materials properties modelling</strong></p>
<p><strong>(Leader: SINTEF; Participants: ACC; Duration: M6-M36)</strong></p>
<p>An efficient empirical-based model will be developed to predict the concrete mechanical properties using ANN Model Order Reduction techniques. It will be fed with a parametric study from a combination of experimental data of laboratory mixing and testing from WP4 (composition and mechanical strength) and the in-house numerical tool developed by SINTEF for LWA concrete to generate sufficient training data amount (if experimental data are lacking). The main foreseen parameters are binder composition (addition of GGBFS and fly ash class F to the cement binder), the current LWA types (glass wastes) the current natural aggregate to LWA ratio (in agreement with task 4.1 of WP4) and the presence of reinforcement fibres as substitute for aggregates (Ultra High-Performance Concrete). The prediction capabilities of the tool will be validated by specific tests in task 4.5.</p>
<p><strong>Task 5.2. Coupled aero-hydrodynamic analysis and design optimization (Leader: MARIN; Participants: CEIT, SINTEF; Duration: M6-M18)</strong></p>
<p>Subtasks 5.2.1 and 5.2.2 were completed and the results are published in “D5.1: Report on coupled aero-hydrodynamic analyses and design optimization. Subtask 5.2.3 was delayed due to 1) The numerical model preparations in Task 5.2.2 proved to be more complex and time-consuming than anticipated. Additionally, although in the Grant Agreement initially we proposed a single 10 MW unit, we discovered that this might not be optimal due to maintenance and biofouling concerns. Consequently, all consortium members agreed on designing an additional 5 x 2 MW systems, which are easier to maintain and have a reduced impact on biodiversity.</p>
<p><u>Subtask 5.2.3 Coupled aero-hydrodynamic analysis and design optimization:</u></p>
<p>Using the aero/hydrodynamic analysis in subtask 5.2.1/5.2.2, a design optimisation step can be performed. The CFD simulations can be used to determine the required gap between the breakwater and the solar panel modules in worst-case scenarios. The low-fidelity simulations provide the wave field inside the area protected by the breakwater. The FPV and breakwater configuration will be automatically optimised in terms of inter-connector and mooring lines loads and material costs through genetic algorithm-based optimization techniques.</p>
<p><strong>Task 5.3. Structural integrity assessment (Leader: CEIT; Participants: MARIN, SINTEF, ACC; Duration: M12-M36)</strong></p>
<p>The main objective of this task is to ensure the safe, reliable and durable operation of the critical parts of the structure via the application of fracture, fatigue and structural integrity solutions.</p>
<p><u>Subtask 5.3.1 Identification of the critical parts/components of the structure</u> A set of finite element analyses (FEA) will be performed to ensure the structural integrity of the entire structure and to identify its most critical parts taking as input the geometries, boundary conditions and loading conditions of Task 5.2, as well as the material properties predicted in Task 5.1. Based on these analyses, some critical components (e.g. the breakwater, the mooring, mechanical joints) will be selected for a more strict structural integrity assessment (Subtask 5.3.2).</p>
<p><u>Subtask 5.3.2 Advanced Fracture Mechanics</u> The structural integrity of the critical components selected in Subtask 5.3.1 will be assessed by means of analytical, numerical or/and advanced finite element techniques. For this purpose, the specifications defined in WP2 and the guidelines of sectorial standards (such as BS7910) will be followed. Both strength and fatigue analyses will be performed taking into account corrosion and environmental effects. For this purpose, a sub-modelling methodology would be used. This methodology is based on obtaining a global solution (Subtask 5.3.1) using a coarse finite element mesh.</p>
<p><u>Subtask 5.3.3 Definition of the structural monitoring strategy</u> In this task, the location and monitoring techniques (accelerometers, strain gauges, displacement sensors, corrosion sensors, etc.) to monitor the structural health of the components selected in Subtask 5.3.1 will be defined according to the results obtained in Subtask 5.3.2.</p>
<p><strong>Task 5.4. Structural Health Management System (L: CEIT; Participants: MARIN, SIS; Duration: M6-M32)</strong></p>
<p>This task was planned to start in M6 but subtask 5.4.1 was started in M4. This task aims to develop and implement a Structural Health Management System (SHMS) in the form of easy-to-use APP to evaluate the structural integrity and reliability of the PV solution in service. The SHMS will enable to predict the remaining fatigue life of critical components and to establish inspection, maintenance and replacement periods by combining structural monitoring, corrosion management and fatigue assessments.</p>
<p><u>Subtask 5.4.1 Development of fatigue crack propagation algorithms:</u></p>
<p>Fatigue crack propagation algorithms for simplified 2D and 3D crack fronts will be developed and implemented. After the selection of the crack geometry (elliptic or portion of ellipse), stress intensity factors (SIFs) will be calculated at various points of the crack front using the approximate solutions generated from finite elements (Task 5.3). Using appropriate propagation laws (Paris law or similar), the incremental propagation at each point of the crack front will be evaluated. The resulting new crack front will be adjusted to a geometry for which FIT solutions are available.</p>
<p><u>Subtask 5.4.2 Translation tool for finite elements results</u></p>
<p>Development of a tool that translates the tensional results obtained by means of finite elements (T5.3) to the geometries and crack configurations of the approximate calculations (subtasks 5.4.1 and 5.4.3). This module will allow the incorporation of new finite element calculations (design modifications, re-assessment, etc.) to the SHMS, as well as its use by non-expert users.</p>
<p><u>Subtask 5.4.3 Development and implementation of the SHMS</u></p>
<p>The components developed in subtasks 5.4.1 and 5.4.2 will be integrated into the SHMS in the form of easy-to-use APP to manage structural integrity and reliability of the FPV solution through its life in service. In addition, data generated in targeted test campaigns (WP4 and WP6) and in-service monitored data will be seamlessly stored in the database of the system to feed fatigue propagation analyses and to manage corrosion.</p>
<p><strong>Task 5.5: Comparative validation of the predictive computational tools (Leader: CEIT; Participants: MARIN, SINTEF; Duration: M30-M34):</strong> The main scope of this task is to verify, calibrate and validate the developed modelling and simulation tools using the data produced by targeted validation campaigns (WP4 and WP6), as well as with data collected from literature, standards and available third-party databases.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 98%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p><em>Described Work Carried Out During the Reporting Period Towards Each Listed Objective</em></p>
<p><strong>O5.1: Predict the material properties of the novel materials developed in WP4</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status: Completed</strong></p>
<p>Objective O5.1 was addressed through <strong>Task 5.1: Concrete Materials Properties Modelling</strong> and reported in <strong>Deliverable D5.2: Empirical-Based Concrete Properties Modelling Tool</strong>.</p>
<p><strong>Task 5.1: Concrete materials properties modelling (Leader: SINTEF; Participants: ACC; Duration: M6-M36)</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status:</strong> Completed</p>
<p><strong>SINTEF</strong> led the development of the data-driven concrete material-properties modelling framework, including compilation and processing of the concrete-mixture database, evaluation of machine-learning methods, model training and validation, and implementation of the <strong>Surewave-ANN</strong> web-based prediction platform. <strong>ACC</strong> provided the experimental mix designs and measured material properties generated during the development of the HPC, LWAC and cellular-concrete formulations.</p>
<p>During M19-M48, an empirical machine-learning platform, <strong>Surewave-ANN</strong>, was developed to predict concrete compressive strength from mix-composition parameters. A structured database combining experimental data generated in WP4 with published concrete data was compiled, cleaned and used for model development. The final database contained information from <strong>more than 1,000 concrete mixtures</strong>, including conventional, lightweight and high-performance concretes.</p>
<p>Several machine-learning approaches were evaluated, and an automated model-development framework based on <strong>AutoGluon AutoML</strong> was selected to replace the initially proposed backward ANN algorithm. The final implementation used weighted ensemble modelling, which provided the best predictive performance among the assessed approaches.</p>
<p>A web-based graphical interface was developed to allow users to:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>enter concrete mix-design parameters;</p></li>
<li><p>predict compressive strength;</p></li>
<li><p>add new experimental results to the database;</p></li>
<li><p>manage and update the underlying dataset; and</p></li>
<li><p>compare predictions with experimental values.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The prediction capability was evaluated using independent concrete formulations developed within WP4. The validation showed that the tool could reproduce the measured strength trends and support preliminary screening and optimisation of circular concrete formulations. The platform can therefore reduce the number of experimental formulations required during early material development, although laboratory testing remains necessary for final qualification.</p>
<p><strong>Main measurable outcomes</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Compilation of a database containing <strong>more than 1,000 concrete mixtures</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Integration of experimental results from the <strong>LWAC and HPC formulations developed in WP4</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Evaluation of multiple machine-learning models and selection of a <strong>weighted ensemble model</strong> as the best-performing approach.</p></li>
<li><p>Development of the operational <strong>Surewave-ANN web application</strong> for concrete-property prediction and database management.</p></li>
<li><p>Validation of the prediction workflow using concrete mixtures produced and tested within WP4.</p></li>
<li><p>Completion of <strong>D5.2: Empirical-Based Concrete Properties Modelling Tool</strong>.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Achievement of O5.1:</strong> Objective O5.1 was <strong>fully achieved</strong>. The project delivered and validated an ANN/AutoML-based tool for predicting the compressive strength of circular concrete materials using composition data. The resulting <strong>Surewave-ANN</strong> platform links the experimental material development in WP4 with the predictive modelling activities in WP5 and provides a reusable tool for future concrete-mixture screening and optimisation</p>
<p><strong>O5.2: Optimise the aero-hydrodynamic design of the floating PV solution developed in WP3</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status: Completed</strong></p>
<p>Objective O5.2 was addressed through <strong>Task 5.2: Coupled Aero-Hydrodynamic Analysis and Design Optimisation</strong> and documented principally in <strong>D5.1: Report on Coupled Aero-Hydrodynamic Analyses and Design Optimisation</strong>, with final verification reported in <strong>D5.7: Modelling Tools Validation Report</strong>. Experimental data from <strong>D6.2: Report on Basin Test Results</strong> were also used to validate the hydrodynamic modelling framework.</p>
<p><strong>Task 5.2: Coupled aero-hydrodynamic analysis and design optimization (Leader: MARIN; Participants: CEIT, SINTEF; Duration: M6-M18)</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status:</strong> Completed</p>
<p><strong>MARIN</strong> led the development and validation of the hydrodynamic models used to predict FPV and breakwater motions, connection loads and mooring response. <strong>SINTEF</strong> performed aerodynamic CFD analyses and contributed to integrated system and mooring optimisation, while <strong>CEIT</strong> provided connection characteristics and structural-response inputs.</p>
<p>During M19-M48, the aerodynamic and hydrodynamic models initiated in Subtasks 5.2.1 and 5.2.2 were completed, validated and applied to optimise the offshore FPV concept developed in WP3. The work combined low-fidelity potential-flow and time-domain models with high-fidelity CFD simulations to assess FPV motions, connector loads, mooring loads, wave attenuation and aerodynamic shielding under representative offshore conditions.</p>
<p>The hydrodynamic model was validated against the wave-basin measurements generated in WP6 and subsequently scaled to a conceptual <strong>2 MWp offshore FPV farm comprising 3,660 FPV modules protected by 32 interconnected floating breakwaters</strong>. The large-scale numerical model included <strong>22,152 degrees of freedom</strong> and was executed using high-performance computing. Simulations were conducted with and without breakwater protection over multiple wave frequencies and directions to quantify its influence on FPV motions, connector loads and wave-drift forces.</p>
<p>The analyses showed that the breakwater effectively reduced short-wave energy and the associated FPV connector loads. Vertical connector loads and overturning moments were reduced by up to <strong>50% in steep irregular waves</strong>. However, the analyses also identified standing waves inside the breakwater ring and increased wave-drift loads, demonstrating the need to consider both local hydrodynamic amplification and higher mooring requirements when optimising the system layout.</p>
<p>Aerodynamic CFD simulations were performed for different FPV-array sizes, wind directions and panel angles. Correlations were developed to estimate lift and drag forces as a function of the number of FPV modules. Simulations with and without breakwater protection confirmed that the breakwater also functions as a wind barrier, substantially reducing aerodynamic loads for relevant angles of attack. The results highlighted the importance of optimising the breakwater height, location and separation distance from the FPV array.</p>
<p>For Subtask 5.2.3, the validated models were incorporated into a design-optimisation workflow using surrogate models, time-domain simulations and genetic algorithms. The optimisation considered mooring-line loads, connector loads, system response and material use. For the Mediterranean and North Sea design cases, the optimised configurations achieved approximately <strong>34–56% reductions in mooring-system mass</strong> while satisfying the applicable structural constraints.</p>
<p>The originally proposed single 10 MW configuration was supplemented by a modular multi-unit approach following consortium assessment of maintenance access, biofouling management and environmental considerations. Detailed hydrodynamic analyses were consequently performed for a representative 2 MWp unit, providing a scalable basis for future multi-unit farms. This refinement did not change the project scope or objective, but improved the maintainability, modularity and practical deployment potential of the concept.</p>
<p><strong>Main measurable outcomes</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Completion of coupled aerodynamic and hydrodynamic analyses for representative Baltic Sea, Mediterranean Sea and North Sea conditions.</p></li>
<li><p>Validation of the hydrodynamic numerical framework against WP6 wave-basin measurements.</p></li>
<li><p>Simulation of a <strong>2 MWp FPV farm with 3,660 FPV modules and 32 floating breakwaters</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Development of a high-performance-computing model containing <strong>22,152 degrees of freedom</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Demonstration of reductions of up to <strong>50% in vertical connector loads and overturning moments</strong> under steep irregular wave conditions.</p></li>
<li><p>Quantification of increased wave-drift loads and local standing-wave effects introduced by the breakwater ring.</p></li>
<li><p>Development of aerodynamic lift- and drag-force correlations for large FPV arrays.</p></li>
<li><p>Confirmation that the breakwater provides both wave attenuation and aerodynamic shielding.</p></li>
<li><p>Achievement of approximately <strong>34–56% reduction in mooring-system mass</strong> for selected optimised configurations.</p></li>
<li><p>Completion of <strong>D5.1: Report on Coupled Aero-Hydrodynamic Analyses and Design Optimisation</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Final validation and documentation of the modelling framework in <strong>D5.7: Modelling Tools Validation Report</strong>.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Achievement of O5.2</strong></p>
<p>Objective O5.2 was <strong>fully achieved</strong>. The project delivered validated aerodynamic and hydrodynamic modelling tools and applied them to the optimisation of the integrated FPV-breakwater and mooring configuration. The work quantified the protective benefits and associated design trade-offs of the breakwater, reduced mooring-system material requirements and provided a validated modelling basis for scaling the SUREWAVE concept towards commercial multi-unit offshore FPV farms.</p>
<p><strong>O5.3: Ensure the structural integrity of the PV solution and its components</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status: Completed</strong></p>
<p>Objective O5.3 was addressed through <strong>Task 5.3: Structural Integrity Assessment</strong> and documented mainly in <strong>D5.3: Report on Structural Integrity Assessment</strong> and <strong>D5.4: Report on Monitoring Strategy</strong>. The fatigue assessment was further supported by <strong>D5.5: Fatigue Crack Propagation Algorithms</strong> and validated within <strong>D5.7: Modelling Tools Validation Report</strong>.</p>
<p><strong>Task 5.3. Structural integrity assessment (Leader: CEIT; Participants: MARIN, SINTEF, ACC; Duration: M12-M36)</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status:</strong> Completed</p>
<p><strong>CEIT</strong> led the development of the detailed finite-element framework for assessing the structural integrity and fatigue performance of the floating concrete breakwater. <strong>SINTEF</strong> developed the initial finite-element models of the breakwater components and also provided aerodynamic. <strong>MARIN</strong> provided hydrodynamic load inputs for the structural analyses. <strong>ACC</strong> supplied the experimentally measured concrete properties and structural-design data. During M19-M48, a comprehensive finite-element framework was developed to assess the strength, fatigue resistance and long-term structural performance of the floating breakwater and its critical components. The methodology combined global finite-element modelling, reinforcement modelling and detailed submodelling of highly loaded regions.</p>
<p>The concrete constitutive model was calibrated using the experimentally measured properties of the HPC developed in WP4, including a compressive strength of approximately <strong>128 MPa</strong>, an elastic modulus of approximately <strong>44 GPa</strong> and a tensile strength of approximately <strong>11 MPa</strong>. The calibrated material model was implemented in the three-dimensional ABAQUS model of the floating breakwater.</p>
<p>A total of <strong>33 extreme load simulations</strong> were performed using the environmental and system loads generated in Task 5.2 for the Baltic Sea, Mediterranean Sea and North Sea conditions. These analyses considered connector forces, mooring loads and combined extreme-loading events. The assessment identified the mooring attachment areas, breakwater connection regions and geometric stress concentrations as the most critical locations.</p>
<p>Detailed strength and fatigue assessments were subsequently performed for the critical reinforcement and load-transfer regions. The calculated stresses remained below the applicable resistance limits, and the Goodman and Soderberg fatigue assessments produced safety factors above unity for all analysed reinforcement components. The results therefore supported an infinite-fatigue-life classification under the assessed loading scenarios.</p>
<p>Based on the structural analyses, a monitoring strategy was defined for the identified critical locations. The proposed monitoring framework combined accelerometers, temperature sensors and corrosion sensors with wireless LoRa and UWB communication. A novel eddy-current corrosion-monitoring system was developed to detect changes in reinforcement condition through approximately <strong>5–6 cm of concrete cover</strong>. The monitoring strategy provided the technical foundation for the Structural Health Management System developed in the subsequent WP5 activities.</p>
<p><strong>Main measurable outcomes</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Development of a complete three-dimensional finite-element model of the floating breakwater in ABAQUS.</p></li>
<li><p>Calibration of the HPC material model using WP4 experimental results, including a compressive strength of approximately <strong>128 MPa</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Completion of <strong>33 extreme load simulations</strong> for the Baltic Sea, Mediterranean Sea and North Sea scenarios.</p></li>
<li><p>Identification of the mooring connections, breakwater connectors and geometric stress concentrations as the principal critical regions.</p></li>
<li><p>Verification that reinforcement stresses remained below the relevant structural resistance limits.</p></li>
<li><p>Demonstration of fatigue safety factors above unity for all assessed reinforcement components.</p></li>
<li><p>Definition of monitoring locations and suitable sensing technologies based directly on the structural analysis results.</p></li>
<li><p>Development of an eddy-current corrosion sensor capable of monitoring reinforcement through approximately <strong>5–6 cm of concrete cover</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Completion of <strong>D5.3: Report on Structural Integrity Assessment</strong> and <strong>D5.4: Report on Monitoring Strategy</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Integration of the structural and monitoring results into the fatigue and SHMS developments reported in <strong>D5.5–D5.7</strong>.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Achievement of O5.3</strong></p>
<p>Objective O5.3 was <strong>fully achieved</strong>. The project developed and applied an advanced structural-integrity methodology to identify critical components and verify their strength and fatigue performance under representative operational and extreme offshore loads. The resulting assessment confirmed the adequacy of the breakwater design and provided a risk-based monitoring strategy for the most highly loaded and degradation-sensitive regions.</p>
<p><strong>O5.4 A Structural Health Management System (SHMS) will be developed</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status: Completed</strong></p>
<p>Objective O5.4 was addressed through <strong>Task 5.4: Structural Health Management System</strong> and <strong>Task 5.5: Comparative Validation of the Predictive Computational Tools</strong>. The SHMS integrates structural-integrity assessment, fatigue-crack-propagation algorithms, corrosion monitoring and remaining-life estimation to support condition assessment and inspection planning. This work is documented mainly in <strong>D5.3: Report on Structural Integrity Assessment</strong>, <strong>D5.4: Report on Monitoring Strategy</strong>, <strong>D5.5: Fatigue Crack Propagation Algorithms</strong>, <strong>D5.6: SHMS Demonstrator</strong> and <strong>D5.7: Modelling Tools Validation Report</strong>. The corrosion and vibration-monitoring technologies were further demonstrated under marine conditions in <strong>D6.4: Marine Long-Term Degradation of the Concrete Breakwater.</strong></p>
<p><strong>Task 5.4. Structural Health Management System (L: CEIT; Participants: MARIN, SIS; Duration: M6-M32)</strong></p>
<p>Although Task 5.4 was formally scheduled to start in M6, work on <strong>Subtask 5.4.1: Development of Fatigue Crack Propagation Algorithms</strong> began in M4 to ensure timely integration with the structural analyses and monitoring activities.</p>
<p><strong>CEIT</strong> led the development of the fatigue-crack-propagation methodology, the translation of finite-element results into crack-assessment inputs, and the integration of these components into the SHMS demonstrator. <strong>MARIN</strong> provided hydrodynamic-load histories and system-response data required for fatigue assessment. <strong>SIS</strong> contributed FPV-system and connection information and supported the definition of relevant operational and monitoring requirements.</p>
<p>During M19–M48, new stress-intensity-factor solutions were developed for narrow elliptical cracks in circular reinforcement bars. A total of <strong>598 finite-element simulations</strong> were completed for different crack shapes and relative crack depths. Crack-front propagation was evaluated at <strong>153 points</strong>, allowing non-uniform crack growth under complex stress distributions to be represented. The resulting solutions were implemented in automated crack-propagation algorithms based on fracture-mechanics and Paris-law principles.</p>
<p>A translation tool was developed to convert stress results from the structural finite-element analyses into the geometries, load histories and crack configurations required by the fatigue algorithms. This enables updated structural calculations, design modifications and reassessment results to be incorporated into the SHMS without requiring the user to reproduce the complete finite-element workflow.</p>
<p>The fatigue, structural and monitoring components were integrated into a MATLAB-based SHMS demonstrator. The system provides three principal analysis modes:</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p>propagation of a defined crack with a specified orientation;</p></li>
<li><p>identification of the most critical crack orientation; and</p></li>
<li><p>determination of the critical initial crack size.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>The SHMS combines structural stresses, environmental loading, material properties, corrosion-related reinforcement loss and fatigue-crack propagation to estimate remaining life and support inspection planning. A conservative routine inspection interval of <strong>four years</strong> was implemented, supplemented by condition-triggered inspections when calculated stress-intensity factors reach defined proportions of the critical value.</p>
<p>The monitoring strategy included eddy-current corrosion sensors, accelerometers, temperature measurement and wireless LoRa and UWB communication. The corrosion sensor was developed to assess reinforcement condition through approximately <strong>5–6 cm of concrete cover</strong>. Corrosion and vibration-monitoring technologies were subsequently operated on the breakwater prototype during the marine campaign, providing relevant field data for the monitoring and SHMS framework.</p>
<p><strong>Main Measurable Outcomes</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Development of fatigue-crack-propagation algorithms for elliptical cracks in reinforcing bars.</p></li>
<li><p>Completion of <strong>598 finite-element simulations</strong> for stress-intensity-factor calculation.</p></li>
<li><p>Evaluation of crack-front propagation at <strong>153 points</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Development of a translation tool linking structural finite-element results with the fatigue-assessment modules.</p></li>
<li><p>Integration of structural, fatigue, corrosion and inspection-planning functions into an SHMS demonstrator.</p></li>
<li><p>Implementation of three analysis modes covering defined cracks, critical orientation and critical crack size.</p></li>
<li><p>Establishment of a conservative <strong>four-year routine inspection interval</strong>, supplemented by condition-based inspection triggers.</p></li>
<li><p>Integration of corrosion-related reinforcement loss into the remaining-life assessment.</p></li>
<li><p>Development and marine demonstration of corrosion and vibration-monitoring technologies.</p></li>
<li><p>Completion of <strong>D5.4–D5.7</strong>.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Achievement of O5.4</strong></p>
<p>Objective O5.4 was <strong>fully achieved at demonstrator level</strong>. The project delivered an operational SHMS framework that integrates structural assessment, fatigue-crack propagation, corrosion monitoring, remaining-life prediction and inspection planning. The system was validated using structural calculations, laboratory data, wave-basin results and marine-monitoring evidence.</p>
<p>Longer-term open-sea data are still required to refine the inspection criteria, validate remaining-life predictions and quantify reductions in inspection frequency, maintenance interventions and O&amp;M costs.</p>
<p><strong>Task 5.5: Comparative validation of the predictive computational tools (Leader: CEIT; Participants: MARIN, SINTEF; Duration: M30-M34):</strong></p>
<p>CEIT led the comparative validation of the structural-integrity, fatigue and SHMS tools developed in WP5. MARIN validated the hydrodynamic models against analytical solutions and measurements from the wave-basin campaigns, while SINTEF contributed to the validation of the aerodynamic and component-level finite-element models.</p>
<p>The validation used experimental material data from WP4, connection and fatigue tests from WP6, wave-basin measurements, marine corrosion and vibration data, relevant standards, published literature and third-party datasets. The work confirmed the consistency of the predictive framework and its suitability for design optimisation, structural assessment and inspection planning.</p>
<p>The validation also identified remaining limitations, including simplified treatment of some nonlinear wave effects, absence of full probabilistic uncertainty analysis, incomplete modelling of localised corrosion and the need for longer-term open-sea monitoring data.</p>
<p><strong>Main measurable outcomes</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Comparative validation of the hydrodynamic, aerodynamic, structural-integrity, fatigue and SHMS tools.</p></li>
<li><p>Calibration of structural models using WP4 concrete-material data.</p></li>
<li><p>Validation of hydrodynamic predictions against WP6 wave-basin measurements.</p></li>
<li><p>Verification of fatigue and remaining-life methods using connection-test results and established fracture-mechanics solutions.</p></li>
<li><p>Integration of marine corrosion and vibration-monitoring evidence into the validation framework.</p></li>
<li><p>Documentation of model applicability, assumptions and remaining development needs.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Related deliverable:</strong> <strong>D5.7: Modelling Tools Validation Report</strong>,</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

Significant Results

<colgroup>
<col style="width: 98%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><ul>
<li><p>A structured, multi-layer database containing <strong>1,030 concrete mixtures</strong> was established to support data preprocessing, analysis and machine-learning applications for different concrete classes and uses.</p></li>
<li><p>The <strong>Surewave-ANN</strong> web application was developed to predict concrete compressive strength from mixture parameters, including binder composition, lightweight aggregates, admixtures and additives. The selected weighted-ensemble model achieved a test RMSE of approximately <strong>3.99 MPa</strong>. <strong>Related deliverable:</strong> D5.2.</p></li>
<li><p>Hydrodynamic modelling tools were developed for the modular <strong>2 MW FPV system</strong>, including the FPV modules, connectors, floating breakwater and mooring system. The analyses assessed module size, marine growth, wave-current interaction, system motions and connector loads.</p></li>
<li><p>Time-domain simulations, surrogate models and genetic algorithms were applied to optimise the mooring system. The optimisation achieved calculated mooring-mass reductions of approximately <strong>34–56%</strong> for selected Mediterranean and North Sea configurations while satisfying the applied structural constraints.</p></li>
<li><p>Aerodynamic CFD analyses quantified wind-induced lift and drag on the FPV array and demonstrated the shielding effect of the floating breakwater. The results were disseminated through a peer-reviewed journal publication. <strong>Related deliverable:</strong> D5.1.</p></li>
<li><p>A complete three-dimensional ABAQUS model of the floating concrete breakwater was implemented, including concrete material behaviour, reinforcement, boundary conditions, connector and mooring loads, and detailed submodels of critical regions.</p></li>
<li><p>The Concrete Damaged Plasticity model was calibrated using the experimental HPC results generated in WP4. The structural assessment included <strong>33 extreme-load simulations</strong> for Baltic Sea, Mediterranean Sea and North Sea conditions. <strong>Related deliverable:</strong> D5.3.</p></li>
<li><p>A monitoring strategy was defined for the most highly loaded and degradation-sensitive regions. The strategy combined corrosion sensors, accelerometers, temperature measurements and wireless LoRa and UWB communication. <strong>Related deliverable:</strong> D5.4.</p></li>
<li><p>Fatigue crack-propagation algorithms were developed using <strong>598 finite-element simulations</strong> and new stress-intensity-factor solutions for elliptical cracks in circular reinforcement bars.<br />
<strong>Related deliverable:</strong> D5.5.</p></li>
<li><p>An operational MATLAB-based Structural Health Management System demonstrator was implemented for use by non-expert users. The system integrates structural stresses, fatigue-crack propagation, corrosion-related reinforcement loss, remaining-life assessment and inspection planning.<br />
<strong>Related deliverable:</strong> D5.6.</p></li>
<li><p>The predictive tools were calibrated and comparatively validated using material data from WP4, laboratory and fatigue tests from WP6, wave-basin measurements, marine-monitoring results, standards, literature and third-party datasets. <strong>Related deliverable:</strong> D5.7.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>

Exploitation of results

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 98%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p>The principal exploitable results generated through WP5 are summarised below:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>ConcreteDB and Surewave-ANN applications, SINTEF:</strong> A structured concrete database and web-based machine-learning platform were developed for managing concrete-mixture data and predicting compressive strength from mix-composition parameters. The tools can be exploited for preliminary screening and optimisation of conventional and circular concrete formulations, as well as adapted to other cement-based materials. <strong>Related deliverable:</strong> D5.2.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Large-scale hydrodynamic modelling, MARIN:</strong> New modelling approaches were developed for simulating large arrays containing hundreds or thousands of interconnected floating bodies. These methods can be exploited through engineering and consultancy services for other floating-solar farms and multi-body offshore structures. <strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D5.1 and D5.7.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Mooring-optimisation methodology, MARIN:</strong> A workflow combining time-domain simulations, surrogate models, high-performance computing and genetic algorithms was developed. The methodology achieved calculated mooring-mass reductions of approximately <strong>34–56%</strong> in selected cases and can be transferred to other moored renewable-energy and floating-infrastructure systems.<br />
<strong>Related deliverable:</strong> D5.1.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Automated simulation tool for mechanical testing, CEIT:</strong> A tool was developed to automatically generate, execute and post-process ABAQUS simulations of three-point bending tests. The approach can be extended to other mechanical tests, material models and calibration studies, reducing the effort required for repeated finite-element analyses. <strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D5.3 and D5.7.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Structural-integrity assessment methodology, CEIT:</strong> The global-local finite-element and submodelling methodology can be applied to other complex marine structures where global response, local stress concentrations and variable load histories must be evaluated together. Potential exploitation routes include structural-engineering services, component qualification and fatigue assessment.<br />
<strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D5.3 and D5.7.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Structural Health Management System, CEIT:</strong> The SHMS integrates structural analysis, fatigue-crack propagation, corrosion-related reinforcement loss, remaining-life estimation and inspection planning. It can be further exploited for predictive-maintenance applications in floating breakwaters, ports, offshore energy structures and other reinforced-concrete marine infrastructure. <strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D5.4–D5.7 and D6.4.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>

Deviations

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 98%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><ul>
<li><p>Delay in deliverables:</p></li>
<li><p>Deliverable D5.3 was delayed by a month (from M36 to M37). Reasons for the delay included natural difficulties encountered during the development of the project, and changes in personnel (key researcher retired, another key researcher left the project, a new senior researcher joined the team but it was not possible to get them up to speed to help with the tasks/deliverable). This delay had no influence on the progress of other tasks.</p></li>
<li><p><strong><u>Deviation in Task 5.2:</u></strong></p></li>
<li><p>During our technical discussions, we recognized that designing the complete FPV+breakwater system depends not only on the forces (hydrodynamic and aerodynamic) but also on engineering feasibility. One of our goals was to avoid modification to the current Sunlit Sea FPV design. Consequently, we focused on creating an optimal breakwater layout to minimize wave impact on the FPV. We explored 5 different layouts using criteria such as wave energy distribution, force concentration, anchoring, production, installation, costs, space utilization, and maintenance (see Table below). Our analysis (low/high fidelities), including FLOW-3D simulations, revealed that a circular breakwater layout effectively attenuated critical short wavelength waves affecting the FPV (see Table below). This option has other additional advantages.</p></li>
</ul>

<td style="text-align: center;"><strong>1</strong></td>
<td style="text-align: center;"><strong>4</strong></td>

<td style="text-align: center;"><strong>3</strong></td>

</tbody>
</table>
<ul>
<li><p>In the SUREWAVE grant agreement, we proposed optimization studies for the complete system in WP5. However, significant work was already done in WP3, specifically regarding breakwater design and layout (documented in D3.4: Summary of global system design). Study performed as a part of this report highlighted that a complete design optimization was unnecessary since the number of breakwaters, PV panels, anchoring, connection systems, and the gap between breakwater and PV panels were already optimized in WP2 and WP3 (D3.4 and D3.2).</p></li>
<li><p>In addition, we observed during the basin tests (Task 6.4) that the coupling between the breakwater and the floating PV is one-way, meaning the breakwater affects the floating PV but not vice versa. This allows us to study these two components independently. Therefore, the effect of the floating breakwater in terms of reducing PV panel motions and hinge loads is explored in D5.1 (Section 5.5) only for the final floating breakwater design presented in D3.2.</p></li>
<li><p>In D5.1, section 5 primarily focuses on the floating PV, while section 6 examines the breakwater layout for three offshore conditions (Baltic, Mediterranean, and North Sea). These studies indicate that the connections between the components require special attention. Consequently, we focused on critical components such as panel connections (hinges) and the breakwater-FPV interface —through targeted hydrodynamic and wave tank experiments. The impact of the breakwater on the FPV was thoroughly studied through wave tank experiments. These analyses revealed that while the breakwater attenuates short wavelength waves, the FPV can withstand the forces. However, simulations and experiments identified that the connections between panels (hinges) were weak and needed to be redesigned. We also found that while the breakwater itself was robust, its connection systems required improvement. Consequently, our focus shifted to improving connections between breakwater segments, FPV panels, and the interface between breakwater and FPV. These targeted studies were sufficient to pinpoint critical components likely to fail, avoiding the need for comprehensive system optimization (breakwater + FPV).</p></li>
<li><p>Additionally, we observed that current mooring lines are excessively thick and made of traditional steel, leading to high costs. We decided to allocate the remaining budget for mooring studies, which we believe would be more beneficial. While we could conduct high-fidelity breakwater-FPV simulations as per the Grant Agreement, these would not yield significant new insights beyond producing visually appealing results.</p></li>
<li><p>In summary, during the project it was learned that preparing a high-fidelity model of the full system, including breakwater ring, mooring lines, surface mooring, and hundreds of interconnected FPV panels, would (1) not be essential, because key elements could be studied in a decoupled way, and (2) would require a lot of resources, be very computationally demanding, and it would be very challenging to obtain stable and reliable results. Therefore, rather than assessing all elements together, our approach combined low/high fidelity modelling, wave tank experiments, and engineering feasibility studies to design all elements forming the complete floating breakwater + FPV system, effectively optimizing the system. By doing so, we believe we are not missing significant knowledge necessary for an optimal design of the entire system.</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

<td><p>As part of Subtask 5.2.3, mooring line optimization studies will be carried out. While most design optimizations for the FPV + Breakwater system were completed in WP2 and WP3, a comprehensive technical risk assessment of the SUREWAVE FPV design is planned in Task 7.2, "Assessment of Technological Risks and Barriers to Sustainability." For this assessment, detailed force data acting on the entire system is required.</p>
<p>To support Task 7.2, hydrodynamic simulations of the 2 MW farm have been performed of the full 2MWp FPV farm including floating breakwater protection and representation of connector and surface mooring stiffness. See Task 7.2.</p></td>
