Publishable summary

<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 98%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p>WP6 will perform the technical validation of the global FPV solution developed in WP3, the breakwater of WP4 and the modelling tools developed in WP5, as well as system installability and O&amp;M. It includes:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Precise instrumentation and monitoring during various tests.</p></li>
<li><p>Laboratory testing (ultimate strength, fatigue, and lifetime) for assessing the behavior of critical components, with a focus on joints between different components of the floating PV system.</p></li>
<li><p>Long-term durability assessment of the concrete breakwater prototype under sea-exposed conditions, considering harsh factors such as high wind, high current, high wave loads, corrosion, and biofouling.</p></li>
<li><p>To validate the integrated breakwater and solar modules system through scale model testing in a basin.</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Advanced sensing and monitoring solutions, including a motion sensor for basin tests and a corrosion sensor for concrete components, have been developed, with findings reported and published in a conference paper. Ultimate strength tests on critical connections, between breakwater-to-breakwater, PV-to-PV, and breakwater-to-PV, were conducted using SINTEF’s in-house lab facilities. The study revealed that the current PV-to-PV hinge connections are critical and require redesign for improved performance. Initially, fatigue assessments were planned for all three connection types; however, static analysis (Ultimate strength tests) indicated that the hinge system was the weakest. Consequently, the consortium members agreed to work on the improving of the hinge system. Fatigue testing of the hinge system is currently underway</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

Objectives

| The objective of WP6 is to perform the technical validation of the global FPV solution developed in WP3, the breakwater of WP4 and the modelling tools developed in WP5, as well as system installability and O&M. It includes: |
|----|

Work planned for this reporting period (M19-M48)

<table style="width:96%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 95%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p>O1: Accurate instrumentation and monitoring of the different tests</p>
<p>O2: Lab testing (through advanced hydrodynamic test bench facilities of MARIN and structural laboratory at SINTEF) to assess the behaviour of the full-scale critical components (Mainly the joints between the different components of the floating PV system), testing ultimate strength, fatigue Life-time.</p>
<p>O3: Sea-exposed durability validation of the concrete breakwater prototype for long term degradation, considering harsh conditions: high wind, high current, high wave loads, corrosion, biofouling.</p>
<p>O4: Scale model test validation of the integrated break water and solar modules system in basin.</p>
<p><strong>Task 6.1. Advanced sensing &amp; monitoring solutions for basin tests and concrete component</strong></p>
<p><strong>(L: CEIT; P: SINTEF, SIS, ACC, MARIN; Duration: M15-M36)</strong></p>
<p>T6.1 will define and implement the sensing and monitoring strategy to instrument the FPV basin-scale model (T6.4) and the breakwater prototype (T6.5) taking into account a wireless data acquisition. The sensors will enable to gather data during the testing phase, which will be used to validate the models and structural management system developed in WP5. The location of the sensors and the monitoring techniques to be used will be key for this task. The monitoring system will be capable of detecting failures on critical points of the structure so that the designer can optimize the properties of the material (WP4) and/or the geometry (WP3) of the structure for future improved designs. Parameters that should be monitored include:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>Elevations, motion, accelerations, wave loads for the model test performed at MARIN (for task 6.4)</p></li>
<li><p>Long term Biofouling deposition and corrosion in rebars on the concrete breakwater in harbor (for task 6.5)</p></li>
</ul>
<p>This task was planned to be started in M15, but we started to work earlier to have the corrosion sensor prepared on-time. During this time of work, some preliminary work has been done related to the design of the sensing and monitoring solutions for basing tests and concrete components.</p>
<p><strong>Task 6.2. Critical connections´ ultimate strength performances (SINTEF)</strong></p>
<p><strong>(Leader: SINTEF; Participants: SIS, CEIT, CLEMENT, ACC; Duration: M16-M22):</strong></p>
<p>The aim of the task is to assess the ultimate strength of the critical components of the floating PV system defined in WP3, to check the survivability during extreme scenarios (Storm conditions). It is foreseen that the main critical components that need to be developed during this project are the connections: They are the weak point of the system with highest failure rate probability. The task will thus focus on assessing the ultimate strength of the following components:</p>
<p>▪ Mechanical joint between two breakwater components (to be designed in the project, schematically similar to Figure 1-3 made of steel rope and rubber hinge)</p>
<p>▪ Connections between breakwater and solar modules (to be designed in the project)</p>
<p>▪ Connections between solar modules. (polyurethane geometry to be further analysed)</p>
<p>The connections´ prototypes to be tested will be implemented by CLEMENT and SIS. For each of these connections, main failure modes will be identified (It is foreseen that tension and bending failure will be the two main critical loads, but this will be confirmed by structural analyses during the project). Results will also be compared to the structural analyses done in WP5 as validation for the numerical models. For ultimate strength assessment, the connections will be tested until failure to determine the maximum load. A minimum of 5 samples will be tested per connections to ensure repeatability of the tests. Since two2 of the mechanical connections of this project are tailor-designed (A combination of materials and joint technology), SINTEF will use standard- and inhouse test methods, which allow to adapt the setup to specific requests. SINTEF has access to a wide range of servo-hydraulic machines for testing depending on the load level expected, ranging from a few kN (Dartec) to 1000kN (Schenk-Instron) as well as temperature chambers and a workshop which can machine any type of specific test setup if required. It is also planned to useo using advanced strain measurements technique (DIC) and cameras which will provide detailed information about the localised deformation of the joints.</p>
<p><strong>Task 6.3. Critical components Fatigue performance (SINTEF)</strong></p>
<p><strong>(L: SINTEF; P: SIS, CEIT, CLEMENT; Duration: M20-M28):</strong></p>
<p>Task 6.3 started at M20, and was aiming at investigating the Fatigue life expectancy of the critical connections defined in task 6.2. Fatigue analyses was supposed to be performed on the three mechanical joints. It was anticipated that tension for different amplitudes will be the main failure mode and thus the main test configuration (2 different load amplitudes are planned to be tested to give a rough SN curve for each connection). A minimum of three repetitions should be made per connections assessed and test configurations. However, and after the analyses of the results in task 6.2, which showed that the connections between the modules were the critical part of the system, the work done in this period has mainly focused on redefining the scope of the task together with Sunlit Sea and MARIN. In particular, this task will now focus on testing the second generation of hinges systems since it was found that these were the most critical part of the system, and not perform fatigue tests on the connection between breakwaters and connection systems between breakwater and solar modules. The components geometry to be tested have been discussed with Sunlit sea, and testing of these new hinges can start once they are delivered, which should be in the beginning of M26. Due to the change of scope, the start of the task is delayed, but we are confident that it will not affect the completion of the task, nor the deliverable completion date (M28).</p>
<p><strong>Task 6.4. Basin system validation (Marin) (L: MARIN; P: SIS, SINTEF, CEIT; Duration: M13-M26)</strong></p>
<p>The hydrodynamic performance of the combined breakwater and PV modules in both operational and survival sea states is validated by means of model tests in a wave basin at MARIN. The wave basin is 220 m long, 4 m wide and has a 3.6 m water depth; the flap-type wave maker is able to generate waves up to 0.7 m waveheight. A segment of the breakwater and PV field including interconnectors will be geometrically reproduced at a model scale of approximately 1:20. The scale model will be instrumented to measure motions, accelerations, mechanical joints loads, and high-frequency slamming loads due to breaking wave impacts in operational &amp; survival sea states.</p>
<p>The basin test data will be used to tune and validate the hydrodynamic optimization in WP5 and to demonstrate the TRL 5 of the final PV field design. Two test campaigns are foreseen:</p>
<p>1) An initial concept of the combined breakwater-PV configuration will be tested to measure wave overtopping the breakwater and their impacts onto the PV field as essential data for the numerical model in T5.2. This first campaign is completed during this reporting period. This test campaign is completed.</p>
<p>2) At final stage, the fully developed FPV field including breakwater and interconnectors will be reproduced and validated in operational and survival sea states. The second campaign will start in second reporting period.</p>
<p><strong>Task 6.5. Long-term degradation of the concrete component exposed in harbor (ACCIONA)</strong></p>
<p><strong>(L: ACC; P: CEIT, SINTEF, CLEMENT; Duration: M20-M36)</strong></p>
<p>The work in this task started in M20. The task aims at validating the long-term behaviour of the concrete breakwater solution developed in WP4. Two main mechanisms are responsible for long term degradation of the concrete in marine environment: biofouling deposition and corrosion degradation of the rebars. Using sensing technology developed in task 6.1, long term degradation will be assessed on a small-scale concrete breakwater prototype (1 m wide, 0.5 m height, 2 m length) at the R&amp;D maritime environment demo site of ACC in Port of Gijón (Spain). During this testing, the monitoring systems will provide valuable data for the models developed in WP5 as well as insight on the behaviour of the overall technical solutions developed in WP3 and WP4.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

Progress

<table style="width:96%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 95%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p><em>described the work carried out during the reporting period towards the achievement of each listed objective. Provide clear and measurable details.</em></p>
<p><strong>O6.1: Accurate instrumentation and monitoring of the different tests</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status: Completed</strong></p>
<p>Objective O6.1 was addressed primarily through <strong>Task 6.1: Advanced Sensing and Monitoring Solutions for Basin Tests and Concrete Components</strong>. The developed monitoring solutions were implemented in <strong>Task 6.4: Basin System Validation</strong> and <strong>Task 6.5: Long-Term Degradation of the Concrete Component Exposed in Harbour</strong>. Instrumentation used in the mechanical and fatigue testing under Tasks 6.2 and 6.3 provided additional experimental data for model validation. The results are documented across <strong>D6.1–D6.4</strong>, with the basin and marine-monitoring results reported principally in <strong>D6.2 and D6.4</strong>.</p>
<p><strong>Task 6.1: Advanced sensing and monitoring solutions for basin tests and concrete component</strong></p>
<p><strong>Leader:</strong> CEIT; <strong>Participants:</strong> SINTEF, Sunlit Sea, ACCIONA, MARIN</p>
<p><strong>Status:</strong> Completed</p>
<p><strong>CEIT</strong> led the development and data analysis of the corrosion and vibration-monitoring technologies. <strong>SINTEF</strong> provided structural-analysis inputs for identifying critical sensor locations, while <strong>Sunlit Sea</strong> supplied FPV-module and connection information relevant to basin-test instrumentation. <strong>ACCIONA</strong> supported sensor integration into the concrete prototype and its deployment, recovery and post-exposure inspection. <strong>MARIN</strong> was responsible for wave-basin instrumentation and measurements of waves, motions, connection loads and mooring response. The monitoring solutions were demonstrated through laboratory testing, wave-basin campaigns and approximately nine months of marine exposure at the Port of Gijón.</p>
<p>During M19-M48, sensing and data-acquisition systems were defined and implemented for the laboratory tests, wave-basin campaigns and marine demonstration. The instrumentation was selected to measure the physical responses required for validating the numerical models and Structural Health Management System developed in WP5.</p>
<p>For the wave-basin campaigns in Task 6.4, the 1:10-scale FPV-breakwater model was instrumented to measure wave elevations, FPV and breakwater motions, accelerations, relative motions between FPV modules, mooring response and high-frequency slamming pressures. The measurements enabled assessment of breakwater attenuation performance, FPV response and connector behaviour under operational and survival wave conditions. The data were also used to identify buckling and snap-loading in the original hinge design and to confirm that these behaviours were eliminated in the redesigned connection.</p>
<p>For the connection tests in Tasks 6.2 and 6.3, force, displacement and deformation measurements were recorded during tensile, compression, ultimate-strength and fatigue testing. Digital Image Correlation and camera-based measurements were applied where appropriate to characterise local deformation and validate the associated numerical models.</p>
<p>For the marine demonstration in Task 6.5, the breakwater prototype was equipped with an eddy-current corrosion-monitoring system, wireless accelerometers, temperature monitoring and remote communication technology. The monitoring equipment operated during approximately nine months of exposure at the Port of Gijón. The corrosion sensor monitored reinforcement condition through the concrete cover, while the accelerometer system identified dominant structural-response frequencies in the range of approximately <strong>0.35–0.45 Hz</strong>. Environmental compensation reduced the practical uncertainty of the corrosion-sensor response from approximately <strong>±448 Ω to ±135 Ω</strong>, improving its capability to distinguish structural degradation from temperature and moisture effects.</p>
<p><strong>Main measurable outcomes</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Instrumentation of <strong>two 1:10-scale wave-basin campaigns</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Measurement of waves, motions, accelerations, relative FPV motions, slamming pressures and mooring responses.</p></li>
<li><p>Instrumented tensile, compression, ultimate-strength and fatigue testing of the critical connection systems.</p></li>
<li><p>Deployment of corrosion, acceleration and temperature monitoring on the breakwater prototype.</p></li>
<li><p>Approximately <strong>nine months of monitoring</strong> under real marine conditions.</p></li>
<li><p>Detection of dominant prototype-response frequencies around <strong>0.35–0.45 Hz</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Approximately <strong>70% reduction in corrosion-sensor uncertainty</strong>, from ±448 Ω to ±135 Ω, through environmental compensation.</p></li>
<li><p>Generation of experimental datasets for validation of the hydrodynamic, structural and SHMS tools developed in WP5.</p></li>
<li><p>Documentation of the results in <strong>D6.1, D6.2, D6.3 and D6.4</strong>.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Achievement of O6.1</strong></p>
<p>Objective O6.1 was <strong>fully achieved</strong>. Accurate instrumentation and monitoring systems were successfully implemented across the laboratory, wave-basin and marine-exposure tests. The resulting datasets supported design improvement, quantified the performance of the integrated FPV-breakwater system and enabled validation of the predictive modelling and structural-health-management tools developed in WP5.</p>
<p><strong>O6.2: Laboratory testing to assess the behaviour, ultimate strength and fatigue lifetime of critical components</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status: Completed</strong></p>
<p>Objective O6.2 was addressed through <strong>Task 6.2: Critical Connections’ Ultimate-Strength Performance</strong> and <strong>Task 6.3: Critical Components’ Fatigue Performance</strong>. The redesigned hinge was subsequently assessed at system level under <strong>Task 6.4</strong>, as reported separately under Objective O6.4. The principal results of O6.2 are documented in <strong>D6.1 and D6.3</strong>.</p>
<p><strong>Task 6.2: Critical Connections’ Ultimate Strength Performance</strong></p>
<p><strong>Leader:</strong> SINTEF <strong>Participants:</strong> Sunlit Sea, CEIT, CLEMENT and ACCIONA</p>
<p><strong>Status:</strong> Completed</p>
<p><strong>SINTEF</strong> led the experimental programme, including the development of test arrangements, mechanical testing, digital image correlation and analysis of connection stiffness, ultimate capacity and failure modes. Sunlit Sea supplied the FPV hinges and relevant connection-design information. <strong>CLEMENT</strong> provided the steel-wire breakwater connections and breakwater-to-FPV transition components, together with their design requirements. <strong>CEIT</strong> contributed structural and fatigue-assessment inputs, while <strong>ACCIONA</strong> provided information on the breakwater configuration and connection interfaces.</p>
<p>During M19-M48, a comprehensive experimental campaign was conducted at SINTEF to characterise the mechanical behaviour and ultimate capacity of the most critical connections in the SUREWAVE system. The tests covered the polyurethane hinges between adjacent FPV modules, steel-wire connections between floating-breakwater units, and connections between the breakwater, transition elements and FPV system.</p>
<p>For each connection type, the testing programme evaluated stiffness, force-displacement behaviour, deformation, ultimate strength and failure mode. These results provided essential experimental input for the structural models developed in WP5 and enabled verification of the connection designs against the loads calculated through the aero-hydrodynamic analyses.</p>
<p>The original FPV hinge achieved an average tensile capacity of approximately <strong>3.69 kN</strong>. However, the compression tests revealed insufficient compressive stiffness and premature buckling. This was identified as a critical technical issue because repeated buckling and snap loading could affect the connection’s long-term performance under offshore wave conditions.</p>
<p>The steel-wire breakwater connections achieved an average ultimate capacity of approximately <strong>402 kN</strong>, demonstrating adequate capacity for transferring loads between adjacent breakwater units. The breakwater-to-FPV transition connection achieved an average ultimate strength of approximately <strong>41 kN</strong>, confirming that the proposed transition-element concept had sufficient capacity for the evaluated design loads.</p>
<p>The Task 6.2 testing therefore confirmed the suitability of the breakwater and transition connections while identifying the original FPV hinge as the component requiring further improvement. The complete results and recommendations were reported in <strong>D6.1</strong>.</p>
<p><strong>Task 6.3. Critical components Fatigue performance (L: SINTEF; P: SIS, CEIT, CLEMENT; Duration: M20-M28):</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status:</strong> Completed</p>
<p><strong>SINTEF</strong> led the fatigue-testing programme and assessment of the critical mechanical connections. <strong>Sunlit Sea</strong> provided the FPV hinges and their design and operational-load information. <strong>CEIT</strong> contributed fatigue and crack-propagation methodologies and supported interpretation of the results for structural-health assessment. <strong>CLEMENT</strong> provided the breakwater and transition-connection requirements relevant to the fatigue evaluation. The work assessed the fatigue performance of the redesigned FPV hinge and indicated compatibility with the intended 25-year service-life requirement under the evaluated loading assumptions. The results also supported the connection redesign, numerical modelling and SHMS development.</p>
<p>Based on the findings from Task 6.2 and the first MARIN wave-basin campaign, the FPV hinge was redesigned to increase its tensile strength, improve its compression resistance and prevent buckling. The redesigned connection incorporated modified polyurethane geometry and a stiffening rod to provide additional support under compressive loading.</p>
<p>The updated connection was tested under tensile and compressive loading. The configuration incorporating the stiffening steel rod achieved an average tensile capacity of approximately <strong>8.36 kN</strong>, representing more than twice the tensile capacity of the original hinge. The redesigned connection also achieved an average compression capacity of approximately <strong>20.23 kN</strong> and eliminated the premature buckling observed in the original configuration.</p>
<p>Fatigue performance was evaluated through a combination of experimental testing, measured material behaviour and finite-element analysis. The assessment considered the expected cyclic loads, stress levels and environmental conditions over the intended operational lifetime. Under the assumptions and load conditions evaluated, the maximum service stress remained below the allowable long-term stress, indicating that the redesigned connection could satisfy the required <strong>25-year design lifetime</strong>.</p>
<p>Digital Image Correlation and deformation measurements were also used to compare the experimental response with the numerical predictions. The agreement between the experimental and simulated behaviour increased confidence in the finite-element methodology and its application to future connection optimisation.</p>
<p>These activities demonstrated how the testing framework was used not only to verify the design but also to identify a weakness, implement a corrective design modification and experimentally validate the improved solution. The results were documented in <strong>D6.3</strong>.</p>
<p><strong>Main measurable outcomes</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Completion of full-scale mechanical testing for three types of critical connections.</p></li>
<li><p>Average tensile capacity of approximately <strong>3.69 kN</strong> for the original FPV hinge.</p></li>
<li><p>Average ultimate capacity of approximately <strong>402 kN</strong> for the breakwater steel-wire connection.</p></li>
<li><p>Average ultimate strength of approximately <strong>41 kN</strong> for the breakwater-to-FPV transition connection.</p></li>
<li><p>Identification of premature buckling and snap loading in the original FPV hinge.</p></li>
<li><p>Redesign of the FPV connection using modified geometry and a stiffening rod.</p></li>
<li><p>Increase in average FPV-connection tensile capacity from approximately <strong>3.69 kN to 8.36 kN</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Achievement of an average compression capacity of approximately <strong>20.23 kN</strong> for the redesigned connection.</p></li>
<li><p>Demonstration of fatigue performance compatible with the required <strong>25-year service life</strong> under the assessed loading assumptions.</p></li>
<li><p>Confirmation that the redesigned hinge eliminated the measurable buckling and snap-loading behaviour observed during the first campaign.</p></li>
<li><p>Generation of experimental datasets for validation of the hydrodynamic, structural and fatigue models developed in WP5.</p></li>
<li><p>Completion of <strong>D6.1: Report on Ultimate-Strength Capacity of Each Connection</strong> and <strong>D6.3: Report on Fatigue Performance</strong>, with subsequent system-level validation reported in <strong>D6.2</strong>.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Achievement of O6.2</strong></p>
<p>Objective O6.2 was <strong>fully achieved at the component-testing and fatigue-assessment level</strong>. The programme characterised the stiffness, ultimate capacity, compression response and fatigue performance of the critical connections. It confirmed the capacity and governing failure modes of the breakwater and transition connections, identified buckling in the original FPV hinge, and supported development of a redesigned connection with substantially improved tensile and compressive performance. The redesigned hinge was subsequently validated at system level in Task 6.4.</p>
<p><strong>O6.3: Sea-exposed durability validation of the concrete breakwater prototype under long-term degradation conditions</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status: Completed</strong></p>
<p>Objective O6.3 was addressed primarily through <strong>Task 6.5: Long-Term Degradation of the Concrete Component Exposed in Harbour</strong>, supported by the sensing and monitoring systems developed in <strong>Task 6.1</strong>. The results are documented in <strong>D6.4: Marine Long-Term Degradation of Concrete Breakwater</strong>.</p>
<p><strong>Task 6.5. Long-term degradation of the concrete component exposed in harbor</strong></p>
<p><strong>(L: ACC; P: CEIT, SINTEF, CLEMENT; Duration: M20-M36)</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status:</strong> Completed</p>
<p><strong>ACCIONA</strong> led the marine deployment, monitoring coordination, recovery and post-exposure assessment of the concrete breakwater prototype, including evaluation of concrete condition, chloride ingress, reinforcement and biofouling. <strong>CEIT</strong> provided the corrosion and vibration-monitoring technologies and analysed the sensor data. <strong>SINTEF</strong> contributed structural and marine-degradation assessment inputs, while <strong>CLEMENT</strong> provided the breakwater design information required to evaluate prototype performance against the intended structural function.</p>
<p>During M19-M48, the reduced-scale floating concrete breakwater prototype developed and manufactured in WP4 was deployed at the <strong>Port of Gijón, Spain</strong>, to assess its durability and structural behaviour under real marine exposure. The prototype measured approximately <strong>3.40 m × 1.70 m × 1.05 m</strong> and incorporated the circular concrete solutions developed within the project.</p>
<p>The prototype was installed in September 2025 using a dedicated four-line catenary mooring arrangement connected to gravity anchors and remained exposed until June 2026, providing <strong>nine months of continuous marine exposure</strong>. During this period, it was subjected to naturally occurring wind, waves, currents, tidal variations, seawater exposure, wetting and drying cycles, corrosion conditions and biological colonisation.</p>
<p>The monitoring and inspection programme included visual assessment concrete surfaces, biofouling accumulation, corrosion-related degradation of reinforcement and structural vibration monitoring.. Corrosion monitoring combined continuous measurements with post-exposure inspections, chloride content profiling and reinforcement examination. The objective was to determine whether the concrete materials, reinforcement and prototype configuration experienced significant deterioration under relevant marine conditions.</p>
<p>The corrosion monitoring results indicated <strong>no significant structural deterioration of the concrete prototype</strong> and no measurable reduction in reinforcement thickness. Localised chloride ingress was detected close to openings introduced for monitoring equipment on the upper surface. This was attributed to the local detailing around the instrumentation openings rather than general degradation of the concrete materials. The remaining assessed areas maintained satisfactory integrity after the exposure period.</p>
<p>Biofouling developed extensively across the exposed prototype surfaces. Coverage exceeded approximately <strong>85% on all evaluated surfaces</strong> and reached <strong>100% on one lateral surface</strong>. The measured wet biomass on the assessed lateral surfaces ranged from approximately <strong>6.3 to 9.7 kg/m²</strong>. Mussels, algae and barnacles represented the main biological groups, with their distribution influenced by surface orientation and hydrodynamic exposure. These results provided quantitative information for future assessment of additional mass, hydrodynamic roughness, maintenance requirements and ecological interaction.</p>
<p>The prototype was also equipped with the eddy-current corrosion-monitoring technology developed in Task 6.1. The monitoring campaign showed that the sensor could operate in a real marine environment, while also identifying the need to compensate for temperature, moisture and wetting-cycle effects. Application of environmental compensation reduced the practical variation in the sensor response from approximately <strong>±448 Ω to ±135 Ω</strong>, representing an improvement of around <strong>70%</strong>.</p>
<p>Wireless accelerometers were installed to monitor the dynamic response of the prototype. The monitoring system operated throughout the exposure campaign and identified dominant response frequencies in the range of approximately <strong>0.35–0.45 Hz</strong>, consistent with the expected natural-response range of the prototype. The successful operation of the corrosion and vibration sensors demonstrated the feasibility of remote structural-health monitoring under harbour conditions.</p>
<p><strong>Main measurable outcomes</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Deployment of a <strong>3.40 m × 1.70 m × 1.05 m</strong> floating concrete breakwater prototype at the Port of Gijón.</p></li>
<li><p>Completion of <strong>nine months of continuous marine exposure</strong>, from September 2025 to June 2026.</p></li>
<li><p>Exposure to real wind, waves, currents, seawater, wetting and drying cycles, corrosion conditions and biofouling.</p></li>
<li><p>No significant deterioration of the concrete structure detected after the monitoring period.</p></li>
<li><p>No measurable reduction in reinforcement thickness identified during the post-exposure inspection.</p></li>
<li><p>Identification of localised chloride ingress only near instrumentation openings.</p></li>
<li><p>Biofouling coverage exceeding <strong>85%</strong> on the evaluated surfaces and reaching <strong>100%</strong> on one lateral surface.</p></li>
<li><p>Measurement of approximately <strong>6.3–9.7 kg/m²</strong> of wet biofouling biomass on the evaluated lateral surfaces.</p></li>
<li><p>Successful operation of the eddy-current corrosion sensor under real marine conditions.</p></li>
<li><p>Approximately <strong>70% reduction in corrosion-sensor uncertainty</strong>, from ±448 Ω to ±135 Ω, following environmental compensation.</p></li>
<li><p>Successful long-term operation of the wireless accelerometer system.</p></li>
<li><p>Detection of dominant structural-response frequencies of approximately <strong>0.35–0.45 Hz</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Completion of <strong>D6.4: Marine Long-Term Degradation of Concrete Breakwater</strong>.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Achievement of O6.3</strong></p>
<p>Objective O6.3 was fully achieved. The floating concrete breakwater prototype was successfully deployed and monitored under real marine conditions for approximately nine months. The results confirmed the satisfactory durability of the developed concrete solutions, with no significant structural deterioration or measurable reinforcement loss observed during the exposure period.</p>
<p>The campaign also quantified biofouling development and successfully demonstrated corrosion and vibration monitoring technologies under harbour conditions. Although the exposure site did not continuously reproduce the most extreme design conditions considered for the full-scale North Sea case, it provided a relevant marine environment for validating material durability, monitoring-system performance and degradation-assessment procedures. The results therefore provide a sound basis for longer-term and larger-scale offshore demonstration activities.</p>
<p><strong>O6.4: Scale-model validation of the integrated floating-breakwater and solar-module system in a wave basin</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status: Completed</strong></p>
<p>Objective O6.4 was addressed through <strong>Task 6.4: Basin System Validation</strong>, supported by the instrumentation developed under <strong>Task 6.1</strong> and the connection testing and redesign activities undertaken in <strong>Tasks 6.2 and 6.3</strong>. The results are documented in <strong>D6.2: Report on Basin Test Results</strong>.</p>
<p><strong>Task 6.4: Basin System Validation</strong></p>
<p><strong>Leader: MARIN | Participants: Sunlit Sea, SINTEF and CEIT</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status:</strong> Completed</p>
<p><strong>MARIN</strong> led the planning, execution and analysis of two wave-basin campaigns using a <strong>1:10-scale model</strong> of the integrated floating-breakwater and FPV system. <strong>Sunlit Sea</strong> provided the FPV-module, hinge and array-design inputs required for the physical model. <strong>SINTEF</strong> contributed breakwater-loading and system-performance inputs and supported interpretation of the experimental results. <strong>CEIT</strong> provided connection and monitoring requirements and contributed to the assessment of hinge behaviour.</p>
<p>During M19-M48, two wave-basin test campaigns were conducted at MARIN using a <strong>1:10-scale physical model</strong> of the integrated SUREWAVE system. The tested configuration comprised floating-breakwater modules, an internal array of interconnected FPV modules, transition connections and the associated mooring arrangement.</p>
<p>The campaigns assessed the response of the integrated system under representative operational and survival wave conditions. Tests were performed with and without floating-breakwater protection to quantify its influence on transmitted waves, FPV motions, relative module motions, connector behaviour, mooring response and slamming loads.</p>
<p>The model was instrumented to measure incident and transmitted wave elevations, six-degree-of-freedom motions of the breakwater and FPV modules, accelerations, relative motions between neighbouring FPV modules, mooring response and local impact pressures. The resulting datasets were used to evaluate system performance and validate the numerical hydrodynamic models developed in WP5.</p>
<p>The first campaign tested the original FPV hinge design. The measurements identified compression-induced hinge buckling and subsequent snap-loading events under certain wave conditions. These results were consistent with the component-level findings from Task 6.2 and led directly to the redesign of the hinge under Task 6.3.</p>
<p>The second campaign was performed with the redesigned hinge connection. Under the tested conditions, <strong>no measurable hinge buckling or snap loading was observed</strong>, confirming that the design modifications had successfully addressed the behaviour identified during the first campaign.</p>
<p>The basin tests also confirmed the effectiveness of the floating breakwater in attenuating short-period waves and reducing the motions of the protected FPV modules. In the relevant frequency range, FPV motions were reduced by factors of approximately <strong>2–3</strong> compared with the unprotected configuration. The maximum measured local slamming pressure was approximately <strong>126 kPa</strong>, and the subsequent structural assessment did not identify concerns regarding the integrity of the breakwater design.</p>
<p>The experimental results were used to validate the numerical models applied to larger FPV farms. This enabled the validated models to be used for system-level simulations and optimisation that could not practically be performed through physical testing of the complete full-scale installation.</p>
<p><strong>Main measurable outcomes</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Completion of <strong>two wave-basin test campaigns</strong> at MARIN.</p></li>
<li><p>Testing of an integrated floating-breakwater and FPV system at <strong>1:10 scale</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Evaluation of the system with and without floating-breakwater protection.</p></li>
<li><p>Measurement of wave elevations, breakwater and FPV motions, accelerations, relative module motions, connector response, mooring behaviour and slamming pressures.</p></li>
<li><p>Identification of buckling and snap loading in the original FPV hinge during the first campaign.</p></li>
<li><p>Validation of the redesigned hinge during the second campaign, with <strong>no measurable buckling or snap loading</strong> under the tested conditions.</p></li>
<li><p>Demonstration of reductions in FPV motions by factors of approximately <strong>2–3</strong> within the relevant frequency range.</p></li>
<li><p>Measurement of maximum local slamming pressures of approximately <strong>126 kPa</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Generation of motion-response, wave-attenuation, connector-load, mooring-response and slamming datasets.</p></li>
<li><p>Validation of the hydrodynamic models developed in WP5 using the basin-test results.</p></li>
<li><p>Completion of <strong>D6.2: Report on Basin Test Results</strong>.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Achievement of O6.4</strong></p>
<p>Objective O6.4 was <strong>fully achieved at the scale-model validation level</strong>. Two 1:10-scale campaigns quantified the protective effect of the floating breakwater, identified buckling and snap loading in the original hinge, and confirmed that these behaviours were not measurable for the redesigned connection under the tested conditions. The resulting datasets were used to validate the WP5 hydrodynamic models.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

Exploitation of results

<table style="width:96%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 95%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p>WP6 results were exploited across several dimensions:</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p><strong>Technical:</strong> Testing identified weaknesses in the original FPV hinge and supported a redesigned connection with improved tensile, compression and fatigue performance. The flexible breakwater-to-FPV connection was also successfully validated for future offshore applications.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Economic:</strong> Validated connection capacities and monitoring technologies reduced design uncertainty, over-engineering and the risk of component failure. Condition-based monitoring can also reduce future inspection, maintenance and vessel-operation costs.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Scientific:</strong> Laboratory, wave-basin and marine-exposure campaigns generated datasets for validating the hydrodynamic, structural and fatigue models developed in WP5. The results also supported peer-reviewed publications on protected offshore FPV systems.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Digital:</strong> Eddy-current corrosion sensors, wireless accelerometers and remote-monitoring methods were demonstrated under marine conditions and integrated into the Structural Health Management System.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Environmental and operational:</strong> The marine campaign confirmed satisfactory prototype durability and generated quantitative data on corrosion and biofouling for future material selection, maintenance planning and offshore design.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Overall, WP6 reduced technical and investment risks and provided experimental evidence supporting future component qualification, larger-scale demonstration and commercial deployment of the SUREWAVE concept.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>

Deviations

<table style="width:97%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 97%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><ul>
<li><p>D6.1 was delayed of 2 months (M22 to M36), due to more time than anticipated to design the tailor-made connection between the BW and FPV modules, which delayed the testing campaign and reporting.</p></li>
<li><p>The final wave basin tests (Task 6.4) have been delayed in order to incorporate the latest hinge design. As a result, the scheduled deliverable D6.2 will be delayed by 3 months from M26 to M29.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>D6.3 – Report on Fatigue Performance</strong> was due in <strong>M28</strong> and submitted, approximately two weeks after the planned deadline. The delay was caused by the additional time required to complete the mechanical testing and fatigue assessment of the redesigned FPV connection and to incorporate the final results into the deliverable.</p></li>
<li></li>
<li><p><strong><u>Change in task description (Task 6.3)</u></strong></p></li>
<li><p>Task 6.3 aims at investigating the Fatigue life expectancy of the critical connections defined in task 6.2 to this end, fatigue analyses will be performed on the three mechanical joints." These are the critical connections:</p></li>
<li></li>
<li><p>1. Mechanical joint between two breakwater components</p></li>
<li><p>2. Connections between breakwater and solar modules (to be designed in the project)</p></li>
<li><p>3. Connections between solar modules. (polyurethane geometry to be further analysed)</p></li>
<li></li>
<li><p>SINTEF has conducted experimental studies on the 'Mechanical Joint Between Two Breakwater Components.' Our preliminary findings confirm that this mechanical joint can withstand the design load, eliminating the need for further validation or additional resource allocation. Following the experimental tests conducted at the SINTEF lab, the ultimate strength of the steel wire system was measured at 402 kN, which is below the critical failure threshold. These results confirm that the system meets expectations in terms of mechanical reliability. Additionally, testing of the connections between breakwater and PV modules revealed an ultimate strength of 41 kN, aligning with the requirements outlined in D3.3. This connection system is robust, operating well within the desired load range. If necessary, the strength of the connection can be easily increased by using a stronger rope. Moreover, improvements to the bracket’s geometry, such as smoothing sharp edges to avoid rope damage, could further enhance the system’s ultimate strength. However, our lab experimental and modelling studies showed that the connections between the solar modules (hinges) are relatively weak when subjected to fatigue and snapping loads. To overcome this problem, project partner Sunlit Sea (SIS) has developed a new hinge system, which we intend to test through lab experiments as a part of Task 6.3. The project partners have agreed that the focus should be more on hinge system since other connections are strong and can withstand the loads. Therefore, fatigue tests will be performed on the materials constituting the hinges. As a trade-off the fatigue tests will not be performed on the steel wires and connections between breakwater and modules, since they are not critical. The budget is redirected from one activity to the another.</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

Corrective actions

<table style="width:98%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 97%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p>Following discussion and agreement with the PO, the scope of <strong>Task 6.3: Critical Components’ Fatigue Performance</strong> was refined to focus on fatigue testing and assessment of the FPV hinges, which had been identified as the most critical connection in the system. This allowed the available resources to be concentrated on evaluating the fatigue performance of the original and redesigned hinge configurations under the relevant cyclic-loading conditions.</p>
<p>The revised scope was implemented through experimental testing and numerical assessment and did not alter the overall project objectives. The results supported validation of the redesigned hinge against the intended 25-year service-life requirement under the evaluated loading assumptions.</p></td>
</tr>

Publishable summary

<table style="width:97%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 97%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>This work package contributes a comprehensive sustainability assessment of the floating PV solutions developed within the SUREWAVE project. It covers environmental, economic, and social sustainability aspects as well as technological risks and barriers.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>First, this WP has linked up with the other WPs to support a good integration with the overall project. As planned, the main activities will start later.</p>
<p>Second, most of the qualitative definitions and settings necessary to perform the sustainability assessment has been collected and agreed upon amongst all partners involved. Additionally, some quantitative data could be collected and agreed upon.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

Objectives

| An integrated sustainability assessment of selected scenarios on the developed floating PV solutions will be performed taking into account technological, environmental, societal and economic sustainability aspects. This will include the identification of optimisation potentials as well as conclusions and recommendations on further steps towards a sustainable implementation of the value chains. |
|----|

Work planned for this reporting period (M19-M48)

<table style="width:97%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 97%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Task 7.1. Sustainability framework definition and mass &amp; energy balances analysis (SINTEF) (L: SINTEF; P: IFEU, SIS; Duration: M1-M36)</strong></p>
<p>Main work was to collect all qualitative definitions and settings necessary to perform the sustainability assessment and to agree upon amongst all partners involved.</p>
<p><strong>Task 7.2: Assessment of technological risks and barriers to sustainability (L: SINTEF; P: SIS, IFEU; Duration: M19-M32)</strong></p>
<p>The technological assessment is about evaluating technological indicators of the systems and reference systems defined in task 7.1. Indicators such as maturity, circularity/suitability for a circular economy, durability and risks for accidents will be selected and evaluated to capture technological aspects (mainly risks and barriers but also positive drivers) that can affect sustainability.</p>
<p><strong>Task 7.3: Environmental Assessment (L: IFEU; P: SINTEF, SIS, ACC, CLEMENT; Duration: M13-M32):</strong></p>
<p>A particular focus will be on how to reduce GHG emissions while increasing the amount of renewable electricity by tapping into marine surface area as a resource extending the land surface that can be used for PV, thus contributing to the objectives of the <strong>Mission Healthy oceans.</strong> Screening LCAs will follow the guidelines of ISO 14040/14044 on product LCA and taking into account the ILCD Handbook, EC Recommendation C/2021/9932 on PEF and the DNSH principle. Potential local/regional environmental impacts of the planned marine installations, which are not covered by LCA, will additionally be analysed using elements of an environmental impact assessment (EIA).</p>
<p><strong>Task 7.4: Economic assessment (L: SIS; P: SINTEF, ACC, CLEMENT, IFEU; Duration: M19-M32)</strong></p>
<p>The LCoE (considering yield, expected degradation, up-time, O&amp;M, decommissioning) will be calculated for the global coupled FPV solution resulting from WP3, and will also consider the reduction of the risks impacting on finance and insurance costs as well as on the production costs owing to reduced GHG anergy footprints (based on a current and future CO2 tariff predictions). The system will be simulated in different use-case locations (heavily impacting yield, component and O&amp;M) hence illustrating the economic viability and replicability potential.</p>
<p><strong>Task 7.5: Social assessment (L: IFEU; P: SIS; Duration: M19-M32):</strong></p>
<p>This task assesses social sustainability aspects thus fully integrating SSH into the sustainability assessment framework. It will be based on the UNEP guidelines (2020) for social LCA and methods from social impact assessment (SIA). Employment, income, gender, rural development, inclusiveness, labour rights, health and safety, competition about local use of the marine space</p>
<p><strong>Task 7.6 is not active yet.</strong></p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

Progress

<table style="width:96%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 95%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><p><strong>O7.1: An integrated sustainability assessment of selected scenarios on the developed floating PV solutions will be performed taking into account technological, environmental, societal and economic sustainability aspects.</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status: Completed</strong></p>
<p>Objective O7.1 was addressed through the complete set of WP7 tasks: <strong>Task 7.1 Sustainability Definitions and Settings, Task 7.2 Technological Assessment, Task 7.3 Environmental Assessment, Task 7.4 Economic Assessment, Task 7.5 Social Assessment, and Task 7.6 Integrated Sustainability Assessment</strong>. The results are documented in D7.1 Interim Report on Sustainability Definitions and Settings and D7.2–D7.6.</p>
<p><strong>Task 7.1. Sustainability framework definition and mass &amp; energy balances analysis (L: SINTEF; P: IFEU, SIS; Duration: M1-M36)</strong></p>
<p><strong>SINTEF</strong> led the development of the integrated sustainability framework, coordinated the collection of technical data and established the mass and energy balances for the FPV system, floating breakwater, mooring, installation, operation, maintenance and end-of-life stages. <strong>IFEU</strong> contributed the environmental lifecycle methodology, inventory modelling and definition of indicators required for the environmental assessment. <strong>Sunlit Sea</strong> supplied FPV-specific information, including module composition, manufacturing inputs, system configuration, expected lifetime, energy production and replacement assumptions.</p>
<p>During M19-M48, a common life-cycle sustainability framework was finalised to ensure consistency across the technological, environmental, economic and social assessments. Common functional units, cradle-to-grave system boundaries, reference systems, datasets and assessment assumptions were established. Three representative deployment locations were assessed: the <strong>Baltic Sea, Mediterranean Sea and North Sea</strong>, representing mild, moderate and harsh offshore conditions.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables: D7.1</strong></p>
<p><strong>Task 7.2: Assessment of Technological Risks and Barriers to Sustainability</strong></p>
<p><strong>Leader:</strong> SINTEF | <strong>Participants:</strong> Sunlit Sea and IFEU | <strong>Duration:</strong> M19–M32</p>
<p><strong>Status:</strong> Completed</p>
<p><strong>SINTEF</strong> led the development and application of the technological risk-assessment framework. <strong>Sunlit Sea</strong> provided technical information on the FPV modules, hinges and system configuration, while <strong>IFEU</strong> contributed to the assessment of circularity and sustainability barriers.</p>
<p>The assessment covered <strong>19 technological risk indicators</strong> related to durability, technology maturity and circularity. Severity and probability were evaluated for representative Baltic Sea, Mediterranean Sea and North Sea conditions using technical results from WP3 and WP6, numerical analyses, expert input and relevant literature.</p>
<p>The FPV hinge and full-system hydrodynamic survivability were identified as the principal risks. The generation-v3 hinge increased the estimated axial capacity from <strong>14 kN to 37 kN</strong> and reduced the predicted maximum loads to approximately <strong>34 kN in the Baltic Sea, 33 kN in the Mediterranean Sea and 40 kN in the North Sea</strong>. The redesigned hinge therefore provided a positive calculated capacity margin for the Baltic and Mediterranean cases, while the North Sea demand remained approximately <strong>8% above</strong> the estimated capacity.</p>
<p>The assessment also identified risks related to corrosion, biofouling, UV and saltwater degradation, mooring reliability, electrical safety, installability and maintainability. Circularity barriers included limited recycling pathways for PV laminates, polyurethane and mixed-material components. Recommended measures included further hinge optimisation, full-scale validation, structural-health monitoring, preventive maintenance, design for disassembly and improved end-of-life planning.</p>
<p>The assessment concluded that the integrated offshore FPV concept reached <strong>TRL 5</strong>. Further component qualification, nonlinear coupled-load analysis, long-term open-sea monitoring and a larger offshore demonstrator are required for progression towards TRL 6 and subsequently TRL 7.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverable:</strong> <strong>D7.2: Report on Technological Qualitative Assessment.</strong></p>
<p><strong>Task 7.3: Environmental Assessment</strong></p>
<p><strong>Leader:</strong> IFEU | <strong>Participants:</strong> SINTEF, Sunlit Sea, ACCIONA and CLEMENT | <strong>Duration:</strong> M13–M32<br />
<strong>Status:</strong> Completed</p>
<p><strong>IFEU</strong> led the environmental assessment, including the cradle-to-grave Life Cycle Assessment and the assessment of potential local environmental impacts. <strong>SINTEF</strong> coordinated the technical inputs and mass and energy balances. <strong>Sunlit Sea</strong> supplied FPV-system, energy-yield and component information. <strong>ACCIONA</strong> provided data on the circular concrete materials and prototype manufacture, while <strong>CLEMENT</strong> supplied breakwater, mooring, anchoring and installation data.</p>
<p>The assessment covered raw-material production, manufacturing, transport, installation, operation and maintenance, component replacement, decommissioning and end-of-life treatment. Scenarios were assessed for the Baltic Sea, Mediterranean Sea and North Sea, including alternative system layouts and breakwater-material configurations.</p>
<p>The results indicated that offshore FPV electricity could achieve <strong>more than 90% lower greenhouse-gas emissions than coal-based electricity generation</strong>, depending on the deployment scenario and modelling assumptions. The principal environmental hotspots were PV-module production, vessel operations, anchoring and mooring components, and breakwater construction.</p>
<p>The Mediterranean scenario showed the strongest environmental performance because of its higher energy yield and more favourable installation and operating conditions. The North Sea scenarios showed higher impacts because of harsher environmental requirements, greater material demand and longer electrical connections.</p>
<p>Potential local impacts included seabed disturbance, shading, underwater noise, interaction with marine fauna, navigation and competing uses of marine space. Recommended mitigation measures included careful site selection, avoidance of sensitive habitats and migration routes, reduced vessel use, port-side pre-assembly, shared offshore infrastructure and improved material reuse and recycling.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverable:</strong> <strong>D7.3: Environmental Assessment Report</strong>,</p>
<p><strong>Task 7.4: Economic Assessment</strong></p>
<p><strong>Leader:</strong> Sunlit Sea | <strong>Participants:</strong> SINTEF, ACCIONA, CLEMENT and IFEU | <strong>Duration:</strong> M19–M32<br />
<strong>Status:</strong> Completed</p>
<p>Sunlit Sea led the techno-economic assessment and development of the financial model. SINTEF coordinated the technical inputs and deployment assumptions, ACCIONA provided breakwater material and manufacturing-cost data, CLEMENT supplied mooring, anchoring and installation inputs, and IFEU supported consistency with the lifecycle and integrated sustainability assessments.</p>
<p>The assessment covered <strong>27 cases</strong>, combining three deployment locations, three system configurations and three financial scenarios. CAPEX, OPEX, site-specific energy yield, degradation, component replacement, financing, decommissioning and reuse of the structural infrastructure were included. The main financial indicators were LCOE, NPV, IRR and payback period.</p>
<p>For the representative <strong>10 MW base cases</strong>, the Mediterranean showed the strongest economic performance:</p>
<table style="width:61%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 17%" />
<col style="width: 11%" />
<col style="width: 11%" />
<col style="width: 8%" />
<col style="width: 11%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><strong>Location</strong></th>
<th><strong>LCOE</strong></th>
<th><strong>NPV</strong></th>
<th><strong>IRR</strong></th>
<th><strong>Payback</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>Mediterranean Sea</td>
<td><strong>€62/MWh</strong></td>
<td><strong>€8.9 million</strong></td>
<td><strong>10.7%</strong></td>
<td><strong>7–8 years</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Baltic Sea</td>
<td><strong>€96/MWh</strong></td>
<td><strong>€2.1 million</strong></td>
<td><strong>2.2%</strong></td>
<td><strong>14–16 years</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>North Sea</td>
<td><strong>€139/MWh</strong></td>
<td>Negative</td>
<td>Negative</td>
<td>No payback</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>The Mediterranean was identified as the preferred location for initial demonstration and commercial deployment. The Baltic case showed conditional viability under favourable financing and grid conditions. The North Sea case was not financially viable as a stand-alone installation under the base assumptions and would require cost reduction, shared infrastructure, hybridisation or targeted support.</p>
<p>Sensitivity analysis confirmed that solar resource, discount rate, installation scale and electrical connection strongly influence economic performance. Short or shared connections to offshore substations provide a substantially stronger business case than dedicated long-distance export cables. Smaller 2 MW configurations were generally less attractive because of limited economies of scale.</p>
<p>The final Mediterranean LCOE of <strong>€62/MWh</strong> remained above the original project target of <strong>€54/MWh</strong>, but demonstrated a credible route towards competitive offshore FPV in favourable locations. Further reductions will depend on industrial-scale production, lower financing risk, shared offshore infrastructure and operational learning from a larger demonstrator.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverable:</strong> <strong>D7.4: Economic Assessment Report</strong>,</p>
<p><strong>Task 7.5: Social Assessment</strong></p>
<p><strong>Leader:</strong> IFEU | <strong>Participant:</strong> Sunlit Sea | <strong>Duration:</strong> M19–M32</p>
<p><strong>Status:</strong> Completed</p>
<p><strong>IFEU</strong> led the Social Life Cycle Assessment, including definition of the methodology, modelling of social risks and interpretation of the results. <strong>Sunlit Sea</strong> supplied FPV-system, component, supply-chain and operational information required for the assessment.</p>
<p>The assessment covered the supply chains of PV cells and modules, aluminium, steel, copper and marine fuel, together with installation, operation, maintenance and decommissioning activities. Social risks were evaluated for the Baltic Sea, Mediterranean Sea and North Sea scenarios using the Social Hotspots Database and expressed as medium-risk working-hour equivalents per MWh of electricity generated.</p>
<p>The principal social-risk hotspots were associated with PV-cell manufacturing and the supply of aluminium, steel and copper, particularly where production was concentrated in countries with elevated labour, governance or human-rights risks. The Mediterranean scenario showed the lowest social risk per MWh because of its higher electricity yield and shorter electrical connection, while the North Sea scenario showed higher risks because of greater material demand and longer cable requirements.</p>
<p>The assessment recommended responsible sourcing, supplier traceability, supply-chain diversification, increased use of recycled materials, rigorous offshore-worker safety requirements and strengthened European manufacturing. Offshore FPV may present lower social risks than coal-based electricity when high-risk supply chains and resource-intensive configurations are avoided; however, its greater material demand can result in higher social risks than conventional land-based PV.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverable:</strong> <strong>D7.5: Social Risks of Offshore Floating PV Systems</strong></p>
<p><strong>Task 7.6: Integrated Sustainability Assessment</strong></p>
<p><strong>Leader:</strong> IFEU | <strong>Participants:</strong> SINTEF and Sunlit Sea | <strong>Duration:</strong> M25–M36</p>
<p><strong>Status:</strong> Completed</p>
<p>IFEU led the integrated assessment, consolidating the technological, environmental, economic and social results into a common Life Cycle Sustainability Assessment framework. SINTEF provided the technological-risk results, system-design assumptions and supporting technical data, while Sunlit Sea contributed the economic, energy-yield and commercial-deployment inputs.</p>
<p>The assessment compared scenarios for the Baltic Sea, Mediterranean Sea and North Sea, including alternative system layouts, breakwater materials and electrical-connection options. The Mediterranean scenario showed the strongest overall sustainability performance because of its higher energy yield, more favourable metocean conditions and shorter grid connection. The Baltic Sea showed conditional potential, while stand-alone North Sea deployment required further cost reduction, hybridisation or shared offshore infrastructure.</p>
<p>Across the assessed scenarios, offshore FPV achieved <strong>more than 90% lower greenhouse-gas emissions than coal-based electricity generation</strong>. The Mediterranean 10 MW case also achieved the strongest economic performance, with an estimated <strong>LCOE of €62/MWh</strong> and an <strong>IRR of approximately 10.7%</strong>. Key improvement opportunities included responsible sourcing, reduced vessel activity, shorter or shared electrical connections, modular design, improved recyclability, longer component lifetimes and further qualification of the redesigned FPV hinge.</p>
<p>The assessment identified the Mediterranean as the preferred region for initial demonstration and market deployment. Further progress requires an intermediate-scale open-sea demonstrator, long-term monitoring, component qualification, certification and site-specific environmental assessment.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverable:</strong> <strong>D7.6: Integrated Sustainability Assessment Report</strong></p>
<p><strong>Main Measurable Outcomes</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><p>Assessment of <strong>three representative offshore locations</strong> covering mild, moderate and harsh environmental conditions.</p></li>
<li><p>Identification and evaluation of <strong>19 principal technological risks</strong> related to durability, maturity and circularity.</p></li>
<li><p>Confirmation that the integrated concept reached <strong>TRL 5</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Demonstration of <strong>more than 90% lower lifecycle greenhouse-gas emissions</strong> than coal-based electricity generation, depending on the assessed scenario and assumptions.</p></li>
<li><p>Completion of <strong>27 techno-economic cases</strong> covering three locations, three system configurations and three financial scenarios.</p></li>
<li><p>Estimated Mediterranean LCOE of approximately <strong>€62/MWh</strong>, with an NPV of approximately <strong>€8.9 million</strong> and an IRR of approximately <strong>10.7%</strong> for the representative 10 MW base case.</p></li>
<li><p>Identification of PV-cell manufacturing and aluminium, steel and copper supply chains as the principal social-risk hotspots.</p></li>
<li><p>Identification of the Mediterranean scenario as the preferred option for initial offshore demonstration and market deployment.</p></li>
<li><p>Completion of the integrated technological, environmental, economic and social sustainability assessment in <strong>D7.6: Integrated Sustainability Assessment Report</strong>.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Achievement of O7.1</strong></p>
<p>Objective O7.1 was <strong>fully achieved at the integrated sustainability-assessment level</strong>. The project combined technological, environmental, economic and social results into a consistent lifecycle-based assessment of the selected offshore FPV scenarios.</p>
<p>The assessment identified the Mediterranean scenario as the strongest option for initial demonstration and deployment because of its higher energy yield, favourable economic performance and lower environmental and social impacts per unit of electricity generated. It also produced recommendations covering responsible sourcing, site selection, design optimisation, infrastructure sharing, reduced vessel activity, improved recyclability and further component qualification.</p>
<p>The results demonstrate that offshore FPV can become a sustainable complement to established renewable-energy technologies. Progression towards commercial deployment will require larger-scale open-sea validation, certification, responsible supply-chain development, reduced installation and maintenance requirements, and access to shared electrical and operational infrastructure.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables:</strong> D7.1–D7.6.</p>
<p><strong>O7.2: Identification of Optimisation Potential and Recommendations for Sustainable Value-Chain Implementation</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status: Fully achieved</strong></p>
<p>Objective O7.2 was addressed primarily through <strong>Task 7.6: Integrated Sustainability Assessment</strong>, drawing on the technological, environmental, economic and social assessments completed in <strong>Tasks 7.2–7.5</strong>.</p>
<p>The integrated assessment identified optimisation opportunities across the complete offshore FPV value chain. The principal recommendations were to:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>prioritise high-irradiance locations with mild-to-moderate metocean conditions, with the <strong>Mediterranean Sea</strong> identified as the preferred region for initial demonstration;</p></li>
<li><p>use modular system configurations to improve manufacturing, installation, access, maintenance and component replacement;</p></li>
<li><p>reduce vessel activity through port-side assembly, efficient installation planning and condition-based maintenance;</p></li>
<li><p>share cables, substations, vessels and maintenance infrastructure with offshore wind or other marine-energy installations;</p></li>
<li><p>continue structural and mooring optimisation to reduce material consumption and avoid unnecessary over-engineering;</p></li>
<li><p>further qualify the redesigned FPV hinge, particularly for harsh North Sea conditions;</p></li>
<li><p>increase recycled content, component durability, design for disassembly and recovery of materials at end of life;</p></li>
<li><p>strengthen supplier traceability and responsible sourcing for PV cells, aluminium, steel and copper; and</p></li>
<li><p>validate the integrated concept through an intermediate-scale open-sea demonstrator, followed by independent qualification and certification.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The assessment concluded that sustainable implementation depends strongly on site selection, short or shared grid connections, responsible supply chains, long component lifetimes and reduced installation and maintenance requirements. It also recommended a staged deployment pathway beginning with a Mediterranean demonstrator, followed by component qualification, certification and subsequent replication in the Baltic Sea and selected shared-infrastructure or hybrid North Sea applications.</p>
<p><strong>Related deliverables:</strong> <strong>D7.6: Integrated Sustainability Assessment Report</strong>.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>

Significant Results

<table style="width:96%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 95%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th style="text-align: left;"><p>WP7 delivered an integrated sustainability assessment covering technological, environmental, economic and social performance. The principal results were:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>A harmonised lifecycle sustainability framework was developed and applied to <strong>three representative offshore locations</strong> covering mild, moderate and harsh environmental conditions.</p></li>
<li><p>The technological assessment evaluated <strong>19 principal risk indicators</strong> and supported classification of the integrated offshore FPV concept at <strong>TRL 5</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>The environmental assessment indicated the potential for <strong>more than 90% lower lifecycle greenhouse-gas emissions than coal-based electricity generation</strong>, depending on the deployment scenario and modelling assumptions.</p></li>
<li><p>A total of <strong>27 techno-economic cases</strong> were evaluated across three locations, three system configurations and three financial scenarios.</p></li>
<li><p>The representative <strong>10 MW Mediterranean case</strong> achieved the strongest economic performance, with an estimated <strong>LCOE of €62/MWh</strong>, <strong>NPV of €8.9 million</strong>, <strong>IRR of 10.7%</strong> and a <strong>7–8-year payback period</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>The social assessment identified PV-cell manufacturing and the aluminium, steel and copper supply chains as the principal social-risk hotspots.</p></li>
<li><p>The Mediterranean scenario was identified as the preferred option for initial offshore demonstration and market deployment because of its favourable combined technological, environmental, economic and social performance.</p></li>
<li><p>Key optimisation opportunities were identified, including modular deployment, responsible sourcing, shorter or shared grid connections, reduced vessel operations, infrastructure sharing, improved recyclability, longer component lifetimes and further qualification of the redesigned FPV connections.</p></li>
<li><p>The technological, environmental, economic and social results were consolidated in <strong>D7.6: Integrated Sustainability Assessment Report</strong>, supported by <strong>D7.1–D7.5</strong>.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
