**Publishable summary**

<table style="width:96%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 95%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p>The core focus of Work Package 8 (WP8) is to create effective strategies for the dissemination, communication, and engagement of stakeholders. Led by Sunlit Sea, our aim is to ensure that the benefits and results of our project are widely communicated and utilized for maximum societal impact.</p>
<p>We are developing a master plan for dissemination, exploitation, and communication. This includes training for effective communication on social media, workshops for identifying key exploitable results, and monitoring the effectiveness of our strategies using digital tools.</p>
<p>To ensure broad and effective dissemination, we plan on producing peer-reviewed research papers, technical brochures, infographics, factsheets, journalistic articles, and interviews with scientists, engineers, and other stakeholders. This also involves developing promotional content and establishing a strong online presence, including a community on LinkedIn.</p>
<p>Finally, we are working on exploitation pathways and a business plan to create the foundation for agreements between technology developers and exploiters. This includes conducting analyses of key exploitable results and intellectual property rights, market and value innovation, testing value propositions, and developing business models and plans.</p>
<p>In summary, WP8 is a comprehensive plan for ensuring the project results are communicated effectively, reach the right stakeholders, and continue to have an impact long after the project has ended.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>Since the start of the project, we have established a homepage on surewave.eu and a presence on LinkedIn and Twitter. We have also participated in multiple events to present our project; namely, Intersolar 2023 in Munich and Seagriculture 2023 in Trondheim. Furthermore, we have established vital cross-collaboration relations with the project Natursea-PV, funded under the same call, by participating in a stakeholder workshop. Future collaboration to share knowledge is planned.</p>
<p>Content has been produced for social media and the web at two-week intervals. Additionally, the first newsletter has been published, and subsequent newsletters will be created every six months to summarize recent progress.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

**Objectives**

<table style="width:96%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 95%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p>This work package is designed to accomplish the following key objectives:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>O1: To raise awareness and inform about the project, its results, and potential benefits to industry and society.</p>
<p>O2: To disseminate project results in appropriate and impactful channels.</p>
<p>O3: To identify and engage with critical stakeholders from industry, research, certification bodies, media, etc.</p>
<p>O4: To develop exploitation strategies to ensure that the project will have a long-lasting impact.</p>
</blockquote></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

**Work planned for this reporting period (M19-M48)**

<table style="width:96%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 95%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><p>Task 8.1: Societal Engagement (L: IFEU; Participants: All; Duration: M13-M36)</p>
<p>Key stakeholders from diverse societal groups will be identified such as local marine industries, citizens, coastal associations, etc. with the aim of analysing and defining social needs/requirements by-design (feeding WP2) to ensure that the FPV design will be compatible with other sea activities (i.e. fishing, fish-farms, maritime navigation, tourism-leisure or other), also considering the spatial planning aspects. A SWOT workshop with SAB &amp; project partners will be performed to ensure social aspects consideration in the solution and to support SUREWAVE social awareness &amp; acceptance.</p>
<p><strong>Task 8.2: Dissemination, Exploitation and Communication Strategy, and Monitoring of its Implementation</strong></p>
<p><strong>(L: SIS; Participants: All; Duration: M1-M36)</strong></p>
<p>This task defines the Dissemination, Exploitation and Communication Master Plan (D8.1) based in communication measures and targeted audiences are described in section 2.2.</p>
<p>Subtask 8.2.1 Internal Communication Training: within the first 8 months, a course on social media will provide an insight to the consortium on how to communicate best on relevant issues.</p>
<p>Subtask 8.2.2 Internal Exploitation Workshops: As part of the exploitation strategy, two internal exploitation workshops will be conducted by all partners to accurately define the definitive Key Exploitable Results (KER).</p>
<p>Subtask 8.2.3. Monitoring: MATOMO (a GDPR version of Google Analytics) will be used for the website, FALCON platform for social media channels. The results will feed into the second version of DEC Master Plan D8.1 in M18.</p>
<p><strong>Task 8.3: Dissemination Activities</strong></p>
<p><strong>(L: SIS; P: All; Duration: M1-M36)</strong></p>
<p>Subtask 8.3.1 Scientific Publications, Scientific Conferences, Fairs and other events. The consortium will produce 10+ articles of peer-reviewed research papers, submitted to open-access scientific journals and technical papers.</p>
<p>Subtask 8.3.2 Technical Brochure. SINTEF &amp; SIS will write a 20+ page technical brochure (D8.3) for industry stakeholders summarising the results, which will include graphics and photographs and be edited by SIS.</p>
<p><strong>Task 8.4: Exploitation pathways and Business Plan</strong></p>
<p><strong>(L: SIS; P: ALL; Duration: M19-M48):</strong></p>
<p>The objective is to work on the basis for the exploitation agreements between technology developers and exploiters, according to the exploitation strategy and business plan defined in section 2.2. The task will set the ground for decisions on the business models for technology transfer and the rationale for establishing a fair price. SIS with support and participation of all the partners will carry out the following steps are considered: 1) Key Exploitable Results (KER) analysis and IPR analysis; 2) Value innovation analysis and market analysis; 3): Value proposition and value proposition testing; 4) Development of business models, business plans and exploitation action plans</p>
<p><strong>Task 8.5: Communication Activities</strong></p>
<p><strong>(L: SIS; Participants: All; Duration: M1-M36)</strong></p>
<p>A variety of promotional and visual content will be developed to share on specialist media publications. The <strong>website</strong> will become a meeting place for all stakeholders and will establish links with social media channels and other relevant projects and initiatives. A community on Twitter and LinkedIn will boost the visibility and impact of the results. A logo and promotional communication material (<strong>1 flyer</strong>, rollups) will be developed. <strong>6+ infographics, 3 factsheets, 6+ journalistic articles</strong> and <strong>9+ interviews</strong> with scientists, engineers and other stakeholders will be developed to approach the project from different angles. <strong>2 videos</strong> (intro and final) will be produced to summarise the results.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

**Progress**

<table style="width:96%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 95%" />
</colgroup>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><p><strong>Objective 1 (O1): Raise Awareness and Inform about the Project</strong></p>
<p>SUREWAVE consortium members actively participated in multiple conferences and workshops. Our primary goal was to raise awareness and disseminate information about the project and its results. A detailed description of these activities is provided in Task 8.2. Furthermore, we have established vital cross-collaboration relations with the project Natursea-PV, funded under the same call, by participating in a stakeholder workshop. Future collaboration to share knowledge is planned.</p>
<p><strong>Objective 2 (O2): Disseminate Project Results Effectively</strong></p>
<p>We developed two versions of the Dissemination, Exploitation, and Communication Master Plan (DECMP). These plans identify suitable channels for impactful dissemination of our project results. The DECMP covers the strategies based on dissemination measures.</p>
<p><strong>Objective 3 (O3): Engage Critical Stakeholders</strong></p>
<p>SAB is incorporated and we are in the process of organizing a workshop with them.</p>
<p><strong>Objective 4 (O4): Long-Lasting Impact through Exploitation Strategies</strong></p>
<p>Key exploitable results and exploitation strategies have been identified.</p>
<p><strong>Task 8.1: Societal Engagement (L: IFEU; Participants: All; Duration: M13-M36)</strong></p>
<p>Continued engagement with key societal groups, including a public workshop webinar in November 2036 with industry and research stakeholders. Stakeholder engagement was also strengthened through consistent updates on the Surewave website and social media platforms.</p>
<p><strong>Task 8.2: Dissemination, Exploitation and Communication Strategy, and Monitoring of its Implementation (L: SIS; Participants: All; Duration: M1-M36):</strong></p>
<p>Monitoring activities on dissemination channels indicate over 500 LinkedIn likes, showing significant social media engagement growth. Website traffic and user engagement data were tracked with MATOMO, and regular updates were published on surewave.eu.</p>
<p><strong>Task 8.3: Task 8.3: Dissemination Activities (L: SIS; P: All; Duration: M1-M36):</strong></p>
<p>Expanded dissemination through four scientific publications, increasing the academic reach of project findings. Sunlit Sea hosted a workshop in Oslo, engaging stakeholders in discussions on technological advancements and environmental impact. Surewave continued participation in the Stakeholder Advisory Board (SAB) meetings, with minutes shared among project partners.</p>
<p><strong>Task 8.5</strong>: Communication activities (L: SIS; Participants: All; Duration: M1-M36):</p>
<p>Maintained bi-weekly updates on LinkedIn and other platforms, boosting project awareness. The public workshop webinar in November 2036 further expanded Surewave’s network by fostering discussions on regulatory, financial, and environmental topics. These activities contribute to building a robust foundation for future communication and public outreach.</p></td>

### 1.3 Impact

Please describe the progress of the project so far towards delivering scientific impact, based on its objectives and towards delivering impact in any of the following fields (if applicable): scientific, economic, societal or industrial production or processes. Report on changes to the expected impacts presented in your DoA (if any) and the effects on the project/need for adaptations.

Where necessary, provide further details of your monitoring and evaluation strategy, including: references to baselines, benchmarks, assumptions used (with justification) as well as calculations performed to quantify the impacts. If necessary, provide this information in a separate deliverable / a dedicated section of a deliverable.

***<u>Topic Outcome 1</u>**: Improved overall lifetime, reliability, installability, operability and maintainability of marine substructures, mechanical joints and energy connection systems for ocean energy devices and/or offshore floating PV to reduce degradation and failure rates and thus investment risk.*

<table>
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 100%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>DoA:</strong> <u>Lifetime</u>: SUREWAVE will improve the <strong>overall lifetime</strong> not only considering the offshore extreme conditions but also in comparison with land-locked waters FPV which is below 20 years<a href="#fn1" class="footnote-ref" id="fnref1" role="doc-noteref"><sup>1</sup></a>. SUREWAVE will put <strong>the offshore FPV</strong> lifetime over <strong>20 years achieving the rates of ground mounted solar</strong><a href="#fn2" class="footnote-ref" id="fnref2" role="doc-noteref"><sup>2</sup></a>, through three main ways:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>1) <strong>Robust mechanical connections</strong> will be specifically designed in the project and validated by use of fatigue testing to ensure reasonable lifetime of these critical parts up to <strong>20 years</strong> with low maintenance, <strong>and flexible</strong> enough to <strong>avoid critical failure</strong> of the components.</p></li>
<li><p>2) <strong>The FPV design mainly using corrosion/aging resistant materials</strong> (Al/concrete/Polymers) with low degradation in offshore conditions (expected durability of the components is <strong>around 20 years</strong>).</p></li>
<li><p>3) <strong>Improved numerical models</strong> with reduced uncertainties provides optimal estimate of load and stress distribution in the component leading to optimized component design with <strong>reduced failure rate (by 10%</strong>) and i<strong>mproved remaining useful fatigue lifetime by 25%</strong> of the different elements of the FPV.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p><strong><u>SUREWAVE justification:</u></strong> The <strong>DoA</strong> anticipated that SUREWAVE would enable an offshore FPV lifetime exceeding <strong>20 years</strong>, compared with the reported lifetime of less than 20 years for some FPV installations in sheltered inland waters. The target was to approach the lifetime of ground-mounted photovoltaic systems by improving the durability of mechanical connections, selecting corrosion- and ageing-resistant materials, and applying validated numerical and structural-health-management tools.</p>
<p>A <strong>25-year design life</strong> was adopted as the project benchmark for the integrated FPV system. The design basis included operational, ultimate, fatigue, accidental and serviceability limit states, together with corrosion, marine growth, UV exposure, seawater ingress and repeated wetting and drying. The anchoring system was designed with corrosion allowances for a service life of at least <strong>40 years</strong>, allowing the breakwater and anchoring infrastructure potentially to support more than one generation of PV modules.</p>
<p><strong>Evidence:</strong> D2.1, D2.2, D2.3 and D3.3.</p>
<p><strong>Progress and impact achieved</strong></p>
<p><strong>Mechanical connections</strong></p>
<p>The original FPV hinge achieved an average tensile capacity of approximately <strong>3.69 kN</strong>, but testing revealed insufficient compressive stiffness and premature buckling. Based on these findings, the connection was redesigned and retested.</p>
<p>The redesigned hinge achieved:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>an average tensile capacity of approximately <strong>8.36 kN</strong>;</p></li>
<li><p>an average compression capacity of approximately <strong>20.23 kN</strong>;</p></li>
<li><p>elimination of the measurable buckling and snap loading observed in the original design; and</p></li>
<li><p>fatigue performance compatible with the project’s <strong>25-year service-life requirement</strong> under the assessed operational loading conditions.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The increase in average tensile capacity was calculated as:</p>
<p><span class="math display">$$\frac{8.36 - 3.69}{3.69} \times 100 \approx 127\%
$$</span></p>
<p>The redesigned connection therefore achieved approximately <strong>127% higher tensile capacity</strong> than the original hinge. It was subsequently validated during the second wave-basin campaign, where no measurable buckling or snap loading was observed under the tested conditions.</p>
<p><strong>Evidence:</strong> D6.1, D6.2 and D6.3.</p>
<p>The breakwater wire connection and the breakwater-to-FPV transition connection were also experimentally validated, reaching average ultimate capacities of approximately <strong>402 kN</strong> and <strong>41 kN</strong>, respectively.</p>
<p><strong>Evidence:</strong> D3.3 and D6.1.</p>
<p><strong>Corrosion- and ageing-resistant materials</strong></p>
<p>The internal FPV design incorporated marine-grade aluminium, polyurethane hinges, corrosion-resistant components and protected electrical connections. The floating breakwater used specially developed HPC, LWAC and cellular-concrete formulations.</p>
<p>The selected materials achieved:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>approximately <strong>128 MPa</strong> compressive strength for HPC;</p></li>
<li><p>approximately <strong>49 MPa</strong> compressive strength for LWAC;</p></li>
<li><p>no measurable water penetration in the selected HPC; and</p></li>
<li><p>chloride-migration coefficients of approximately <strong>0.4–0.9 × 10⁻¹² m²/s</strong>, corresponding to extremely high resistance to chloride penetration.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Evidence:</strong> D4.1 and D4.2.</p>
<p>The breakwater prototype was exposed at the Port of Gijón for approximately <strong>nine months</strong>. Post-exposure inspections identified no significant structural deterioration and no measurable reduction in reinforcement thickness. Localised chloride ingress occurred near openings introduced for monitoring equipment, but this was not representative of general degradation of the concrete structure.</p>
<p><strong>Evidence:</strong> D4.3 and D6.4.</p>
<p><strong>Predictive modelling and remaining-life management</strong></p>
<p>A detailed structural model of the breakwater was developed and assessed through <strong>33 extreme-event simulations</strong> covering Baltic Sea, Mediterranean Sea and North Sea conditions. The maximum calculated reinforcement stress remained at approximately <strong>7% of the 500 MPa yield strength</strong>. The lowest reported Soderberg fatigue-safety coefficient was approximately <strong>15.84</strong>, supporting an infinite-fatigue-life classification for the assessed reinforcement under the ideal, non-corroded conditions modelled.</p>
<p><strong>Evidence:</strong> D5.3 and D5.7.</p>
<p>The fatigue crack-propagation methodology was supported by <strong>598 finite-element simulations</strong>, with crack-front growth evaluated at <strong>153 points</strong>. These calculations were incorporated into the Structural Health Management System to support remaining-life assessment, critical-crack evaluation and inspection planning.</p>
<p><strong>Evidence:</strong> D5.5, D5.6 and D5.7.</p>
<p>The SHMS incorporated a conservative routine inspection interval of <strong>four years</strong>, supplemented by condition-triggered exceptional inspections. The system can incorporate corrosion-sensor data and calculate the effect of reinforcement-section loss on fatigue performance.</p>
<p><strong>Evidence:</strong> D5.6 and D5.7.</p>
<p><strong>Scientific and industrial impact</strong></p>
<p>Scientifically, SUREWAVE delivered validated methodologies for:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>coupled environmental-load prediction;</p></li>
<li><p>structural analysis of reinforced-concrete floating breakwaters;</p></li>
<li><p>crack-growth assessment in reinforcement;</p></li>
<li><p>corrosion-informed fatigue analysis; and</p></li>
<li><p>condition-based inspection planning.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Industrially, the results reduced uncertainty concerning the design life of critical connections and concrete structures. The project identified a potentially critical hinge failure mechanism before commercial deployment, redesigned the component and validated the improved solution. This reduces the risk of premature component failure and provides a stronger basis for future qualification and certification.</p>
<p>The SHMS, corrosion sensor and remaining-life models also provide a pathway from fixed-interval maintenance towards condition-based maintenance, with potential reductions in offshore inspection, vessel use, replacement and downtime.</p>
<p>The objective of supporting an offshore FPV service life exceeding <strong>20 years</strong> was <strong>fully achieved at the TRL 5 design, component-validation, farm-scale simulation and relevant-environment demonstration level</strong>. The project adopted a <strong>25-year operating-life requirement</strong> for the FPV system, demonstrated fatigue compatibility of the redesigned hinge under the evaluated loading assumptions, and designed the anchoring infrastructure for a service life of at least <strong>40 years</strong>.</p>
<p>The system-level assessment was strengthened through validated hydrodynamic simulation of a complete conceptual <strong>2 MWp offshore FPV farm</strong>, comprising <strong>3,660 interconnected FPV modules protected by 32 floating breakwaters</strong>. The model included the FPV connections, surface-mooring arrangement and breakwater connections, with approximately <strong>22,152 degrees of freedom and 212,700 boundary elements</strong>. Following validation against wave-basin measurements, the model was used to assess module and breakwater motions, wave attenuation and connection loads at farm scale. <a href="#fn3" class="footnote-ref" id="fnref3" role="doc-noteref"><sup>3</sup></a></p>
<p>The farm-scale simulations showed average reductions in axial connector loads and reductions of up to approximately <strong>50% in vertical connector loads and overturning moments</strong> under selected steep irregular-wave conditions. The simulations also identified local load amplification caused by standing waves and horizontal dynamic response, providing design recommendations for reducing fatigue and survival loads.</p>
<p>The DoA further anticipated a <strong>10% reduction in failure rate</strong> and a <strong>25% improvement in remaining useful fatigue life</strong>. These percentages could not be measured statistically because the project did not operate comparable baseline and improved offshore FPV installations over their complete service lives. Nevertheless, the project generated substantial TRL 5 evidence of improved reliability and lifetime:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>The redesigned hinge increased average tensile capacity from <strong>3.69 kN to 8.36 kN</strong>, corresponding to an improvement of approximately <strong>127%</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Average compression capacity reached approximately <strong>20.23 kN</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p>Hinge buckling and snap loading observed with the original connection were not measurable during the second wave-basin campaign.</p></li>
<li><p>Fatigue assessment indicated compatibility with the intended <strong>25-year service life</strong> under the evaluated load spectrum, material properties and modelling assumptions.</p></li>
<li><p>The validated 2 MWp farm model quantified connection loads across thousands of interconnected modules and identified both system-wide load reductions and local fatigue-critical regions.</p></li>
<li><p>Calculated breakwater reinforcement stresses remained substantially below yield, with fatigue-safety coefficients above unity.</p></li>
<li><p>No significant structural deterioration or measurable reinforcement loss was observed during approximately nine months of prototype marine exposure.</p></li>
<li><p>The SHMS demonstrator incorporated fatigue-crack propagation, corrosion assessment, remaining-life estimation and condition-based inspection functions.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Accordingly, the DoA targets for failure-rate reduction and remaining useful fatigue life were <strong>substantially supported by validated component tests, wave-basin experiments, farm-scale simulations and marine prototype evidence at TRL 5</strong>. The 2 MWp study provides particularly important system-level evidence because the validated model represented the complete protected farm rather than extrapolating solely from individual modules or small arrays.</p>
<p><strong>Overall Assessment</strong></p>
<p>The service-life objective was <strong>fully achieved at TRL 5</strong>. The numerical targets for failure-rate reduction and remaining useful fatigue life were not measured as statistical operational KPIs</p>
<p><strong>Deliverable references: D3.3, D5.3 and D5.7.</strong></p>
<p>In addition, the Structural Health Management System combines structural analysis, corrosion measurements and fatigue crack-propagation models to detect degradation, estimate remaining life and trigger condition-based inspections before critical failure occurs. This further reduces failure risk and supports longer and safer operation, although the resulting percentage improvement in remaining useful life can only be quantified after longer-term operation of a larger offshore demonstrator. <strong>Deliverable references: D5.4, D5.5, D5.6 and D5.7.</strong></p>
<p>The project instead demonstrated substantial improvements through increased connection capacity, elimination of observed failure mechanisms, 25-year fatigue compatibility, long-life infrastructure design and predictive structural-health monitoring.</p>
<p><strong>Monitoring and evaluation strategy</strong></p>
<p>Progress towards the lifetime impact was monitored using the following indicators:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>adopted system design life;</p></li>
<li><p>ultimate and fatigue capacity of critical connections;</p></li>
<li><p>occurrence of buckling and snap loading in basin tests;</p></li>
<li><p>structural stress relative to material yield;</p></li>
<li><p>fatigue-safety coefficients;</p></li>
<li><p>water and chloride penetration;</p></li>
<li><p>corrosion and reinforcement-section loss;</p></li>
<li><p>crack-growth and remaining-life calculations;</p></li>
<li><p>marine-exposure inspection results; and</p></li>
<li><p>operational performance of corrosion and vibration sensors.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The monitoring approach combined requirements from WP2, detailed design from WP3, material development and testing from WP4, predictive modelling and SHMS development from WP5, and laboratory, basin and marine validation from WP6.</p>
<p><strong>Overall assessment</strong></p>
<p><strong>The lifetime impact was largely achieved.</strong> SUREWAVE established a 25-year design basis, validated critical components against this requirement, developed highly durable marine materials and delivered predictive tools for remaining-life assessment and condition-based maintenance.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><u>Reliability</u>: Specifically, reliability will be improved by:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>The solar panel flat-low design directly in contact with water reduces the potential damages (by 70%) caused by lifting forces caused by surface wind and Aluminium solar panel shell structure very resistant to external loads and un-submersible to maximize availability and generation capacity</p></li>
<li><p>Decreased wind and wave exposure (up to 30% decrease intensity) thanks to optimal structural attenuating design of the breakwater shape through advanced predictive computational modelling and simulation tools (both for the material properties and for the whole structural integrity), and thanks to the shielding effect of breaking wave on the solar modules leading to a reduced lifting force, slamming load and potential damages.</p></li>
<li><p>Concrete breakwater extremely resilient to severe mechanical loads and to physical degradation in water environment (corrosion &amp; biofouling).</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Reliability</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status: Largely achieved, with the main load-reduction targets demonstrated through numerical modelling, laboratory testing and wave-basin validation.</strong></p>
<p>SUREWAVE improved system reliability through three complementary measures: <strong>the low-profile aluminium FPV design</strong>, <strong>protection by the floating breakwater</strong>, and development of a mechanically robust and durable concrete breakwater. The results demonstrate substantial reductions in the environmental loads acting on the FPV system and confirm the structural adequacy of the main components. However, reductions in actual damage frequency and system availability will require confirmation through a larger, long-term offshore demonstration.</p>
<p><strong>1. Low-profile aluminium FPV design</strong></p>
<p>The internal FPV system retained a flat, low-profile configuration close to the water surface. The modules use marine-grade <strong>5083-H111 aluminium</strong>, selected for its low weight, corrosion resistance and suitability for marine structures. Anodised components, polyurethane hinges, protected electrical connections and multiple sealing arrangements were incorporated to improve resistance to seawater, UV exposure, ageing and water ingress. <strong>Deliverable references: D3.1 and D3.4.</strong></p>
<p>The proposal anticipated a <strong>70% reduction in potential wind-lifting damage</strong>. Damage frequency itself could not be measured because no comparable baseline was operated over an extended period. Nevertheless, the aerodynamic simulations provided strong evidence of a substantial reduction in the underlying wind loads.</p>
<p>For a representative <strong>14 × 14 FPV matrix</strong>, the calculated:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>drag coefficient decreased from approximately <strong>372 without breakwater protection to 10 with the breakwater</strong>, corresponding to a reduction of approximately:</p></li>
</ul>
<p><span class="math display">$$\frac{372 - 10}{372} \times 100 \approx 97\%
$$</span></p>
<ul>
<li><p>lift coefficient decreased from approximately <strong>2,350 to 300</strong>, corresponding to a reduction of approximately:</p></li>
</ul>
<p><span class="math display">$$\frac{2350 - 300}{2350} \times 100 \approx 87\%
$$</span></p>
<p>These results exceed the original 70% target in terms of <strong>calculated aerodynamic load reduction for the evaluated configuration</strong>. The results cannot, however, be translated directly into an 87–97% reduction in damage frequency because damage also depends on load cycles, environmental directionality, material degradation and component resistance. <strong>Deliverable reference: D5.1.</strong></p>
<p>The low-profile design was also evaluated in the wave-basin campaigns. These tests demonstrated the importance of flexible connections between adjacent modules and led to the redesign of the original hinge after compression-induced buckling and snap loading were observed. The redesigned hinge eliminated these behaviours under the wave conditions tested during the second campaign. <strong>Deliverable references: D6.1, D6.2 and D6.3.</strong></p>
<p><strong>Impact assessment:</strong> The intended reduction of wind-induced loading was achieved and exceeded <strong>numerically for the representative configuration</strong>. The corresponding <strong>70% reduction in actual wind-related damage</strong> remains to be confirmed through long-term operation of a larger offshore installation.</p>
<p><strong>2. Reduction of wind and wave exposure through breakwater protection</strong></p>
<p>The floating breakwater was optimised through the assessment of <strong>17 alternative configurations</strong>, considering wave transmission, dynamic response, stability, manufacturability and transportability. The selected full-scale module measures approximately <strong>20 m × 5 m × 2.5 m</strong>, with a mass of approximately <strong>123.4 tonnes</strong>, a draft of <strong>2.03 m</strong> and a freeboard of <strong>0.47 m</strong>. <strong>Deliverable reference: D3.2.</strong></p>
<p>Hydrodynamic modelling showed that, for the FPV-critical wave-period range of <strong>1–3 seconds</strong>, the transmitted wave height was reduced to no more than approximately <strong>40% of the incident value</strong>. This corresponds to wave attenuation of at least approximately: <span class="math inline">60%</span>. The achieved attenuation therefore exceeded the original target of up to <strong>30% reduction in wave exposure</strong> for the relevant short-wave conditions. The breakwater was less effective for long-period waves, but the flexible FPV modules were able to follow these longer waves without generating comparably high connector loads. <strong>Deliverables references: D3.2 and D5.1.</strong></p>
<p>Two 1:10-scale wave-basin campaigns confirmed the breakwater’s protective effect. In the relevant frequency range, the breakwater reduced FPV motions by factors of approximately <strong>2–3</strong> compared with the unprotected configuration. The tests also generated measurements of wave transmission, FPV motions, relative-module motions, mooring response and slamming pressures. The maximum measured local slamming pressure was approximately <strong>126 kPa</strong>, and the associated structural assessment did not identify a threat to the breakwater’s integrity. <strong>Deliverable reference: D6.2.</strong></p>
<p>Validated large-scale hydrodynamic simulations (2MW) further showed:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>reductions of up to approximately <strong>50% in vertical connector loads and overturning moments</strong> under steep irregular-wave conditions;</p></li>
<li><p>reductions in vertical hinge loads by factors of approximately <strong>5–10</strong> for relevant short-period conditions; and</p></li>
<li><p>reductions in horizontal hinge loads by approximately a factor of <strong>3</strong>.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The analyses also identified standing-wave effects within the breakwater ring and increased wave-drift loads. These findings were incorporated into the mooring and system-design recommendations. <strong>Deliverables references: D5.1, D5.7 and D6.2.</strong></p>
<p>The breakwater therefore provided combined protection against wind and waves. Its performance depended on wave period, wind direction, breakwater height, separation distance and system layout. These dependencies were quantified through the modelling framework and used to improve the integrated design.</p>
<p><strong>Impact assessment:</strong> The original <strong>30% exposure-reduction target was achieved and exceeded</strong> for the relevant short-period waves and representative aerodynamic conditions. The results were supported by both validated numerical models and wave-basin experiments.</p>
<p><strong>3. Resilience of the concrete breakwater</strong></p>
<p>The breakwater’s reliability was supported by the development of high-strength, low-permeability and chloride-resistant circular concrete materials.</p>
<p>The selected formulations achieved:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>approximately <strong>128 MPa compressive strength</strong> for HPC;</p></li>
<li><p>approximately <strong>49 MPa compressive strength</strong> for LWAC;</p></li>
<li><p>no measurable water penetration in the selected HPC; and</p></li>
<li><p>chloride-migration coefficients of approximately <strong>0.4–0.9 × 10⁻¹² m²/s</strong>, indicating extremely high resistance to chloride ingress.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Mechanical testing showed that HPC shell-core beams achieved average static bending capacities of approximately <strong>25.14 kN</strong> in the strong direction and <strong>15.83 kN</strong> in the weak direction. HPC also showed approximately <strong>50% higher strength-to-weight performance than LWAC</strong>, together with superior cyclic and impact behaviour, supporting its selection as the principal structural-shell material. <strong>Deliverables references: D4.1 and D4.2.</strong></p>
<p>A detailed structural-integrity assessment was subsequently performed using a global and local ABAQUS framework incorporating more than <strong>2,300 reinforcement bars</strong>. Across <strong>33 extreme-event simulations</strong> for the Baltic Sea, Mediterranean Sea and North Sea, the maximum reinforcement stress remained at approximately <strong>7% of the 500 MPa yield strength</strong>. The lowest reported Soderberg fatigue-safety coefficient was approximately <strong>15.84</strong>, confirming substantial fatigue margins for the assessed reinforcement under ideal, non-corroded conditions. <strong>Deliverables references: D5.3 and D5.7.</strong></p>
<p>The reduced-scale prototype was successfully manufactured and deployed at the Port of Gijón. After approximately <strong>nine months of marine exposure</strong>:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>no significant structural deterioration was identified;</p></li>
<li><p>no measurable reduction in reinforcement thickness was observed;</p></li>
<li><p>localised chloride ingress was detected only near openings introduced for monitoring equipment; and</p></li>
<li><p>the corrosion and vibration-monitoring systems remained operational.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Biofouling coverage exceeded approximately <strong>85%</strong> on all evaluated surfaces and reached <strong>100%</strong> on one lateral surface. Wet biofouling biomass of approximately <strong>6.3–9.7 kg/m²</strong> was measured on the assessed lateral surfaces. Although the biofouling did not cause measurable structural degradation during the monitoring period, these results provide important inputs for calculating added mass, hydrodynamic resistance and future cleaning requirements. <strong>Deliverable reference: D6.4.</strong></p>
<p>The breakwater connections were also validated. The steel-wire connection between adjacent breakwater units achieved an average ultimate capacity of approximately <strong>402 kN</strong>, while the breakwater-to-FPV transition connection achieved approximately <strong>41 kN</strong>. <strong>Deliverable reference: D6.1.</strong></p>
<p><strong>Impact assessment:</strong> The concrete breakwater demonstrated high mechanical strength, very low permeability, strong chloride resistance and satisfactory short-term marine durability. Its resilience was supported by material testing, structural simulation, connection testing and marine demonstration. Full validation under multi-year open-sea exposure and the most extreme North Sea conditions remains a future qualification step.</p>
<p><strong>Adaptations and deviations</strong></p>
<p>The main reliability-related adaptation concerned the FPV hinge. Laboratory and wave-basin testing revealed compression-induced buckling and snap loading in the original design. The connection was consequently redesigned, increasing its tensile capacity from approximately <strong>3.69 kN to 8.36 kN</strong>, achieving approximately <strong>20.23 kN compression capacity</strong>, and eliminating the observed buckling and snap loading during the second basin campaign. This adaptation did not alter the project scope and substantially improved the final system reliability. <strong>Deliverable references: D6.1, D6.2 and D6.3.</strong></p>
<p>The proposal target was formulated as a <strong>70% reduction in potential wind-related damage</strong>. At <strong>TRL 5</strong>, this impact was assessed through validated aerodynamic simulations and supporting experimental activities rather than long-term offshore operational data. For the evaluated configuration, the floating breakwater reduced the calculated aerodynamic lift and drag loads by approximately <strong>87% and 97%</strong>, respectively. These results exceed the load-reduction level associated with the original target and support the technical effectiveness of the breakwater as aerodynamic protection. However, the project did not quantify the resulting reduction in component failures or damage frequency. Therefore, the objective was <strong>achieved at the validated modelling and TRL 5 level</strong>, while confirmation of the corresponding damage reduction requires long-term monitoring of a larger open-sea demonstrator.</p>
<p><strong>Overall reliability impact</strong></p>
<p>The reliability impact was <strong>largely achieved</strong>. SUREWAVE demonstrated:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>aerodynamic-load reductions exceeding the original 70% target for the representative simulated configuration;</p></li>
<li><p>at least approximately <strong>60% wave attenuation</strong> in the critical 1–3-second period range, exceeding the original 30% target;</p></li>
<li><p>FPV-motion reductions by factors of approximately <strong>2–3</strong>;</p></li>
<li><p>substantial reductions in connector loads;</p></li>
<li><p>elimination of the main failure mechanism identified in the original hinge;</p></li>
<li><p>large structural and fatigue-safety margins in the breakwater; and</p></li>
<li><p>satisfactory material and prototype performance during marine exposure.</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><u>Installability</u>: It will be drastically reduced thanks to:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>1) The breakwater concept which unlocks the <strong>possibility to moor the solar array directly to the breakwater</strong>. The following assumptions have been made: One tugging operation for a bundle of four elevated seaworthy offshore FPV rafts (for instance Moss Maritime or Solar Duck). Assuming 400 Wp solar panels. This results in 76 tugging operations for deploying 10 MWp FPV installation. By employing the SUREWAVE solution and using a seagoing barge, the number of tugging operations are reduced to 3, meaning a <strong>96% reduction</strong> of tugging.</p></li>
<li><p>2) easy to mould and <strong>port-site casting</strong> of the external <strong>concrete breakwater</strong> thus lowering logistic cost by 30%.</p></li>
<li><p>3) <strong>internal modular and foldable FPV</strong> light weight system of PV modules easy to install modular breakwater reducing the transportation and logistics cost by 30%.</p></li>
<li><p>4) <strong>Energy connection systems</strong> easy to install (40% less time) by routing DC homeruns on breakwater, reducing need for cable tray floats and allows for combining the operation of mooring and DC homerun routing.</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong><u>Installability</u></strong></p>
<p><strong>Status: Mostly achieved, with supporting evidence at TRL 5.</strong></p>
<p>The project developed and validated the main technical features intended to improve offshore FPV installation. At TRL 5, the evidence comprises detailed engineering design, numerical analysis, laboratory testing, wave-basin validation and reduced-scale prototype deployment. A full-scale comparative installation campaign was outside the project scope.</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Integrated mooring:</strong> The FPV array was designed to connect directly to the external breakwater through flexible transition elements, avoiding individual seabed moorings for the internal FPV modules. Preliminary mooring designs were developed, and optimisation reduced calculated mooring mass by approximately <strong>34–56%</strong> in selected cases. Connection testing supported the feasibility of the transition concept, although further optimisation and qualification are required. <strong>Deliverables:</strong> D3.3, D3.4, D5.1 and D6.1.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Port-side manufacture:</strong> A <strong>3.40 m × 1.70 m × 1.05 m</strong> concrete-breakwater prototype was manufactured, transported, launched and moored at the Port of Gijón. This demonstrated the principal construction, handling and deployment sequence at reduced scale. <strong>Deliverables:</strong> D4.1–D4.3 and D6.4.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Modular system architecture:</strong> Factory-assembled FPV strings and matrices were developed to support modular assembly, transport, installation and replacement. The detailed commercial design accommodated approximately <strong>7.16 MWp</strong>, including transition elements, maintenance corridors and electrical equipment. A <strong>420 kWp pilot configuration</strong> was also developed. <strong>Deliverables:</strong> D3.1 and D3.4.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Integrated electrical routing:</strong> DC homeruns were consolidated in designated corridors, with combiner boxes and inverters positioned at accessible locations on the breakwater bridges. This reduced the need for separate cable-support floats and enabled coordination of cable installation with assembly and mooring operations. <strong>Deliverables:</strong> D2.3 and D3.4.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The DoA projections of <strong>96% fewer towing operations, 30% lower logistics costs and 40% shorter electrical-installation time</strong> were not directly verified. These values remain projected commercial-scale benefits because the project did not build and install a complete full-scale system or conduct a controlled comparison with an alternative offshore FPV concept.</p>
<p><strong>Overall Assessment</strong></p>
<p>The installability impact was <strong>partially achieved at TRL 4–5</strong>. The necessary installation concepts, modular architecture, connection systems and electrical-routing solutions were developed, while reduced-scale manufacture and marine deployment provided relevant prototype evidence. However, the quantitative towing, installation-time and logistics-cost targets require validation through a larger open-sea pilot and, subsequently, a commercial-scale installation campaign.</p>
<p><strong>Evidence level:</strong> design and numerical assessment at system level; laboratory and wave-basin validation at component and subsystem level; reduced-scale prototype deployment in a relevant marine environment.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><u>O&amp;M</u>: SUREWAVE ensures high-efficient <strong>operability</strong> and drastic reduction of <strong>maintenance tasks</strong> thanks to:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>1) ease-to-use-app SHMS providing accurate structural integrity diagnosis reducing maintenance, inspection and replacement periods and preventing and reducing the occurrence of critical failures.</p></li>
<li><p>2) improved resilience to mechanical loads and physical marine degradation thanks to novel external protective breakwater made of specifically targeted concrete formulation to increase corrosion and biofouling resistance.</p></li>
<li><p>3) Smart mechanical <strong>connection design</strong> enabling quick and easy replacement if critical failure occurs.</p></li>
</ul></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Operability and Maintenance</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status: Largely achieved at TRL 5.</strong></p>
<p>The project delivered and validated the main technologies foreseen in the DoA for improving operability and reducing maintenance requirements. Evidence was obtained through integrated modelling, full-scale component tests, two wave-basin campaigns and operation of a reduced-scale breakwater prototype in a relevant marine environment.</p>
<ol type="1">
<li><p><strong>SHMS for condition-based maintenance:</strong> An operational SHMS demonstrator was developed by integrating structural analysis, fatigue-crack propagation, corrosion monitoring and remaining-life assessment. The system identifies critical degradation conditions and supports risk-based inspection planning, including routine and condition-triggered inspections. Corrosion and vibration-monitoring technologies were successfully demonstrated during the marine campaign.<br />
<strong>Deliverables:</strong> D5.3–D5.7 and D6.4.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Improved resilience to mechanical loads and marine degradation:</strong> The floating breakwater reduced the wave energy and motions reaching the protected FPV array. The developed HPC and LWAC demonstrated high mechanical strength, low permeability and strong chloride resistance. After approximately nine months of marine exposure, no significant structural deterioration or measurable reinforcement loss was detected. Biofouling was quantified to support future hydrodynamic and maintenance planning.<br />
<strong>Deliverables:</strong> D4.1, D4.2, D6.2 and D6.4.</p></li>
<li><p><strong>Smart and replaceable mechanical connections:</strong> Testing identified compression-induced buckling in the original FPV hinge and led to a redesigned connection. The average tensile capacity increased from <strong>3.69 kN to 8.36 kN</strong>, while the average compression capacity reached <strong>20.23 kN</strong>. The redesigned hinge was compatible with the intended 25-year service life under the assessed fatigue assumptions, and no measurable buckling or snap loading occurred during the second wave-basin campaign. The modular architecture, access corridors and breakwater bridges were designed to facilitate inspection and component replacement.<br />
<strong>Deliverables:</strong> D3.4 and D6.1–D6.3.</p></li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Overall Assessment</strong></p>
<p>The operability and maintenance objective was <strong>largely achieved at TRL 5</strong>. The SHMS, monitoring technologies, durable breakwater materials and replaceable connection system were integrated and validated at component, subsystem and prototype levels in relevant conditions. This provides credible evidence that the concept can support early fault detection, targeted inspection and condition-based maintenance.</p>
<p>The exact reduction in maintenance tasks, offshore interventions, downtime and O&amp;M expenditure was not quantified because the project did not include multi-year operation of a full-scale offshore installation. These quantitative benefits remain to be confirmed through a larger open-sea demonstrator progressing towards TRL 6.</p>
<p><strong>Evidence level:</strong> integrated-system validation at TRL 5, supported by laboratory testing, two wave-basin campaigns and approximately nine months of prototype monitoring in a relevant marine environment..</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

</section>

***<u>Topic Outcome 2</u>**: Better understanding of the device’s real-life performance allowing a safe reduction in the over-engineering of devices’ specifications*

<table style="width:100%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 99%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p>The <strong>Structural Health Management System toolkit</strong> will serve not only for the O&amp;M of the FPV system in service, but it will be also conceived to be used as an easy-to-use-app for having an <strong>optimal and accurate understanding of the FPV system real-life behaviour</strong>, allowing for the offshore FPV <strong>design, sizing, dimensioning, modelling and simulation</strong> to enable the <strong>quick, accurate, efficient and easy replication and customization</strong> of the offshore FPV system based in the novel SUREWAVE concept. This SHSM tool will allow to assess the combination of optimal designs of internal FPV components, mechanical joints, external breakwaters for a specific or a combination of required offshore conditions not only for worst scenarios but in middle and good scenarios for application in other sea basins, allowing a <strong>safe reduction in the over-engineering of the offshore FPV devices´ specifications</strong>.</p>
<p>The SHSM platform will be developed to integrate the novel breakwater solution for offshore extreme conditions with the internal FPV structures and <strong>considering different parametrizable</strong> mechanical connections (anchoring, mooring, internal modules connection) towards an <strong>evaluation of the structural health integrity</strong> for all the use cases based on AI. Specifically, this platform would provide a whole framework, which is able to test and validate the customized models, and to size different components. This simulation platform will <strong>focus on compromises between structural integrity, sustainability and cost-efficiency</strong> to <strong>optimise durability/cost</strong> performances.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Status: Largely achieved at validated modelling and technology-demonstration level.</strong></p>
<p>SUREWAVE developed and validated an integrated framework combining aero-hydrodynamic modelling, structural-integrity assessment, fatigue and corrosion analysis, experimental testing and the Structural Health Management System (SHMS). This framework improved understanding of the response of the FPV modules, connections, breakwater and mooring system under mild, moderate and harsh offshore conditions. It also enabled component optimisation based on calculated and measured loads rather than exclusively on conservative worst-case assumptions.</p>
<p><strong>Improved understanding of system performance</strong></p>
<p>The modelling framework covered the Baltic Sea, Mediterranean Sea and North Sea conditions and included operational, fatigue, accidental and extreme load cases. Hydrodynamic models were validated against wave-basin measurements, while structural models were calibrated using the concrete properties measured in WP4 and checked against the component tests conducted in WP6.</p>
<p>The validated analyses quantified several important aspects of system behaviour:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>The breakwater reduced FPV motions by factors of approximately <strong>2–3</strong> in the relevant frequency range. <strong>Deliverable reference:</strong> D6.2.</p></li>
<li><p>Vertical connector loads and overturning moments were reduced by up to approximately <strong>50%</strong> in steep irregular waves. <strong>Deliverable references:</strong> D5.1 and D5.7.</p></li>
<li><p>Aerodynamic simulations quantified substantial reductions in lift and drag for the protected FPV configuration. <strong>Deliverable reference:</strong> D5.1.</p></li>
<li><p>The models also identified standing-wave effects inside the breakwater ring and increased wave-drift loads, preventing unsafe under-design of the mooring system. <strong>Deliverables references:</strong> D5.1, D5.7 and D6.2.</p></li>
<li><p>The original FPV hinge’s buckling and snap-loading behaviour was identified experimentally, characterised numerically and eliminated through redesign. <strong>Deliverable references:</strong> D6.1–D6.3.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>These results provide a more realistic understanding of where load reductions are possible and where additional design capacity remains necessary.</p>
<p><strong>Safe reduction of over-engineering</strong></p>
<p>The validated modelling and optimisation framework enabled measurable reductions in material use. Mooring optimisation using time-domain simulations, surrogate models and genetic algorithms reduced calculated mooring-system mass by approximately <strong>34–56%</strong> for selected Mediterranean and North Sea configurations while satisfying the applicable structural constraints. <strong>Deliverable reference:</strong> D5.1.</p>
<p>The structural assessment included <strong>33 extreme-event simulations</strong> for the three deployment regions. The maximum reinforcement stress remained at approximately <strong>7% of the 500 MPa yield strength</strong>, while the lowest reported Soderberg fatigue-safety coefficient was approximately <strong>15.84</strong> under the assessed ideal, non-corroded conditions. These results revealed substantial structural margins and provide the basis for future design refinement without compromising safety. <strong>Deliverable references:</strong> D5.3 and D5.7.</p>
<p>The project also developed scenario-specific designs rather than applying the harshest North Sea requirements to all locations. This allows lighter and more cost-efficient solutions to be considered for the Mediterranean and Baltic Sea while retaining the necessary robustness for harsher environments.</p>
<p><strong>Structural Health Management System</strong></p>
<p>The SHMS demonstrator integrates:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>structural stress results;</p></li>
<li><p>environmental load histories;</p></li>
<li><p>fatigue crack-propagation calculations;</p></li>
<li><p>material properties;</p></li>
<li><p>corrosion-related reinforcement loss;</p></li>
<li><p>remaining-life assessment; and</p></li>
<li><p>inspection-planning criteria.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The application supports analysis of a defined crack, determination of critical crack orientation and identification of critical initial crack size. It also includes a conservative routine inspection interval of <strong>four years</strong>, supplemented by condition-triggered exceptional inspections. <strong>Deliverable references:</strong> D5.5–D5.7.</p>
<p>Its fatigue methodology was supported by <strong>598 finite-element simulations</strong> used to develop stress-intensity-factor solutions for reinforcement cracks. Crack-front growth was evaluated at <strong>153 points</strong>, enabling a more detailed representation of crack development under non-uniform stress fields. <strong>Deliverable references:</strong> D5.5 and D5.7.</p>
<p>The eddy-current corrosion sensor and wireless accelerometers were subsequently demonstrated on the marine prototype. The corrosion sensor operated through approximately <strong>5–6 cm of concrete cover</strong>, while environmental compensation reduced practical signal variability by around <strong>70%</strong>, from approximately ±448 Ω to ±135 Ω. <strong>Deliverable references:</strong> D5.4 and D6.4.</p>
<p>These capabilities enable future designs and maintenance plans to be updated using measured condition data, reducing reliance on fixed conservative assumptions.</p>
<p><strong>Replication and customisation</strong></p>
<p>The framework was applied to three representative European offshore environments and to different FPV, breakwater and mooring configurations. A detailed <strong>7.16 MWp commercial concept</strong> and a smaller <strong>420 kWp pilot configuration</strong> were developed, demonstrating the scalability and adaptability of the system. <strong>Deliverable reference:</strong> D3.4.</p>
<p>The Surewave-ANN platform also supported material customisation by predicting concrete compressive strength from mix composition. It was trained using <strong>1,030 concrete mixtures</strong>, and its weighted-ensemble model achieved an RMSE of approximately <strong>3.99 MPa</strong> on the test dataset. <strong>Deliverable reference:</strong> D5.2.</p>
<p><strong>Scientific and industrial impact</strong></p>
<p>Scientifically, SUREWAVE delivered experimentally validated methods for large-scale multi-body hydrodynamic analysis, aerodynamic shielding assessment, reinforced-concrete structural modelling, fatigue-crack propagation and corrosion-informed remaining-life prediction.</p>
<p>Industrially, these tools support:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>site-specific dimensioning instead of uniform worst-case design;</p></li>
<li><p>reduction of unnecessary mooring material;</p></li>
<li><p>identification of critical components before offshore deployment;</p></li>
<li><p>verification of safety margins;</p></li>
<li><p>predictive inspection and maintenance; and</p></li>
<li><p>adaptation of the system to different sea basins and deployment scales.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Changes and limitations</strong></p>
<p>The DoA envisaged an AI-supported platform for evaluating and sizing the complete integrated system. The final solution comprised several connected tools rather than one fully automated AI platform. Artificial intelligence was applied primarily in the Surewave-ANN material-prediction tool, while system assessment relied on validated physics-based models, optimisation algorithms and the SHMS.</p>
<p>The SHMS was demonstrated successfully, but parts of its degradation functionality initially used model-generated corrosion data because long-term field datasets were unavailable. Real corrosion and vibration measurements were subsequently generated during the WP6 marine campaign, but a longer operational dataset is needed to fully validate remaining-life predictions.</p>
<p>The project demonstrated a <strong>34–56% reduction in mooring mass</strong>, but it did not quantify a single project-wide percentage reduction in over-engineering across all components. Design reductions must remain component- and site-specific because the analyses also identified conditions, such as increased wave-drift loading, where additional capacity is necessary.</p>
<p><strong>Overall assessment</strong></p>
<p>Topic Outcome 2 was <strong>largely achieved</strong>. SUREWAVE substantially improved understanding of the real behaviour of the integrated FPV-breakwater system through validated numerical models, laboratory testing, wave-basin campaigns and marine monitoring. The project demonstrated safe material optimisation in the mooring system, identified structural margins, corrected an unforeseen hinge failure mechanism and delivered an operational SHMS for remaining-life assessment and inspection planning.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

***<u>Topic Outcome 3</u>**: Reduction of LCOE in line with the SET Plan targets (actions should clearly justify estimated LCOE at project start and end, using a recognised calculation methodology)*

<table>
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 100%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong>As per DoA, these methodologies were used for LCoE:</strong></p>
<p>SUREWAVE will reduce FPV <strong>CAPEX</strong> mainly with the circular concrete breakwater concept, and other aspects:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><u>Reduction of design &amp; fabrication costs</u>: 1) internal FPV strings from pressed, rolled marine grade Aluminium with reduced material usage and integrating recyclable and pawnable material <strong>reducing cost by 20%</strong> in comparison with current FPV; 2) external <strong>breakwater with up to 30% cost reduction</strong> owing to use of waste aggregates and reduction of 60% of cement; 3) <strong>40% reduction of design time/cost</strong> of the structural specifications of internal FPV, coupling system and external breakwater including engineering of PV field, thanks to accurate insights of the detailed aero and hydrodynamic calculations from the simulation toolkit.</p></li>
<li><p><u>Reduction of the transport &amp; installation costs</u>: 1) individual FPV strings able to be stacked into a common shipping container and rapidly deployed into the water, mechanically connected together <strong>reducing 30% logistic cost</strong>; 2) <strong>breakwater</strong> made of circular concrete mixes <strong>allowing cast on port</strong>, <strong>reducing 30% logistic cost</strong>; 3) Mooring the array directly to breakwater allowing a <strong>reduction of 40%</strong> of the <strong>anchoring &amp; mooring costs</strong>.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>SUREWAVE will reduce <strong>OPEX</strong> thanks to the structural integrity assured by the computational modelling toolkit:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><u>Reduction of failures and maintenance</u>: 1) <strong>Avoid 70% of internal FPV system failure</strong> thanks to strings using corrosion/aging resistant materials 2) <strong>Avoid 100% critical failure</strong> for <strong>fatigue crack of breakwater</strong>; 3) <strong>Avoid 80% of critical failure</strong> of the <strong>mechanical connections and mooring</strong> lines thanks to the capacity of non-linear wind and (breaking) wave impact loading simulations for different breakwater and PV field configurations.</p></li>
<li><p><u>Reduction of insurance costs</u>: The high reliability on the system thanks to solid combination of the design, simulation and in-service predictive O&amp;M provided by the SHMS is expected to <strong>reduce insurance costs by 20%.</strong></p></li>
<li><p><u>Reduction of reparation frequency works</u>: Thanks to an accurate prediction of the Maintenance, inspection and replacement periods provided by the SHMS a <strong>reduction of 30% on the O&amp;M travel expenses</strong> will be achieved.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>SUREWAVE improves the <strong>generable energy</strong> output of the FPV system offshore thanks to several approaches:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><u>Reduction of downtimes of the global system</u>: <strong>Securing the structural integrity of the system</strong>, thanks to the SHMS allowing accurate structural design, guarantees at least a reduction of <strong>50% of downtimes in operational sea-states</strong> and allows for a quick recovery of the availability of the system without critical failure during the low frequent survival sea-states (14 m. waves). This means an average increase of <strong>20% of the energy output</strong>.</p></li>
<li><p><u>Increasing energy efficiency</u>: 1) <strong>increase of 5%</strong> in the system efficiency is expected thanks to the internal FPV modules flat design <strong>maximizing cooling</strong> of PV panels limiting diffusion driven processes; 2) potential <strong>increase of 5%</strong> of available time of generation by optimizing the hydrodynamic behaviour <strong>minimizing splash zone</strong> inside FPV thanks to the breakwater attenuating effect on wave-current-wind conditions.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong><u>SUREWAVE LCoE calculations</u></strong>: A <strong><u>scenario</u></strong> has been estimated: 1) <strong>Location</strong> likely to receive high wind and waves and a substantial solar irradiation outside of the cost of Portugal (39.198900, -9.956500) in Atlantic Sea; 2) <strong>Solar irradiance</strong> of 1,645 kWh/m<sup>2</sup>. (<a href="https://meteonorm.com/en/meteonorm-version-8">Metonorm 8.0</a>); 3) <strong>Wave</strong>: wave height &gt; 8m, SIS calculations; 4) <strong>Plant size</strong>: although the full-scale preliminary target of SUREWAVE future exploitation is a 50MW plant with 150 W/m<sup>2</sup>, for this LCoE calculation a <strong>conservative solution</strong> is selected (with higher LCoE than the full-scale) of <strong>10MWp</strong>, with solar array of 274x274m<sup>2</sup> and breakwaters of 300x300 m<sup>2</sup> with a conservative power peak of 133 W/m<sup>2</sup>.</p>
<p><strong><u>Reference case</u></strong>: Current landlocked waters FPV hypothetically applied for offshore harsh conditions: 1) <strong>15 years lifetime</strong>; 2) 1.1 % annual degradation; 3) <strong>(E)</strong>: The total <strong>annual power production</strong> calculated is <strong>13,879 MWh/year</strong>, for the <strong>first year</strong> of operation. 4) CAPEX 1560 €/kWp 2 times the costs for regular FPV (0.78<a href="#fn1" class="footnote-ref" id="fnref1" role="doc-noteref"><sup>1</sup></a>*2=1.57 €/W); 5) OPEX 25.4 €/kWp/year (2 times typical costs for ground mounted solar 12.7€/kWp/year<a href="#fn2" class="footnote-ref" id="fnref2" role="doc-noteref"><sup>2</sup></a>); 6) Equity discount rate: 8 %; 7) No increasing O&amp;M costs per year. Then <strong>LCoE for the reference case is 161 €/MWh</strong> which is naturally very high since the base case illustrates an immature, expensive technology. It is perceived as a high-risk undertaking, hence the high interest rate. O&amp;M costs are high. The degradation is assumed to be a little higher than what can be expected from a modern ground mounted PV system, due to immaturity of the technology.</p>
<p><strong><u>Project case</u></strong>: <strong>A simulation has been performed</strong> using <strong><a href="https://www.pvsyst.com/">PVsyst</a></strong> (referent SW tool for PV systems analysis) with the following results. There have not been made any optimization of the physical footprint of the installation or electrical optimization. There has not been assumed any transmission losses from the site. The calculated <strong>thermal losses are -5.4 %</strong>. There has not been accounted for any additional gains due to cooling, which can be substantially more than 5 %, due to the average air and water temperature being below standard testing condition (25°C).</p>

<p>detailed bottom-up calculations based on feed stock materials, BOS, anchoring and mooring, machinery depreciation, production costs, labour, installation, soft costs and a <strong>CAPEX of breakwater</strong> of <strong>175 €/kWp</strong> (see <a href="#_Ref96335592">Table 2‑1</a>).</p>
<p>The <strong>LCoE is calculated with the recognized methodology</strong> of the Fraunhofer Institute for Solar Energy Systems (ISE)<a href="#fn3" class="footnote-ref" id="fnref3" role="doc-noteref"><sup>3</sup></a>. As a result, the LCoE for the estimated project case is <strong>54 €/MWh</strong>, significantly improving reference case and close to average in ground-mounted solar (<strong>48.5€/MWh</strong><a href="#fn4" class="footnote-ref" id="fnref4" role="doc-noteref"><sup>4</sup></a>). SUREWAVE LCoE is in line with the SET Plan <strong>LCoE targets for offshore wind</strong> by 2030<sup>4</sup> of between <strong>35 - 45 €/MWh</strong> for <strong>bottom-fixed</strong> or <strong>62 - 106 €/MWh</strong> for <strong>floating</strong>.</p></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>After completing the project this is status</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status: Largely achieved.</strong> SUREWAVE demonstrated a substantial reduction in the estimated LCOE compared with the immature offshore-FPV reference case and achieved a competitive result for Mediterranean deployment. The original project target of <strong>€54/MWh</strong> was not fully reached, but the final Mediterranean estimate of <strong>€62/MWh</strong> is within the <strong>€62–106/MWh SET Plan range for floating offshore wind</strong> cited in the DoA.</p>
<p><strong>Baseline and proposal-stage estimate</strong></p>
<p>At project start, the DoA defined two cases using the recognised Fraunhofer ISE LCOE methodology.</p>
<p>The <strong>reference case</strong> represented an existing landlocked-water FPV technology hypothetically installed under harsh offshore conditions. It assumed:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>10 MWp installed capacity;</p></li>
<li><p>15-year lifetime;</p></li>
<li><p>1.1% annual degradation;</p></li>
<li><p>first-year electricity production of 13,879 MWh;</p></li>
<li><p>CAPEX of €1,560/kWp;</p></li>
<li><p>OPEX of €25.4/kWp/year; and</p></li>
<li><p>an 8% discount rate.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>This resulted in an estimated reference LCOE of <strong>€161/MWh</strong>.</p>
<p>The proposal-stage <strong>SUREWAVE project case</strong> assumed commercial-scale production and lower investment risk, including:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>20-year lifetime;</p></li>
<li><p>0.7% annual degradation;</p></li>
<li><p>CAPEX of €781/kWp;</p></li>
<li><p>OPEX of €12.7/kWp/year;</p></li>
<li><p>a 4% discount rate; and</p></li>
<li><p>the same annual yield as the reference case.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Under these assumptions, the estimated LCOE was <strong>€54/MWh</strong>. This calculation assumed that the proposed reductions in fabrication, installation, maintenance and investment risk had already been achieved at commercial scale.</p>
<p><strong>Final methodology</strong></p>
<p>The final economic assessment replaced the single hypothetical Atlantic case with a more comprehensive, location-specific and lifecycle-based analysis. It evaluated <strong>27 cases</strong>, combining:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>three locations: Mediterranean Sea, Baltic Sea and North Sea;</p></li>
<li><p>several plant configurations and capacities; and</p></li>
<li><p>base, optimistic and pessimistic financial assumptions.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The final methodology considered updated CAPEX and OPEX, location-specific electricity generation, degradation, component replacement, financing, installation, decommissioning and a <strong>50-year lifetime for the structural infrastructure</strong>, with replacement of the PV generation layer after 25 years.</p>
<p><strong>Deliverable references:</strong> D7.1, D7.4 and D7.6.</p>
<p><strong>Final LCOE results</strong></p>
<p>For the representative 10 MW base-case configurations, the final results were:</p>

<colgroup>
<col style="width: 17%" />
<col style="width: 11%" />
<col style="width: 13%" />
<col style="width: 8%" />
<col style="width: 11%" />
<col style="width: 37%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><strong>Location</strong></th>
<th><strong>LCOE</strong></th>
<th><strong>NPV</strong></th>
<th><strong>IRR</strong></th>
<th><strong>Payback</strong></th>
<th><strong>Assessment</strong></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>Mediterranean Sea</td>
<td><strong>€62/MWh</strong></td>
<td><strong>€8.9 million</strong></td>
<td><strong>10.6%</strong></td>
<td><strong>8 years</strong></td>
<td>Economically attractive</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Baltic Sea</td>
<td><strong>€96/MWh</strong></td>
<td><strong>€2.11 million</strong></td>
<td><strong>2.21%</strong></td>
<td><strong>16 years</strong></td>
<td>Marginally viable and sensitive to financing</td>
</tr>
<tr>

<td><strong>€139/MWh</strong></td>
<td><strong>−€2.91 million</strong></td>
<td><strong>−3.11%</strong></td>
