</tbody>
</table>
<p><strong>Deliverable reference:</strong> D7.4.</p>
<p>The Mediterranean result was approximately <strong>14.8% above</strong> the original €54/MWh target:</p>
<p><span class="math display">$$\frac{62 - 54}{54} \times 100 = 14.8\%
$$</span></p>
<p>However, compared with the original immature offshore-FPV reference of €161/MWh, it represents an indicative reduction of approximately <strong>61.5%</strong>:</p>
<p><span class="math display">$$\frac{161 - 62}{161} \times 100 = 61.5\%
$$</span></p>
<p>The two cases are not strictly like-for-like because their technical and financial assumptions differ. Nevertheless, the comparison indicates that SUREWAVE progressed the concept from an immature, high-cost offshore application towards a potentially bankable renewable-energy solution.</p>
<p><strong>Contribution of CAPEX-reduction measures</strong></p>
<p><strong>Modular FPV design and fabrication</strong></p>
<p>The internal FPV system was developed as a modular, low-profile structure using marine-grade aluminium. Modules can be factory-assembled into strings of 14 units and subsequently combined into larger matrices, supporting repeatable production, compact transportation and phased deployment.</p>
<p>The project demonstrated the required design and assembly concept, but the original target of a <strong>20% reduction in FPV fabrication cost</strong> was not measured through a direct cost comparison with an equivalent commercial reference system.</p>
<p><strong>Deliverable references:</strong> D3.1 and D3.4.</p>
<p><strong>Circular concrete breakwater</strong></p>
<p>The project developed HPC, LWAC and cellular-concrete solutions incorporating recycled aggregates, recycled glass and industrial by-products.</p>
<p>The selected cellular concrete used approximately <strong>36 kg of cement per m³</strong>, compared with at least 150 kg/m³ for conventional non-structural concrete. This corresponds to an approximate <strong>76% reduction in cement consumption</strong>:</p>
<p><span class="math display">$$\frac{150 - 36}{150} \times 100 = 76\%
$$</span></p>
<p>The structural HPC used approximately <strong>380 kg of cement per m³</strong>, compared with the 850 kg/m³ conventional UHPFRC reference cited in the project. This corresponds to an approximate <strong>55% reduction</strong>:</p>
<p><span class="math display">$$\frac{850 - 380}{850} \times 100 = 55.3\%
$$</span></p>
<p>The original objective of reducing cement use by 60% was therefore exceeded for the cellular concrete but narrowly missed for the structural HPC (<strong>55.3%</strong> vs <strong>60%</strong>).</p>
<p><strong>Deliverable references:</strong> D4.1 and D4.2.</p>
<p>The final economic assessment found that the advanced circular material configurations were not yet the least-cost breakwater alternatives. Estimated breakwater costs for a 10 MW installation were approximately:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>conventional concrete configuration: €1.34 million;</p></li>
<li><p>hybrid configuration: €1.40 million;</p></li>
<li><p>LWAC configuration: €1.51 million; and</p></li>
<li><p>HPC configuration: €2.15 million.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>Consequently, the proposed <strong>30% breakwater-cost reduction</strong> was not achieved under the final cost assumptions. The circular solutions instead provided verified benefits in material efficiency, durability and environmental performance. Further cost reductions will require industrial scale-up, optimisation of material quantities and consideration of lifecycle benefits.</p>
<p><strong>Deliverable references:</strong> D4.1–D4.3 and D7.4.</p>
<p><strong>Modelling and design optimisation</strong></p>
<p>The aero-hydrodynamic, structural and fatigue tools reduced uncertainty in the loads acting on the FPV array, breakwater, connections and mooring system. These tools enabled site-specific designs rather than applying the harshest North Sea requirements to every location.</p>
<p>The clearest measurable result was the optimisation of the mooring system, which reduced calculated mooring mass by approximately <strong>34–56%</strong> for selected Mediterranean and North Sea configurations while maintaining the required structural constraints.</p>
<p><strong>Deliverable reference:</strong> D5.1.</p>
<p>The original target of a <strong>40% reduction in engineering time and cost</strong> was not directly measured because engineering hours were not recorded against a comparable conventional workflow. Nevertheless, reusable and validated simulation tools were delivered, reducing the need for repeated model development and physical testing in future applications.</p>
<p><strong>Deliverable references:</strong> D5.1, D5.3 and D5.7.</p>
<p><strong>Transport and installation costs</strong></p>
<p>The project developed an installation concept intended to reduce transport, offshore assembly and station-keeping requirements through:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>factory assembly of FPV strings and modular matrices;</p></li>
<li><p>transport of standard FPV modules and string components in conventional freight containers;</p></li>
<li><p>port-side casting and assembly of the concrete breakwater;</p></li>
<li><p>direct connection of the internal FPV array to the breakwater through transition elements;</p></li>
<li><p>use of seabed mooring and anchoring only for the external breakwater, without a separate anchoring system for the internal FPV array;</p></li>
<li><p>consolidated routing of electrical cables; and</p></li>
<li><p>defined procedures for transport, deployment, mooring and commissioning.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The modular Sunlit Sea FPV system uses standard PV modules assembled into strings that can be packed in conventional containers and transported to the assembly port. Port-side assembly reduces the need to tow fully assembled individual FPV rafts over long distances. The concrete breakwater can also be cast and assembled near the deployment port, reducing the transport of large prefabricated structures.</p>
<p>A further benefit is that the internal FPV array is connected directly to the protective breakwater. The principal seabed mooring and anchoring system is therefore required only for the breakwater, avoiding separate seabed anchors and mooring lines for the individual FPV matrices. This simplifies the station-keeping architecture and reduces the number of offshore connection operations.</p>
<p>The installation concept was supported by the manufacture, transport, launch and mooring of a reduced-scale concrete breakwater prototype at the Port of Gijón. Mooring optimisation also achieved calculated mass reductions of approximately <strong>34–56%</strong> in selected configurations, providing evidence of potential reductions in material demand, transport weight and offshore handling requirements.</p>
<p><strong>Deliverable references:</strong> D3.1, D3.3, D3.4, D4.3, D5.1 and D6.4.</p>
<p>The original DoA targets of <strong>30% lower FPV logistics costs, 30% lower breakwater logistics costs and 40% lower anchoring and mooring costs</strong> were not directly measured because a full-scale comparative installation was outside the scope of the TRL 5 project. Nevertheless, the project delivered the technical features required to pursue these reductions: containerised transport of modular FPV components, port-side breakwater manufacture, elimination of separate seabed anchoring for the internal FPV array, and an optimised breakwater-mooring system.</p>
<p>The calculated <strong>34–56% reduction in mooring mass</strong> cannot be interpreted directly as an equivalent reduction in installed cost, because anchors, vessels, mobilisation, labour, weather delays and offshore operations remain important cost drivers. The quantitative DoA targets should therefore be considered <strong>supported by the TRL 5 design and prototype evidence but not yet commercially verified</strong>. Confirmation requires a larger open-sea pilot in which transport volumes, port-assembly hours, vessel operations, installation duration and actual costs are measured against a defined reference FPV system.</p>
<p><strong>Contribution of OPEX-reduction measures</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status: Largely achieved at TRL 5; quantitative savings require operational validation.</strong></p>
<p>The project developed and validated the main technologies intended to reduce failures, maintenance and offshore interventions:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>a redesigned FPV hinge with approximately <strong>127% higher tensile capacity</strong>, improved compression resistance and no measurable buckling or snap loading during the second wave-basin campaign;</p></li>
<li><p>fatigue compatibility with the intended <strong>25-year service life</strong> under the assessed loads and assumptions;</p></li>
<li><p>experimentally characterised breakwater and transition connections;</p></li>
<li><p>corrosion and vibration-monitoring technologies demonstrated during marine exposure; and</p></li>
<li><p>an operational SHMS supporting fatigue assessment, remaining-life estimation and condition-based inspection planning.</p></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>Deliverable references:</strong> D5.3–D5.7 and D6.1–D6.4.</p>
<p>These results provide TRL 5 evidence for earlier fault detection, targeted inspection and reduced risk of unplanned failures. However, the DoA targets of avoiding <strong>70% of internal FPV failures, 100% of critical fatigue-crack failures and 80% of critical connection and mooring failures</strong>, together with reductions of <strong>20% in insurance costs and 30% in O&amp;M travel expenditure</strong>, were not directly measured.</p>
<p>The technical basis for these reductions was demonstrated, but the percentages remain projected commercial impacts. Verification requires a larger open-sea demonstrator with multi-year records of failures, maintenance, vessel use, downtime, insurance and O&amp;M costs.</p>
<p><strong>Energy production and availability</strong></p>
<p><strong>Status: Partially achieved at TRL 5.</strong></p>
<p>The final economic assessment used location-specific initial energy yields of approximately:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>1,306 kWh/kWp/year</strong> for the Mediterranean Sea;</p></li>
<li><p><strong>927 kWh/kWp/year</strong> for the Baltic Sea; and</p></li>
<li><p><strong>898 kWh/kWp/year</strong> for the North Sea.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The analysis included module degradation, electrical losses, wave-induced mismatch and operational downtime. Wave-basin testing and numerical modelling demonstrated that the floating breakwater reduced FPV motions and connector loads, providing TRL 5 evidence of improved system resilience and potential availability.</p>
<p>However, the DoA targets of <strong>50% lower downtime, 20% higher energy output, 5% higher efficiency from cooling and 5% higher availability from reduced splash and wave exposure</strong> were not directly verified through long-term energy-production measurements. The final economic model therefore used site-specific energy yields and did not assume a general 20% production increase or an additional 5% cooling benefit.</p>
<p><strong>Deliverables:</strong> D5.1, D6.2 and D7.4.</p>
<p><strong>Grid-Connection Sensitivity</strong></p>
<p>The Mediterranean LCOE of approximately <strong>€62/MWh</strong> assumes a short, approximately <strong>300 m floating-cable connection</strong> to nearby offshore infrastructure. Including a dedicated submarine export cable increased the Mediterranean LCOE to approximately <strong>€76/MWh</strong>, an increase of about <strong>23%</strong>, and reduced the IRR from approximately <strong>10.7% to 6.2%</strong>. For the assessed North Sea case, the long export-cable requirement increased the LCOE to more than <strong>€450/MWh</strong>.</p>
<p>These results demonstrate that proximity to an offshore substation, wind farm or other shared marine-energy infrastructure is a key condition for competitive deployment.</p>
<p><strong>Deliverable:</strong> D7.4.</p>
<p><strong>Changes from the DoA</strong></p>
<p>The final assessment provides a more conservative and comprehensive evaluation than the original DoA calculation:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>three European sea basins were assessed instead of one hypothetical Atlantic location;</p></li>
<li><p>the base discount rate was increased from <strong>4% to 8.5%</strong> to reflect the current technology and investment risk;</p></li>
<li><p>Mediterranean CAPEX was estimated at approximately <strong>€900/kWp</strong>, compared with <strong>€781/kWp</strong> in the DoA;</p></li>
<li><p>the model included site-specific O&amp;M, decommissioning, a 50-year structural lifetime and replacement of the PV layer after 25 years; and</p></li>
<li><p>the original projections for higher output, lower downtime and reduced O&amp;M were not treated as achieved without operational evidence.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>These adaptations did not change the project scope. They produced a more realistic TRL 5 business case and identified high solar yield, short or shared grid connections, infrastructure reuse and technology maturation as the principal conditions for commercial viability.</p>
<p><strong>Overall achievement of Topic Outcome 3</strong></p>
<p>Topic Outcome 3 was <strong>largely achieved at TRL 5</strong>.</p>
<p>The final techno-economic assessment estimated an LCOE of approximately <strong>€62/MWh</strong> for the representative Mediterranean case. This result:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>is approximately <strong>14.8% above</strong> the original target of €54/MWh;</p></li>
<li><p>represents an indicative <strong>61.5% reduction</strong> from the DoA’s immature offshore-FPV reference of €161/MWh; and</p></li>
<li><p>reaches the lower boundary of the <strong>€62–106/MWh range for floating offshore wind</strong> cited in the DoA.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The Mediterranean scenario demonstrated a credible commercial pathway, with an estimated <strong>NPV of €8.9 million, IRR of approximately 10.6% and payback period of eight years</strong>. The corresponding LCOE estimates were approximately <strong>€96/MWh for the Baltic Sea</strong> and <strong>€139/MWh for the North Sea</strong>. The Baltic case showed conditional economic potential, while stand-alone North Sea deployment was not economically viable under the base assumptions.</p>
<p>At <strong>TRL 5</strong>, these results are based on validated engineering designs, numerical models, laboratory and wave-basin testing, prototype marine demonstration, and detailed techno-economic modelling. They do not represent costs measured from a full-scale commercial installation. The difference between the €62/MWh result and the €54/MWh target reflects the use of more detailed and conservative assumptions for CAPEX, O&amp;M, component replacement, decommissioning and financing.</p>
<p>Further progression towards the €54/MWh target will require industrial-scale manufacturing, continued structural and mooring optimisation, reduced financing risk, shared offshore grid infrastructure, certification and validation through a larger open-sea demonstrator.</p>
<p><strong>Overall conclusion:</strong> Topic Outcome 3 was <strong>largely achieved at TRL 5</strong>, with a competitive Mediterranean business case demonstrated through validated project evidence. Commercial-scale cost and performance must be confirmed during progression towards TRL 6 and subsequent market deployment.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

***<u>Topic Outcome 4</u>**: Contribution to the objectives of the Mission Healthy oceans, seas, coastal and inland waters*

<table>
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 100%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p>SUREWAVE will contribute to “<strong>objective 3 - Make the sustainable blue economy carbon-neutral &amp; circular</strong>” by:</p>
<ul>
<li><p><strong>Massively reducing GHG emissions from maritime economic activities</strong> in the EU thus paving the way towards the net zero maritime emissions. In particular, massively increased renewable power could be used to provide synthetic fuels to decarbonize navigation, for which conclusive strategies are still lacking.</p></li>
<li><p>Keeping <strong>marine ecosystems in balance</strong> by designing low-impact constructions and operating conditions taking into insights from the environmental assessment on potential local environmental impacts on ecosystems.</p></li>
<li><p>Aligned with the Mission, it will be done <strong>through the implementation of R&amp;D</strong> actions in technical solutions for the <strong>use of renewable energy</strong> as it is the offshore FPV.</p></li>
</ul></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Status: Largely achieved.</strong></p>
<p>SUREWAVE contributed to <strong>Objective 3 of the Mission, “Make the sustainable blue economy carbon-neutral and circular,”</strong> by developing and validating a low-carbon offshore FPV concept, incorporating circular materials and assessing its environmental interaction with marine ecosystems. The contribution was evaluated through cradle-to-grave Life Cycle Assessment, local environmental-impact assessment, marine prototype monitoring and integrated sustainability assessment.</p>
<p><strong>Contribution to decarbonising the blue economy</strong></p>
<p>The environmental assessment showed that electricity generated by the SUREWAVE concept can reduce greenhouse-gas emissions by <strong>more than 90% compared with coal-based electricity generation</strong>, depending on the deployment scenario. The Mediterranean scenario achieved the best environmental performance because of its higher electricity yield and more favourable installation and operating conditions.</p>
<p>The assessment identified vessel fuel consumption, PV modules, anchoring and mooring systems, and breakwater construction as the main environmental hotspots. This provides clear priorities for further improvement through reduced vessel operations, low-carbon materials, shared offshore infrastructure and increased recycling.</p>
<p>SUREWAVE therefore demonstrated the potential of offshore FPV to expand renewable-electricity generation without additional land occupation. The electricity could support coastal and maritime activities directly or, in future applications, contribute to renewable hydrogen and synthetic-fuel production. However, fuel production and use in shipping were not demonstrated within the project and remain a potential downstream application.</p>
<p><strong>Deliverable references:</strong> D7.3 and D7.6.</p>
<p><strong>Circular use of materials</strong></p>
<p>The project developed circular HPC, LWAC and cellular-concrete solutions incorporating recycled concrete aggregate, recycled glass and industrial by-products.</p>
<p>The selected cellular concrete used approximately <strong>36 kg of cement per m³</strong>, compared with at least 150 kg/m³ for conventional non-structural concrete, corresponding to approximately <strong>76% lower cement consumption</strong>. The structural HPC used approximately <strong>380 kg of cement per m³</strong>, representing around <strong>55% less cement</strong> than the 850 kg/m³ conventional UHPFRC reference considered in the project.</p>
<p>Marine-grade aluminium was selected for the FPV floats because of its corrosion resistance, low weight and recyclability. The long-life breakwater and anchoring infrastructure were designed to support more than one generation of PV modules, improving material efficiency over the system lifetime.</p>
<p><strong>Deliverable references:</strong> D3.1, D3.3, D4.1, D4.2 and D7.3.</p>
<p>The circular concrete solutions achieved the intended material and environmental benefits, although they were not yet the lowest-cost breakwater alternatives in the final economic assessment. Further industrial optimisation is needed to combine their environmental advantages with lower initial cost.</p>
<p><strong>Protection of marine ecosystems</strong></p>
<p>The project assessed potential effects on marine flora and fauna, water quality, fisheries, navigation, habitats, landscape and competing uses of marine space. The assessment concluded that impacts can be managed through appropriate site selection, marine spatial planning, installation timing, environmental monitoring and stakeholder engagement.</p>
<p>The breakwater prototype was monitored for approximately <strong>nine months</strong> at the Port of Gijón. No significant structural deterioration or measurable reinforcement loss was detected. Biofouling coverage exceeded <strong>85%</strong> on the evaluated surfaces and reached <strong>100%</strong> on one lateral surface, with wet biomass of approximately <strong>6.3–9.7 kg/m²</strong> on the assessed lateral surfaces.</p>
<p>These observations provided project-specific evidence on the interaction between the concrete structure and marine organisms. The results showed that the prototype supported substantial biological colonisation rather than preventing biofouling. Consequently, the project generated useful data for future ecological assessment, hydrodynamic modelling and maintenance planning.</p>
<p><strong>Deliverable references:</strong> D6.4 and D7.3.</p>
<p>The project did not undertake a long-term ecosystem study around a commercial offshore installation. Conclusions regarding ecosystem balance therefore remain based on environmental assessment and short-duration prototype monitoring and should be confirmed through a larger open-sea demonstrator.</p>
<p><strong>R&amp;D contribution to sustainable marine renewable energy</strong></p>
<p>SUREWAVE advanced offshore FPV from an initial concept at approximately <strong>TRL 3 to an integrated concept at TRL 5</strong>. This was achieved through:</p>
<ul>
<li><p>design of an integrated FPV and floating-breakwater system;</p></li>
<li><p>development of circular marine concrete materials;</p></li>
<li><p>aero-hydrodynamic and structural modelling;</p></li>
<li><p>laboratory testing of critical components;</p></li>
<li><p>two wave-basin validation campaigns;</p></li>
<li><p>development of corrosion and structural-health-monitoring technologies; and</p></li>
<li><p>marine deployment of the breakwater prototype.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The project also identified the Mediterranean as the preferred location for initial demonstration because it provided the strongest combined environmental, technical and economic performance.</p>
<p><strong>Deliverable references:</strong> D3.4, D5.7, D6.2, D6.4, D7.2 and D7.6.</p>
<p><strong>Changes and limitations</strong></p>
<p>The original DoA expected SUREWAVE to contribute to the decarbonisation of maritime activities, including the potential production of synthetic fuels. The project demonstrated low-carbon renewable-electricity generation but did not develop or validate a synthetic-fuel production chain.</p>
<p>Similarly, the project assessed potential local environmental impacts and monitored biological colonisation of the prototype, but it did not demonstrate ecosystem effects at commercial scale or over multiple years. These aspects require environmental monitoring during the next open-sea demonstration phase.</p>
<p><strong>Overall assessment</strong></p>
<p>Topic Outcome 4 was <strong>largely achieved</strong>. SUREWAVE demonstrated that offshore FPV can contribute to a carbon-neutral and circular blue economy by delivering very low-carbon electricity, increasing the use of recycled and secondary materials, extending renewable-energy deployment into marine areas and developing approaches for environmentally responsible design and operation.</p>
<p>The project also identified environmental hotspots and generated real marine data on corrosion, durability and biofouling. Further work is required to validate ecosystem impacts at commercial scale and to demonstrate downstream applications such as renewable hydrogen or synthetic fuels for maritime transport.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

<table>
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 100%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th><p><strong><u>Scientific impacts</u></strong>: The work in the SUREWAVE project aims at producing the following scientific impacts:</p>
<table style="width:99%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 21%" />
<col style="width: 76%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th>Scientific impacts</th>
<th style="text-align: left;">Value</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>Number of PhD</td>
<td><strong>(1)</strong>: CEIT (1);</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Peer-reviewed publications or papers</td>
<td style="text-align: left;"><p><strong>(10)</strong>: MARIN (3); CEIT (5): SINTEF (2)</p>
<p>See detail in Section 2.2.2</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Number of new research lines</td>
<td style="text-align: left;"><strong>(4)</strong>: Coupled aero &amp; Hydrodynamic simulation; Structural Health Management; Concrete circular mixes for offshore structures; Material properties modelling of material solution with complex aggregates;</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><strong>Status:</strong> PhD: 1) Upeksha Chathuran Thibbotuwa Deniye Kankanamge and Unai Eibar Arambarri</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p>The DoA target was <strong>10 peer-reviewed publications or papers</strong>. By the end of the project, the consortium had produced at least <strong>six published journal articles and three peer-reviewed conference papers</strong>, together with additional conference contributions, preprints and <strong>2 journal manuscripts are submitted</strong>.</p>
<p>The published outputs addressed breakwater aerodynamics, wave-basin validation, large-scale hydrodynamic modelling, corrosion sensing, wireless monitoring, structural integrity and fatigue-crack modelling.</p>
<p>Based on the currently confirmed publications, <strong>nine peer-reviewed papers</strong> can be counted towards the DoA target. The target should therefore be reported as <strong>largely achieved</strong>, unless at least one additional conference contribution is confirmed as a peer-reviewed proceedings paper.</p></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Research Lines (RL):</strong></p>
<p><strong>RL-1: Coupled aerodynamic and hydrodynamic modelling</strong></p>
<p>SUREWAVE established a new research line combining aerodynamic CFD, potential-flow modelling, time-domain analysis, large-scale multi-body simulation and physical wave-basin validation for protected offshore FPV systems.</p>
<p>The work quantified wind shielding, wave attenuation, FPV motions, connection loads, standing-wave effects, wave-drift loads and mooring response. Models were extended to a <strong>2 MWp farm with 3,660 FPV modules and 32 floating breakwaters</strong>, demonstrating the feasibility of large-scale simulations using high-performance computing.</p>
<p><strong>Deliverable references:</strong> D5.1, D5.7 and D6.2.</p>
<p><strong>RL-2: Structural Health Management</strong></p>
<p>A second research line was established around sensor-informed structural-health management for offshore reinforced-concrete structures. This included: eddy-current corrosion sensing; wireless vibration monitoring; fatigue-crack propagation; corrosion-informed remaining-life assessment; critical-crack evaluation; and condition-based inspection planning.</p>
<p>The resulting SHMS connects numerical structural analysis with monitored degradation and provides a foundation for predictive maintenance of offshore FPV and other marine structures.</p>
<p><strong>Deliverable references:</strong> D5.3–D5.7 and D6.4.</p>
<p><strong>RL-3: Circular concrete materials for offshore structures</strong></p>
<p>SUREWAVE created a research line on circular and lightweight concrete solutions for floating marine infrastructure. The project developed <strong>18 LWAC formulations, two HPC formulations and 26 cellular-concrete formulations</strong>, incorporating recycled concrete aggregate, recycled glass, fly ash and GGBFS.</p>
<p>The selected formulations were validated through mechanical, water-penetration, chloride-ingress, cyclic, impact and marine-exposure testing and were used to manufacture the breakwater prototype.</p>
<p><strong>Deliverable references:</strong> D4.1–D4.3 and D6.4.</p>
<p><strong>RL-3: Data-driven modelling of complex concrete materials</strong></p>
<p>The fourth research line focused on machine-learning prediction of concrete properties. The SUREWAVE-ANN platform was developed using a database of <strong>1,030 concrete mixtures</strong>. Multiple machine-learning approaches were evaluated, with the weighted-ensemble model achieving a test RMSE of approximately <strong>3.99 MPa</strong> for compressive-strength prediction.</p>
<p>The platform provides a reusable basis for screening and optimising future circular concrete formulations while reducing the number of initial experimental trials.</p>
<p><strong>Deliverable reference:</strong> D5.2.</p></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

**<u>Economic impacts</u>**: SUREWAVE will develop an advanced FPV solution for harsh marine environments in the offshore energy sector and will address, solve and test the real problems structural integrity, lifetime, durability, reliability, LCoE reduction, thus favouring the awareness of the end-users and the future adoption of the solution:

| Economic Impacts *(based on exploitation strategy in Section [2.2.3](#_Ref95133767))* | Value |
|----|---:|
| Number of offshore FPV facilities deployed using SUREWAVE project solution by 2032 | 25 |
| Number of energy utilities implementing the SUREWAVE solution by 2032 | 5-10 |
| Forecast of revenues due to the exploitation of SUREWAVE solutions[^1] | 967.2 M€ |
| Investments needed to obtain the revenues[^2] | 50 M€ |

**Status: Progress achieved and commercial pathway established; long-term 2032 targets remain prospective.**

SUREWAVE developed and validated an offshore FPV concept addressing the main barriers to commercial adoption, including structural integrity, durability, mechanical connections, mooring, maintainability and LCOE. The project advanced the integrated concept to **TRL 5** and generated exploitable results covering floating breakwaters, circular concrete materials, FPV connections, modelling tools, corrosion sensors and the Structural Health Management System.

The economic assessment confirmed that commercial potential is strongly location-dependent. For a representative **10 MW Mediterranean installation**, the estimated results were:

- **LCOE:** approximately **€62/MWh**;

- **NPV:** approximately **€8.9 million**;

- **IRR:** approximately **10.6%**; and

- **payback period:** approximately **8 years**.

The corresponding LCOE estimates were approximately **€96/MWh for the Baltic Sea** and **€139/MWh for the North Sea**. These results identify the Mediterranean as the preferred market for initial pilot and commercial deployment. Baltic deployment may become viable under favourable financing and infrastructure conditions, while stand-alone North Sea deployment requires further cost reduction, infrastructure sharing or hybridisation with offshore wind.

**Deliverable references:** D7.4 and D7.6.

The project also provided measurable evidence supporting future cost reductions. Mooring optimisation reduced calculated mooring mass by approximately **34–56%** in selected cases. Modular FPV matrices, integrated anchoring, port-side breakwater manufacture and consolidated electrical routing were developed to reduce installation complexity. Predictive modelling and SHMS technologies were demonstrated to support condition-based maintenance and reduce technical uncertainty.

**Deliverable references:** D3.3, D3.4, D4.3 and D5.1–D5.7.

**Progress towards the DoA economic KPIs**

The DoA economic indicators concern post-project exploitation up to 2032. Since the project concluded at TRL 5, progress against these indicators was assessed through validated system designs, market scenarios, partner exploitation plans and techno-economic calculations rather than realised commercial sales.

| **Economic KPI** | **DoA target by 2032** | **Project evidence and calculated pathway** |
|----|----|----|
| Offshore FPV facilities using project results | **25** | Commercial and pilot configurations were developed, including a 10 MW-class commercial concept and a smaller pilot configuration. Achieving 25 facilities would correspond to an average uptake of **2.5–5 facilities per implementing utility**. |
| Energy utilities implementing the solution | **5–10** | Utilities, developers and other market actors were addressed through stakeholder engagement, dissemination and exploitation activities. The Mediterranean was identified as the priority market for initial implementation. |
| Forecast exploitation revenue | **€967.2 million** | The DoA forecast was based on approximately **1.24 GW of cumulative deployment**, calculated using the assumed selling-price proxy of **€780/kW**: $`\text{€}967.2\text{~million}/\text{€}780\text{/kW} = 1.24\text{~GW}`$. This is equivalent to approximately **62 installations of 20 MW each**, consistent with the original revenue assumptions. |
| Follow-up investment | **€50 million** | The investment requirement represents approximately **5.2% of the forecast revenue**, corresponding to a gross forecast revenue-to-investment ratio of approximately **19:1**. Around **€40 million** was associated with the proposed 25 MW demonstration, with the remaining amount intended for commercialisation and project-development activities. |

The project established a quantified basis for pursuing these targets through:

- validated TRL 5 system and component designs;

- commercial-scale and pilot-scale configurations;

- a Mediterranean 10 MW business case with an estimated **LCOE of €62/MWh**, **NPV of €8.9 million**, **IRR of approximately 10.6%** and an **eight-year payback period**;

- validation of critical connections, breakwater materials and monitoring technologies;

- identification of Key Exploitable Results, ownership and exploitation routes;

- selection of the Mediterranean as the priority entry market; and

- preparation of a staged pathway covering demonstration, qualification, certification and replication.

The original **€967.2 million** figure is an ex-ante exploitation forecast rather than revenue generated during the project. Similarly, the **€50 million** represents the estimated financing required to progress from the validated TRL 5 concept towards demonstration and market deployment. These figures should therefore be reported as **modelled 2032 economic KPIs supported by the project’s technical and economic results**.

**Commercialisation Pathway**

The final project evidence refined the pathway towards the 2032 indicators:

1.  pilot-scale Mediterranean deployment;

2.  component qualification and certification;

3.  first commercial projects in high-irradiance locations with short or shared grid connections;

4.  replication through 5–10 utilities and offshore-energy developers;

5.  Baltic deployment under favourable financing and infrastructure conditions; and

6.  North Sea deployment through co-location, hybridisation or shared offshore infrastructure.

**Overall Assessment**

The project achieved a **significant enabling economic impact at TRL 5**. It delivered a technically validated concept, a positive Mediterranean business case, defined exploitable technologies and a quantified route towards the 2032 deployment and revenue indicators.

The targets of **25 facilities, adoption by 5–10 utilities, €967.2 million in revenue and €50 million in follow-up investment** remain future exploitation KPIs because their target year is 2032. However, the project has established the technical design, economic calculations, priority market and exploitation framework required to pursue them.

### 1.4 Update of the plan for exploitation and dissemination of results (if applicable)

The Dissemination, Exploitation and Communication Plan was updated during the final reporting period to reflect the project’s validated results, revised technology maturity, final economic assessment, stakeholder feedback and post-project exploitation needs. The updated plan builds on the initial strategy in **D8.1**, the stakeholder engagement activities in **D2.4 and D8.2**, the IPR arrangements in **D1.4**, and the final dissemination and exploitation planning reported in **D8.3 and D8.5**.

**Exploitation updates**

The initial exploitation strategy was refined from a broad system-level commercialisation concept into a portfolio of identifiable Key Exploitable Results, including:

- the integrated SUREWAVE offshore FPV concept;

- floating-breakwater, transition-element, mooring and anchoring solutions;

- circular HPC, LWAC and cellular-concrete formulations;

- the redesigned FPV hinge and connection system;

- aero-hydrodynamic modelling and optimisation methodologies;

- the Surewave-ANN concrete-property prediction tool;

- structural-integrity and fatigue-assessment methodologies;

- eddy-current corrosion-monitoring technology; and

- the Structural Health Management System.

For each result, the consortium is consolidating ownership, involved partners, target users, exploitation route, required background knowledge, protection measures and next steps. The principal exploitation routes include direct use by partners, customised engineering and consultancy, software or technology licensing, integration into commercial products, technology transfer, joint development and follow-on demonstration projects.

The final techno-economic assessment also refined the geographical commercialisation pathway. The Mediterranean was identified as the preferred market for initial demonstration and deployment, with an estimated LCOE of approximately **€62/MWh**, NPV of **€8.9 million**, IRR of **10.6%**, and payback period of approximately **eight years** for the representative 10 MW case. The Baltic Sea represents a possible second-stage market, while North Sea deployment will require further cost reduction, shared infrastructure, hybridisation or targeted demonstration support.

The proposed route to market was consequently updated to:

1.  complete result-ownership and partner-specific exploitation plans;

2.  further qualify critical components, particularly the FPV hinge for harsh North Sea conditions;

3.  conduct a kW- to low-MW open-sea demonstrator;

4.  pursue independent technology qualification and certification;

5.  establish partnerships with project developers, utilities and offshore-infrastructure owners; and

6.  progress towards first commercial deployment in favourable Mediterranean locations.

This staged pathway is considered more realistic than moving directly from TRL 5 to the originally envisaged large-scale demonstration. It does not change the long-term ambition but reduces technical and financial risk before major investment.

**IPR and result ownership**

The SUREWAVE Consortium Agreement and the updated IPR Management Plan governed ownership, background, access rights, confidentiality, joint ownership and dissemination review. Results generally remained with the partner that generated them, while joint ownership applied only where intellectual contributions were inseparable. Detailed formulations, engineering designs, numerical models, software, source code and commercially sensitive know-how remained under the control of the respective owners. Public outputs were subject to consortium review and a non-objection procedure before release. No major ownership or access-right dispute requiring formal escalation was reported. Remaining actions include confirming result-specific ownership, protection and licensing arrangements before post-project demonstration or commercial use. The more details on IPR are provided in D1.8.

**Stakeholder and market feedback**

Stakeholder and partner feedback was incorporated into the exploitation activities managed under WP8. The feedback confirmed that market uptake depends on access to nearby grid infrastructure, clear permitting and marine spatial planning, certification and insurability, environmental safeguards, offshore-worker safety and credible inspection and maintenance procedures. Co-location with offshore wind or other marine-energy infrastructure was identified as particularly valuable because electrical connections, vessels and maintenance services may be shared. Result ownership, confidentiality and post-project access requirements will be addressed through licences, supply agreements, service contracts or joint-development agreements where necessary.

**Dissemination updates**

The dissemination plan was updated from a mainly awareness-oriented approach to one focused increasingly on validated results, scientific evidence and market uptake. The main channels included:

- peer-reviewed scientific publications and conference papers;

- presentations at technical and scientific events;

- the project website and social-media channels;

- newsletters, videos and technical communication material;

- the final technical brochure;

- the Stakeholder Advisory Board;

- joint activities with related European projects, including NaturSea-PV;

- participation in BRIDGE and relevant European networks; and

- public access to eligible deliverables, publications and datasets.

The technical brochure presents the final value proposition, validated system performance, technology maturity, sustainability results, regional economic assessment, Key Exploitable Results and remaining development needs. Scientific publications addressed breakwater aerodynamics, wave-basin testing, large-scale hydrodynamic modelling, corrosion sensing, wireless monitoring and fatigue-crack assessment.

Results that remain under review or submission will continue to be disseminated after the project. Confidential information and commercially sensitive results will be subject to the consortium’s established publication-review and IPR procedures.

**Post-project activities**

Partners will continue exploitation and dissemination after project completion through publications, software and modelling services, commercial discussions, follow-on proposals, pilot-development activities and engagement with investors, developers and certification organisations.

Results not exploited directly within the initial post-project period may be promoted through the Horizon Results Platform and relevant European support mechanisms. Partners will also consider services such as the Horizon Results Booster, Innovation Radar, EU IP Helpdesk and standardisation-support initiatives where appropriate.

**Overall assessment**

The exploitation and dissemination plan progressed in line with the project’s technical maturity. The principal update was the transition from an initial broad commercial vision to a result-specific and staged exploitation strategy grounded in validated performance, ownership, regional economics and stakeholder requirements.

No fundamental change was made to the long-term objective of commercial offshore FPV deployment. However, the pathway was adapted to include an intermediate open-sea demonstrator, further component qualification and certification before large-scale commercialisation. This provides a more credible basis for achieving the project’s longer-term deployment, investment and revenue targets.

## 4. Open science

**4.1 Early and Open Sharing of Research**

Public project results were shared through open-access journal publications, conference contributions, public deliverables and research repositories. Where appropriate, public outputs were deposited in Zenodo or the EU Open Research Repository and assigned persistent identifiers. For example, D2.1 is publicly available with the DOI **10.5281/zenodo.22656120**.

The consortium also prepared five journal-style preprints based on public project results:

1.  *Technological Risk Assessment of a Breakwater-Protected Offshore Floating Photovoltaic System Across Three European Sea Basins*;

> https://doi.org/10.20944/preprints202611.1173.v1

2.  

3.  *Experimental Characterization of Critical Connections in an Offshore Floating Photovoltaic System* *:* *https://doi.org/10.20944/preprints202611.1612.v1*

4.  

5.  *Marine Exposure Performance of a Circular-Concrete Floating Breakwater Prototype: Biofouling, Chloride Ingress and Structural Monitoring* *:*

> *https://doi.org/10.20944/preprints202611.1592.v1*

6.  *Layout Selection and Mooring Design of a Floating Breakwater Sheltering an Offshore Floating Photovoltaic Array: A Design Study for Mild, Moderate and Harsh European Seas*;

7.  Economic Assessment of Breakwater-Protected Offshore Floating Photovoltaics Across Three European Sea Basins

8.  

The preprints were derived from public deliverables and prepared following scientific review, authorship confirmation and IPR screening by the relevant partners. Only manuscripts deposited in a public repository and assigned a persistent URL or DOI will be reported as published preprints. Manuscripts not yet deposited will be reported as **preprints prepared** or **in preparation**.

The project produced six published open-access peer-reviewed journal articles and three peer-reviewed conference papers, covering breakwater aerodynamic shielding, wave-basin validation, farm-scale hydrodynamic simulation, corrosion sensing, wireless monitoring, structural integrity and fatigue-crack modelling. Two additional journal manuscripts were submitted during the final reporting period. The consortium also prepared five preprints to support early and open sharing of public project results. Published peer-reviewed papers, submitted manuscripts, preprints, oral presentations and poster contributions are reported separately in the dissemination table to ensure transparent comparison with the DoA publication target.

Pre-registration and registered reports were not used because the principal activities involved iterative engineering design, numerical modelling and prototype testing rather than confirmatory clinical or behavioural research. Crowdsourcing was not considered suitable for the safety-critical engineering analyses. Instead, early access was provided through public deliverables, open-access publications, preprints and repository deposits.

Open sharing was balanced against the need to protect commercially relevant results, confidential partner information and background intellectual property. Publication and repository deposits followed the review procedures defined in the Consortium Agreement and the IPR Management Plan.

**4.2 Measures Supporting Reproducibility**

The consortium implemented the following measures to enable independent understanding, verification and, where the required data are available, reproduction of the project results:

- **Documented experimental procedures:** Publications and public deliverables describe specimen geometries, material compositions, manufacturing procedures, test equipment, loading rates, boundary conditions, instrumentation and data-processing methods.

- **Standardised methods:** Material and component testing used applicable standards and recognised methods, including ISO material-testing procedures, finite-element modelling, Digital Image Correlation, fatigue assessment and lifecycle-assessment standards.

- **Traceable environmental inputs:** The same defined Baltic Sea, Mediterranean Sea and North Sea environmental scenarios were used across system design, modelling, testing and sustainability assessment. The assumptions, water depths, wave conditions and return periods were documented.

- **Model calibration and validation:** Concrete models were calibrated using WP4 experimental data. Hydrodynamic models were compared with analytical solutions and two wave-basin campaigns. Structural models were checked against material and connection tests, while fatigue and monitoring tools were compared with laboratory and marine-exposure results.

- **Transparent model assumptions:** Deliverables and publications describe model geometry, material parameters, boundary conditions, loading assumptions and relevant limitations, including linear-wave assumptions, uniform connector-load distributions and uncertainty in long-term degradation.

- **Replication and result consistency:** Multiple specimens and repeated tests were used where material and prototype availability permitted. Means, standard deviations, observed ranges and failure modes were reported rather than relying only on individual results.

- **Data and software management:** Research data were stored by the responsible partners using controlled project repositories, consistent file structures and versioned datasets. Key model and experimental outputs were retained to allow later verification and further publication.

- **Reusable digital tools:** The Surewave-ANN platform was developed from a documented database of **1,030 concrete mixtures**, while the Structural Health Management System used traceable finite-element, corrosion and fatigue inputs. The applicability limits of these tools were documented.

- **Persistent identifiers and metadata:** Public deliverables, publications, preprints and eligible datasets were deposited with descriptive metadata and persistent identifiers where possible. Relevant DOIs and repository records will be registered in the Funding & Tenders Portal.

- **Clear distinction between evidence types:** Final reporting distinguishes measured, experimentally demonstrated, numerically modelled and projected results. This prevents proposal assumptions or projected benefits from being interpreted as measured outcomes.

Some detailed engineering models, raw commercial cost data, proprietary software components and partner-owned background knowledge could not be released publicly because of confidentiality or IPR restrictions. In these cases, the public deliverables and publications document the methodology, principal assumptions, input categories and aggregated results needed to understand and assess the conclusions. Data may be made available under controlled access or an appropriate agreement where permitted by the owning partner.

**Overall Assessment**

The project **substantially complied with its open-science commitments** and strengthened early sharing. Open-access articles, public deliverables, repository deposits and preparation of five preprints increased the accessibility of the research. Reproducibility was supported through documented methods, common environmental scenarios, model validation, repeated experiments, traceable inputs and retention of the supporting data.

## 5. Deviations from Annex 1 and Annex 2 (if applicable)

The principal deviations from the Description of Action concerned the breakwater materials and prototype configuration, the modular system architecture, the modelling strategy, the scope of fatigue testing, the timing of selected WP6 deliverables and the final LCOE result. These deviations were managed through design updates, validation activities and redistribution of effort within the existing work packages. They did not change the project’s overall ambition or prevent completion of the principal technical objectives.

<tr>
<td><strong>WP3/WP4: Cellular-concrete core density</strong></td>
<td>The lightweight inner-core material was expected to achieve a density of approximately <strong>300 kg/m³</strong>.</td>
<td>The selected cellular-concrete formulation achieved approximately <strong>365 kg/m³</strong>. The difference resulted from the need to balance very low density with integrity, mechanical performance, manufacturability and material stability.</td>
<td>The breakwater material distribution, buoyancy calculations and prototype design were updated using the measured WP4 material properties.</td>
<td><strong>Effect on scope:</strong> No change to the objective of developing a lightweight circular-concrete breakwater. Additional design effort was required in WP3 and WP4, but the prototype was successfully manufactured and deployed.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>WP4, Task 4.4: Reduced-scale prototype core</strong></td>
<td>The breakwater prototype was intended to use the circular concrete material concept developed in WP4.</td>
<td>At reduced scale, a core made entirely from the selected cellular concrete would not provide sufficient buoyancy without increasing the prototype dimensions significantly.</td>
<td>Enclosed EPS was introduced around the cellular-concrete core as a prototype-specific adaptation. The EPS was protected by the concrete shell, including an HPC bottom layer, to prevent direct seawater contact.</td>
<td><strong>Effect on scope:</strong> Limited prototype-level deviation. The full-scale circular-concrete concept remained unchanged. The adaptation enabled manufacture, transport, deployment and nine months of marine exposure.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>WP3: System architecture</strong></td>
<td>The initial concept considered one continuous <strong>10 MW FPV installation</strong>.</td>
<td>Detailed design showed that a single continuous installation would create significant challenges for assembly, access, inspection, maintenance, fault isolation and component replacement.</td>
<td>The commercial architecture was revised to <strong>five modular 2 MW units</strong>, retaining the total <strong>10 MW</strong> capacity ambition. Mooring, anchoring and interface designs were updated for the 2 MW unit.</td>
<td><strong>Effect on scope:</strong> No change to the overall capacity or project objectives. The modular solution improved maintainability, installation sequencing and scalability. Additional WP3 design work was absorbed within the available resources.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>WP5, Task 5.2: Aero-hydrodynamic modelling</strong></td>
<td>The DoA envisaged high-fidelity coupled modelling of the complete FPV-breakwater system.</td>
<td>A single high-fidelity model containing the complete breakwater ring, mooring system and hundreds or thousands of interconnected FPV bodies was found to be computationally demanding and unlikely to provide proportionate additional engineering value.</td>
<td>A validated <strong>multi-fidelity approach</strong> was adopted. Potential-flow and time-domain methods were used for large-system analyses, while CFD was applied to selected component and interaction problems. The models were calibrated and validated against analytical solutions, wave-basin measurements and experimental data. Hydrodynamic simulations of the complete 2 MWp farm were also performed.</td>
<td><strong>Effect on scope:</strong> The WP5 objective remained unchanged. The revised approach enabled large-scale system analysis, mooring optimisation and model validation while using resources more efficiently. The modelling strategy and validation were documented in D5.1 and D5.7.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>WP6, Task 6.3: Fatigue-performance scope</strong></td>
<td>Task 6.3 was initially formulated to assess the fatigue performance of several critical components.</td>
<td>Testing under Task 6.2 and the first basin campaign identified the FPV hinge as the most critical fatigue-sensitive connection.</td>
<td>Following discussion and agreement with the PO, Task 6.3 was refined to focus available resources on fatigue testing and assessment of the original and redesigned FPV hinges.</td>
<td><strong>Effect on scope:</strong> Limited change to the task-level scope; no change to the overall WP6 or project objectives. The focused programme supported redesign of the hinge and assessment against the intended 25-year service-life requirement under the evaluated loading assumptions.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><strong>WP6: D6.1, D6.2 and D6.3 timing</strong></td>
<td>D6.1 was due in M22, D6.2 in M26 and D6.3 in M28.</td>
<td>D6.1 was submitted in M24, D6.2 in M30 and D6.3 in M29. The delays reflected the need to complete and consolidate the connection-testing programme, the two wave-basin campaigns, the hinge redesign and the associated fatigue assessment.</td>
<td>The testing and validation sequence was adjusted so that the final deliverables incorporated the results of the redesigned connection and the second basin campaign. D6.3 was submitted approximately two weeks late after completion of the redesigned-hinge assessment.</td>
<td><strong>Effect on scope:</strong> No change to the technical objectives or final project schedule. The delays improved the completeness of the validation evidence and did not affect D6.4 or the final TRL assessment. The final report should state for each deliverable whether the delay was communicated to the Project Officer in advance.</td>

The report contained deviations, exploitable results, resource use and budget information that consortium partners considered commercially sensitive. ### 5.2 Use of resources *(n/a for MSCA* *and Lump Sums)*

*Include explanations on deviations of the use of resources between actual and planned use of resources in Annex 1, especially related to person-months per work package.*

*Include explanations on transfer of costs categories (if applicable).*

*Include explanations on adjustments to previous financial statements (if applicable).*

**<u>Reported costs EUR:</u>**

**1SIN:** Unforeseen category a2: Personnel costs in this project were originally budgeted under A1 (Actual Costs). However, SINTEF's standard practice is to report personnel costs using A2 (Unit Costs). Therefore, personnel costs have been reported under A2.

**1SIN:** Unforeseen category d2: Infrastructure cost for laboratory rent: 27 849,63 EUR. The reported costs relate to workshop activities, software licenses, and numerical modelling required for the experimental and analytical work in WP6. Support expenses covered the fabrication of fixtures and custom connection systems used in the testing of concrete, steel cables, PU hinges, and the breakwater-FPV connection. Licensing costs relate to the use of eCorr/DIC software for strain field analysis during experimental testing, and Abaqus software for finite element modelling and fatigue-life assessment of concrete beams and PU hinge components. These activities directly contributed to the results presented in Deliverables D6.2 and D6.3

**3CEIT**: CEIT confirm that they’ve report actual costs, NOT budgeted costs.

**5ACC:** The €30,000 initially allocated to subcontracting budget was reallocated to Personnel Costs and Other Goods, Works and Services (OGWS).

Originally, these funds were intended to subcontract an engineering company to support the design of the floating concrete breakwater prototype under Task 4.4. However, during the project, ACCIONA strengthened its SUREWAVE team by incorporating civil and naval engineers with the required expertise. As a result, the design and engineering activities were carried out internally, making the planned subcontract unnecessary. Consequently, part of the subcontracting budget was transferred to Personnel Costs.

The remaining subcontracting budget was transferred to the OGWS category to help cover additional costs associated with the deployment of the floating concrete breakwater prototype at the Port of Gijón under Task 6.5. The budget initially foreseen for these activities proved insufficient due to unforeseen operational and regulatory requirements.

In particular, the Port of Gijón Authority imposed additional maritime safety requirements as a prerequisite for deployment. To comply with these requirements, a buoyage system comprising four yellow buoys equipped with navigation lights, radar reflectors and a St. Andrew’s cross topmark had to be procured and installed. Furthermore, a specialised local contractor providing professional diving and underwater maritime services was required to install and dismantle the prototype and the buoyage system.

**5ACC:** Justification, Purchase cost \>15 % of Personnel Cost:



**7IFEU:** IFEU confirms that their costs are based in actual time records and not estimated, budgeted or imputed.

**<u>Reported Effort (PM):</u>**

**5ACC:** Concerning the use of PMs, the effort reported in WP5 was lower than initially anticipated, as additional effort had to be devoted to WP6 and WP7. This was mainly due to the activities carried out under Task 6.5, which required significantly more resources than originally planned.

**<u>Reported PM Rate:</u>**

**3CEIT:** Lower PM Rate than budget due to a higher allocation of hours from junior profiles, whose hourly rates are lower than those of more senior profiles.

**6CLEMENT:** The personnel month (PM) rate is in total for the project lower than originally estimated because, at the proposal stage, the exact composition of the project team had not yet been fully defined. Consequently, the budgeted PM rate was based on reasonable cost estimates and expected staffing assumptions. During project implementation, the work was carried out by the actual staff assigned to the project, whose average personnel costs resulted in a lower PM rate than initially foreseen. The reported costs are based exclusively on the actual personnel involved and their real personnel costs. Therefore, while the number of personnel months slightly exceeded the initial plan, the lower PM rate led to total personnel costs remaining below the approved budget.

**<u>Explanations on adjustments from RP1:</u>**

**1SIN:** Adjusted hourly rates for 2024 and 2025. 0,01 PM added for WP1 because the system will not allow 0. In reality no adjustments in PM. SINTEF confirm that the adjustment is in alignment with GA Article 6.2.A1

**3CEIT:** The adjustment to P1 is not particularly significant and is due to the following reasons:

Unai Eibar – 2024 hours: At the end of 2024, a correction was made to the number of hours worked. A total of 321 hours had initially been reported for the first period of 2024, whereas the correct figure is 441 hours.

Daily rate calculation template: In the first period, staff departures and new hires at Ceit were not taken into account when calculating the daily rate. Therefore, a minor adjustment has been made to the number of months, taking into consideration the exact start and end dates of each employment period.

In addition, we identified an error in Ceit's internal Excel file used to calculate personnel costs based on the daily rate formula. An incorrect value in one of the cells was propagated to the remaining rows, thereby affecting the total personnel costs reported.

**4MARIN:** Personell Cost: Adjustment due to recalculation of rates.

Purchase Cost: In P1, two invoices were incorrectly reported under C1 instead of C3. In addition, one P1-related invoice was omitted from the original report and has now been included.

Internally invoiced goods and services: Adjustment due to recalculation of rates.

**5ACC:** Personell Cost: Error in the cost calculation of the personnel involved in project. Correction hours assigned of 1 person by error in the 1st Period.

**6CLEMENT**: Change from cat. C2 to C3. Costs were assigned to the wrong cost category. The costs were moved from C2 to C3.

#### 5.2.1 Unforeseen subcontracting (if applicable) (n/a for MSCA)

Specify in this section:

- the work (the tasks) performed by a subcontractor which may cover only a limited part of the project

- explanation of the circumstances which caused the need for a subcontract, taking into account the specific characteristics of the project

- the confirmation that the subcontractor has been selected ensuring the best value for money or, if appropriate, the lowest price and avoiding any conflict of interests

- include also the name of subcontractor and amount.

#### 5.2.2 Unforeseen use of in kind contributions (n/a for MSCA and Lump Sums)

Specify in this section:

- the identity of the third party

- the resources made available by the third party respectively against payment or free of charges

- explanation of the circumstances which caused the need for using these resources for carrying out the work.
